[1] The Hacker News - Switzerland Confirms AI-Assisted Cyberattacks Breached Critical Infrastructure Operators
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 11:34:20 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/switzerland-confirms-ai-assisted.html

[TAM METİN - 734 kelime]
Attackers using AI agents have broken into computer systems at Swiss organizations in four of five confirmed attacks, the first such cases in the country, the country's cybersecurity agency said yesterday.

Three of the attacks targeted critical infrastructure operators, and two of those succeeded, with no major disruptions reported. The victims have not been named, and it is not known who is behind the attacks.

Florian Schütz, who directs Switzerland's National Cyber Security Centre, gave the figures in an interview with public broadcaster SRF.

All five cases were reported in recent days. Small and medium-sized companies were among the organizations hit, and they came forward voluntarily.

The agency's first notice, a security advisory published on Tuesday, said the agents had compromised the victims' IT systems in every case known at that point. Neither the notice nor yesterday's broadcast interview explains why the count changed.

"These cases represent a shift in the cyber threat landscape," the advisory said.

The targeted operators are in at least two sectors. Electricity, drinking water, and telecommunications suppliers are examples of what Switzerland counts as critical infrastructure, according to a news agency report on the interview.

The attacks involve what the Swiss agency calls agentic offensive frameworks, AI agents that find security weaknesses and carry out attacks on their own. Its advisory named ARTEX and CyberStrikeAI as examples of agents reachable over the internet, not as the tools used in the five cases.

The agents are often used to scan for known, unpatched weaknesses and can run many attacks in parallel, the agency says. When defenses respond, the agents switch methods, leaving companies significantly less time to detect an intrusion and respond.

The number of AI attack tools freely reachable online has nearly doubled since October, according to the agency. The figure counts available tools, not attacks or victims.

Raphael Reischuk, co-founder of Switzerland's National Test Institute for Cybersecurity, expects a larger wave of attacks and says companies underestimate the risk.

Italy's national computer security incident response team, CSIRT Italia, described mass compromise campaigns in an official bulletin this week. In the cases it observed firsthand, AI-directed autonomous agents broke into a large number of systems within a few hours.

The agents adapt their actions within seconds, run in parallel without human input, and chain vulnerabilities, misconfigurations, and compromised credentials together, the bulletin says. It names ARTEX, CyberStrikeAI, PentAGI, and DeepSeek Harness, an open-source agent whose Red Team Mode uses a built-in prompt to get past the underlying model's safety limits.

Each action on its own looks like normal user or API traffic. Detection depends on context: where requests come from, how often they arrive, how much data moves, and the order in which things happen.

Britain's National Cyber Security Centre reported the same pattern in agency guidance this week. "Since September 2026, the NCSC has seen a marked rise in AI-assisted cyber attacks," it said.

Some incidents caused downtime on internet-facing services, and others gave attackers a foothold for further agent-driven activity. The weaknesses are old ones: credentials committed to code repositories, misconfigured infrastructure-as-code, default or missing authentication, and weaknesses in APIs.

What has changed is the speed, persistence, and low cost of the attacks, the British agency says. The frameworks are also noisy, which makes them easier to detect.

The European Commission has already been hit. Attackers using ARTEX broke into its human resources, medical reimbursement, and translation systems, the Commission has confirmed, in an earlier intrusion first detected on October 22.

What Defenders Should Do

The strongest common advice from the three agencies is to shrink the time attackers have. Their recommendations overlap:

Install critical security updates on internet-facing systems within 24 hours of release (Switzerland), and patch flaws already being exploited first (Italy).

Disconnect or isolate internet-facing systems that cannot be patched quickly (Italy).

Reduce what is reachable from the internet, using geo-blocking, allow lists, and authentication in front of services (Switzerland, UK).

Require MFA or passkeys on everything reachable from the internet, and grant the lowest privileges that work (Switzerland).

Watch for unusual behavior and data volumes (Switzerland), and correlate logs: agent activity shows up as bursts of requests and high error rates (UK).

Put bot challenges on high-value endpoints, and use decoys such as honeytokens to catch and slow agents (UK).

Load Italy's published indicator feeds into monitoring tools, noting that the indicators change quickly and are valid only for their stated timeframes (Italy).

[2] The Hacker News - Unpatched Cacti Zero-Day Enables RCE in Just Four HTTP Requests via Guest Access
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 11:18:20 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/unpatched-cacti-zero-day-enables-rce-in.html

[TAM METİN - 468 kelime]
Details have emerged about a new zero-day vulnerability in Cacti, an open-source network monitoring tool, that could be exploited to achieve remote code execution.

The proof-of-concept (PoC) exploit, which has yet to be assigned a CVE identifier, has been tracked as 4HTTP2SHELL and 4HTTP2ROOT.

"I found a path from a fresh, unauthenticated HTTP session to command execution as the Cacti server user in four requests," a security researcher who goes by the online alias "ek0ms savi0r" said. "No login. No valid API key. No phishing. Just four HTTP requests against the default guest surface."

The shortcoming affects Cacti Core 1.2.31, the latest version, on an isolated host when the guest account is enabled.

At a high level, the vulnerability allows an attacker to abuse the guest access to write an unvalidated value (i.e., non-numeric text) into a setting called "preview_graphs_per_page" via "auth_profile.php?action=update_data." The configuration parameter controls how many graphs are displayed per page when viewing graphs in Preview Mode.

The crafted value takes the form of arbitrary SQL strings that can be turned into a second-order SQL injection by taking advantage of the fact that Cacti does not validate that the user-provided input is actually an integer value. This, in turn, opens the door for arbitrary SQL query execution when the graph preview page ("graph_view.php") is loaded with default filters.

The SQL query demonstrated by "ek0ms savi0r" is an UPDATE statement to modify a record in Cacti's poller table. In the final stage of the attack, the realtime poller endpoint ("graph_realtime.php") is invoked from the same guest session, which causes the modified row from the poller table to be read and passed as input to PHP's popen() function without any allowlist or sanitization checks.

The researcher has pointed to four failures that ultimately make it possible to achieve unauthenticated remote code execution -

The guest can write a numeric setting as an arbitrary string

The stored default bypasses request validation

Cacti's PHP PDO database driver accepts stacked statements through the SQL LIMIT clause

The poller executes a database-stored command string

Successful exploitation of the flaw can allow an attacker to compromise the Cacti server and provide a pivot point to the target network as it exposes sensitive data, or even suppress alerts by modifying the poller table.

"Because Cacti often stores credentials and connection details for routers, switches, servers, and other devices across a network, an attacker who reaches this level of command execution can gain a foothold reaching far beyond the monitoring server itself," watchTowr said in an advisory.

In the absence of a fix, users are advised to disable the guest account and restrict access to the Cacti web interface to mitigate potential risks. It's also essential to review poller configuration entries for unexpected command values and apply the fixes as soon as they become available.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/unpatched-cacti-zero-day-enables-rce-in.html, thehackernews.com, 27.11.2026)

[3] The Hacker News - ThreatsDay: 10M NGINX Services at Risk, 250+ Malicious Extensions, AI Server Hijacking + 17 More Stories
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 01:09:57 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/threatsday-250-rogue-extensions-10m.html

[TAM METİN - 5806 kelime]
A website can look perfectly fine to the person running it. Log out, visit the same page, and things might look different. Something similar is happening with browser extensions, where even the way you find and install one can change what it does. Checking whether software works as expected only tells you so much.

Then there’s the question of who gets to use what. Stolen logins are opening doors across connected systems, while some AI servers are sitting online for anyone to use at someone else’s expense. Even software responsible for keeping the power on has found its way into an attack.

Meanwhile, authorities are taking down large scam operations, extortion gangs are making comebacks, and ransomware activity is picking up. Some of the more worrying findings this week involve things most people would have little reason to suspect. The details are worth a look.

Stolen credentials expose customer data

Todoist Discloses Data Breach


      Todoist has disclosed that an attacker gained unauthorized access to its production systems and retrieved some customer information on November 23, 2026. The incident affects 6% of its users. "Between November 22 and 23, an attacker used a stolen credential associated with our version control system to modify automated workflows in three repositories and obtain additional credentials," it said. During this time, they could also read our source code. Our investigation found no changes to application code. One of the credentials they obtained allowed them to change how employees access our internal systems. The attacker used it to take over an employee’s work account and gain access to our cloud infrastructure. On November 23, the attacker downloaded a file containing account information for some early Todoist users whose passwords were stored using an older hashing method. They also made targeted queries for account tokens, searched some customer content for credentials, and accessed email information through connected Todoist for Outlook accounts."

Todoist has disclosed that an attacker gained unauthorized access to its production systems and retrieved some customer information on November 23, 2026. The incident affects 6% of its users. The attacker used it to take over an employee’s work account and gain access to our cloud infrastructure. They also made targeted queries for account tokens, searched some customer content for credentials, and accessed email information through connected Todoist for Outlook accounts."

Record cybersecurity acquisition

CrowdStrike to Acquire Zscaler in Record $43.9B Deal


      CrowdStrike said it will acquire Zscaler in a deal worth $43.9 billion, making it the biggest cybersecurity acquisition on record. "Security used to be about keeping the adversary out. Today, safety is required to let AI in," CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said. "The network used to connect people to places. Now it connects agents to data. With Zscaler, we're building cybersecurity's operating system: a single control plane to deploy AI, control AI, and enable any agentic application to run safely. We see a path to become the largest AI safety and security company the world has ever seen."

CrowdStrike said it will acquire Zscaler in a deal worth $43.9 billion, making it the biggest cybersecurity acquisition on record. "Security used to be about keeping the adversary out. Today, safety is required to let AI in," CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said. "The network used to connect people to places. Now it connects agents to data. We see a path to become the largest AI safety and security company the world has ever seen."

Paid ads activate hidden payloads

Ad-Driven Installs Trigger Hidden Browser Extension Behavior


      Microsoft's Edge Extensions Security Team has documented SleeperWidget, a set of over 250 extensions across Microsoft Edge Add-ons, Chrome Web Store, and Mozilla Add-ons that work as advertised unless installed through a paid search ad. Once installed that way, they collect browsing data, inject ads, and change shopping links. "Its extensions pose as maps, recipe finders, package trackers, and other everyday tools. Most deliver the features they advertise, which makes hidden behavior easier to miss," it said. "In the extensions we analyzed, installation through a paid-advertising funnel also activated browsing-data collection, ad injection, and shopping-link modification."

Microsoft's Edge Extensions Security Team has documented SleeperWidget, a set of over 250 extensions across Microsoft Edge Add-ons, Chrome Web Store, and Mozilla Add-ons that work as advertised unless installed through a paid search ad. Once installed that way, they collect browsing data, inject ads, and change shopping links. "Its extensions pose as maps, recipe finders, package trackers, and other everyday tools. Most deliver the features they advertise, which makes hidden behavior easier to miss," it said. "In the extensions we analyzed, installation through a paid-advertising funnel also activated browsing-data collection, ad injection, and shopping-link modification."

LLMjacking without exploits

Internet-Exposed Hosts Could Become Targets for AI Abuse


      An analysis of 20,354 internet-facing hosts has revealed that thousands of servers where AI services or notebook APIs have been deployed and left accessible without authentication, potentially enabling attackers to abuse them for their own purposes. "More than half of the exposed Ollama servers in this scan have already been touched by someone who is not the owner," Rapid7 said. "79% of exposed Ollama hosts list at least one :cloud model, mostly GLM, DeepSeek, MiniMax and Kimi variants. An exposed, signed-in server can provide access to someone else's paid subscription, with the bill landing on the owner. This is the self-hosted version of LLMjacking, and it requires no exploit at all."

Blockchain-backed malware delivery

Fake Minecraft Mod Delivers Python Infostealer


      SonicWall Capture Labs has disclosed details of a Java-based loader from SilentNet, a Java-based remote access trojan and infostealer sold as a malware-as-a-service offering that's advertised on YouTube. The JAR masquerades as a mod for Minecraft Fabric and targets players who download mods from external sources. "Once loaded by the game, it pulls the player's Minecraft session token from memory. It then gets its command-and-control (C2) address from a smart contract on the Polygon blockchain, and downloads a portable Python runtime hidden inside a PNG image to run a second-stage infostealer," SoniWall said. "Every string in the JAR is hidden by per-character arithmetic; it resolves hostnames through DNS-over-HTTPS, and it talks to its C2 through a custom HTTPS client that skips certificate validation."

AI-powered phishing infrastructure

Hugging Face AI Used to Host Phishing Pages


      Threat actors are using DeepSite, Hugging Face's AI website builder, to create dozens of phishing and credential-stealing pages on Hugging Face Spaces impersonating 28 brands, including Microsoft, Google, PayPal, Chase, and Coinbase. "29 of the pages were built with DeepSite, Hugging Face’s AI website builder," Aikido said. "One Microsoft login clone took about 90 minutes of plain-language prompts, and other operators asked the builder to hide their pages from crawlers or send stolen logins to Telegram."

Credential theft spans 127 CVEs

Attackers Exploit CVEs to Harvest Credentials


      According to VulnCheck, requests to steal credentials made up 13% of all vulnerability exploitation recorded between January and November 2026. Credential-harvesting attempts appeared across 127 of 694 CVEs observed in its honeypot network, with open-source software and AI developer tools like Apache, Laravel, Langflow, Flowise, MLflow, and n8n taking up 61% of the CVEs associated with credential-harvesting attempts. "Attackers search application files, logs and environment variables for database passwords, cloud credentials and API keys, with file-read and SSRF vulnerabilities providing opportunities without code execution," VulnCheck said, adding it "observed 1,756 attempts targeting AI provider and coding agent credentials from 406 IP addresses across 27 CVEs."

According to VulnCheck, requests to steal credentials made up 13% of all vulnerability exploitation recorded between January and November 2026. Ransomware disrupts critical services

New BYOD Ransomware Emerges


      A previously unknown ransomware and data-extortion group calling itself BYOD has claimed at least nine organizations between October 5 and November 9, 2026. "Its attacks have had real-world consequences, including ambulance diversion, more than 30 clinics closed, and campus-wide device shutdowns," TrendAI said. "BYOD runs a pressure campaign alongside the intrusion: audio messages on infected computers, public leak-site posts, statements framing its attacks as holding organizations accountable, and outreach to individual victims." Although the complete intrusion chain remains to be analyzed, evidence points to credential compromise and a remote access trojan on an employee's computer for initial access.

Malicious JavaScript delivery platform

Fake WordPress Plugin Turns Sites Into JavaScript Pathways


      A malware campaign has been observed installing a fake WordPress plugin built almost entirely by a name generator, including its title, directories, files, and functions (e.g., trijavascriptly, ultravueal, maxdataic, and quadmicroservicesion). "Behind the generated names is a second-stage delivery platform that runs arbitrary JavaScript in the browser of every anonymous visitor to an infected site, routes that traffic through the victim's own domain, and lies dormant for logged-in users," Wordfence said. The malware has been discovered twice, once in January 2026 and another in September 2026. Exactly how the plugin is installed is not known, but investigation shows that it's likely via a compromise of an administrator account.

Global scam networks disrupted

U.S. Scam Center Strike Force Cracks Down on Scams


      The U.S. Department of Justice (DoJ) has announced that over 17.3 million scam accounts have been deplatformed on Meta, 700 fraudulent crypto investment websites seized, and 12 U.S. arrest warrants issued as part of efforts to tackle foreign and domestic scams over the past six months. The Strike Force has traced and restrained $1,082,260,156.82 in cryptocurrency stolen from U.S. victims. "Our Scam Center Strike Force has continued its work investigating cyber-enabled fraud perpetrated against Americans by transnational organized crime groups, emanating out of Asia and attacking America's main street," said U.S. Attorney Jeanine Ferris Pirro. "The Strike Force teams focus on identifying and pursuing key leaders — including Chinese organized crime affiliates operating in Cambodia, Laos, and Burma, and rapidly expanding around the globe — to bring them to justice." FBI Director Kash Patel said the Operation Blackout scam demolition initiative has "seized nearly $17 billion in illicit funds, taken more than 14,000 digital devices, directly warned over 11,000 Americans from scam attempts, and prevented more than $690 million in potential victim losses." Over 29 scam compounds operating across Asia, Africa, and the Middle East have been dismantled and more than 2,200 trafficked workers have been repatriated. The U.S. State Department has also worked with U.S. and Southeast Asian law enforcement to facilitate the freezing of more than $1 billion in stolen cryptocurrency, the takedown of over 1.4 million fraudulent websites and accounts, and several arrests. Earlier this month, the U.S. Attorney's Office also filed a civil forfeiture action to recover 456,602 USDT (Tether) that's alleged to be proceeds of romance fraud schemes targeting individuals in Massachusetts and elsewhere on online dating sites and Facebook.

Billion-dollar crypto recovery bid

U.S. Files Action to Seize $1B in Crypto Related to Binance Hack


      The Justice Department has filed a civil action to recover over $1.1 billion in cryptocurrency that was illicitly obtained from Binance by North Korean actors in a major hack last month. "To date, the United States has assisted in the freezing, seizing, and/or the return of almost $3 billion worth of cryptocurrency from multiple sources since the theft from Binance on or about October 29," the DoJ said. Brett Leatherman, Cyber Branch Operations Director of the FBI, said, "North Korea depends on sophisticated laundering techniques to convert stolen cryptocurrency into funds that support the regime's illicit activities," adding, "Following the Binance theft, the FBI and our partners moved quickly to trace and freeze stolen assets and share critical information with virtual asset service providers."

Extortion gang resurfaces after arrests

ShinyHunters Return with a New Site


      The ShinyHunters extortion gang claims to have returned on a new data leak site following a series of arrests targeting alleged members. The new TOR site, launched on November 23, features messages in both Russian and English announcing the group's return, while denouncing a former member, Rey (aka Saif), who was reportedly arrested and is "actively cooperating" with law enforcement. "We want to let you know that from now on we will only be using our account on Exploit, which we've held since 2020," the group said. "We won't be active on any other BF-style forums anymore." They also concluded the statement with a shout-out to two other arrested members, Umbreon and Ed Dubrovsky. "Thank you for being with us all this time," the group said. "We won't forget you, and we might even send you a couple of postcards to your rat-infested cells. It was a pleasure working with you. And it's truly a shame that our guys got caught because of such silly mistakes, ones that could easily have been avoided if you fools hadn't gotten involved with the wrong people."

AI misuse detection without data retention

Anthropic Launches Public Beta of Enterprise Frontier Safeguards


      Anthropic has launched a public beta of Enterprise Frontier Safeguards (EFS), allowing organizations to detect potentially harmful AI activity across multiple interactions while maintaining zero data retention (ZDR). The protection employs a second defensive layer, known as automated safety monitoring, that looks for patterns of misuse across multiple requests to provide automated safety monitoring without storing any customer data. "Instead, our automated safety monitoring system analyzes data stored entirely on infrastructure controlled by our customers," Anthropic said. "When automated safety monitoring finds a risk, it creates a Safety Case for you to review. These cases can be read from your cloud storage or through an API, and can be integrated with security information and event management systems. They do not include inputs and outputs. Once it's set up, automated safety monitoring can help your team detect intentional misuse that could lead to serious harm (like offensive cyber operations). It can also help detect agents that take actions beyond a user's intentions."

Ransomware activity jumps 23%

Ransomware Attacks Surge in October 2026


      NCC Group has revealed that ransomware attacks surged 23% in October 2026 compared to September, with 1049 claimed incidents. The Gentlemen, N0n, Qilin, Akira, and Silent Ransom have claimed the most victims. Top targeted verticals included industrials, consumer discretionary, healthcare, IT, and consumer supplies. Check Point Research has reported a record 2,830 ransomware victims in Q3 2026, claimed by 117 ransomware brands, up from 91 in the previous quarter. Of these, 42 are new. "Ransom payments continued to diverge: average payments fell 45% quarter over quarter to just over $1 million, while the median rose 167% to $400,000," Check Point said. "The top 10 groups accounted for just 49% of victims, the first time groups outside the top ten collectively represented the majority." Qilin, The Gentlemen, and INC Ransom emerged as the most active groups during the period. Interestingly, while the top 10 groups accounted for 71% of victims in Q1 2026, the figure has declined to 58% in Q2 and only 49% in Q3, indicating a fragmentation of the threat landscape.

12,200 online stores compromised

One in 195 Online Shops Scanned Hijacked


      A new analysis from Guardio Labs has revealed that one online shop in 195 (i.e., about 12,200 of the 2.38 million stores) was hijacked in October 2026. The phenomenon has been codenamed CartJacked. "Three schemes go straight after shoppers: hand-offs to gambling and scam pages, fake 'I'm not a robot' checks that tell you to paste a command (about 900 shops), and card skimmers," Guardio said. "Of the ten commands the fake checks handed out, three still delivered malware, including a stealer for passwords and crypto wallets."

€26 million crypto trail uncovered

57 Illegal IPTV Services Disrupted


      A Europol-led operation along with authorities from 11 countries has disrupted 57 illegal IPTV services and the identification of 321 websites illegally offering copyrighted content as part of a five-day effort between November 16 and 20, 2026. The sites drew over 7.3 million visits each year. More than €26 million in cryptocurrency transactions have been traced so far. "The operation targeted a criminal market worth billions of euros annually, in which illegal streaming providers sell unauthorized access to premium television channels, live sports and films, often collecting subscription payments through cryptocurrency," Europol said. "The profits generated by these services do not simply disappear. They move through payment accounts, cryptocurrency wallets and intermediaries, making it harder for investigators to identify who is ultimately benefiting." No arrests have been announced.

10 million exposed NGINX services at risk

Millions of Services Impacted by NGINX2Root


      Censys has revealed that more than 10 million exposed NGINX services are running an affected version with HTTP/2 that are vulnerable to a set of flaws called NGINX2Root that "could allow a remote, unauthenticated attacker to corrupt memory in an NGINX worker process when a set of specific features are enabled – leading to code execution where ASLR is disabled or the attacker can bypass it."

Power management software weaponized

Attacker Abuses UPS Management Feature to Conceal Intrusion


      Broadcom-owned Symantec has disclosed details of an intrusion in which the attacker used CyberPower PowerPanel Business (PPB), a software that manages UPS devices, as a way to execute code on a domain controller, dump Active Directory secrets and launch a persistent implant. "PPB can run a script (a .cmd file in an extcmd folder) when a power-related event such as a voltage sag or outage is detected," Symantec said. "The attacker placed their own scripts in that folder through the product's web console and had the PowerPanel service, ppbed.exe, run them. The commands in this attack chain have ppbed.exe as their ancestor and run as SYSTEM. The attacker used this channel to download tools with certutil, then loaded a .NET assembly into memory using PowerShell, and ran ntdsutil and esentutl against the Active Directory database, ntds.dit." The same channel has also been used to launch an implant ("upnpcont.exe") that uses DLL sideloading to launch a suspicious DLL ("atl.dll"). "What this attack shows is that UPS, power-management, and other infrastructure software should be treated like any other potentially dual-use RMM product; used with network restrictions, unique credentials, vigilant patching, and process-level monitoring," Symantec said.

Banking middleware weaponized

BankWeaver Delivers FabricShell, FabricShadow


      A new campaign called BankWeaver has targeted a Temenos Quantum Fabric deployment with an exposed authentication service that was configured to accept anonymous access to obtain administrator privileges and deliver malicious Java components via the platform's extension mechanism. The attack targeted an unspecified financial services institution in early September 2026. "Attackers could generate an anonymous token by accessing a public application and then exchange it for an administrator token on another exposed application," Forescout said. "The new token obtained by the attackers granted access to administrative functions within the Temenos Fabric platform, including managing running applications and creating new services." The malicious components delivered as part of the attack include: FabricShell, which provides arbitrary command execution and database queries, and FabricShadow, which collects host information, stages output locally, and exfiltrates results to remote servers. Temenos has since released updates and guidance to configure the service as private and advised customers to restrict internet access to Spotlight and Fabric administrative and authentication services.

Fake updates deliver Python backdoor

Edgecution Likely Returns with Fake Microsoft Updates


      A new wave of Microsoft Teams social engineering activity exhibiting similarities to Edgecution was uncovered by Arctic Wolf Labs last month. "In the October wave, following a lull in observed cases from August to September, new delivery artifacts and operational patterns were observed," the cybersecurity company said. "The campaign abuses a malicious Edge extension and Chrome Native Messaging to run a Python backdoor outside the browser sandbox. The activity shares early chain tradecraft with PREY-0059, including external-tenant Teams impersonation of IT support, legitimate runtimes paired with scripts, and S3-hosted payloads disguised as legitimate software. While PREY-0059 used S3 buckets named after targeted victims and delivered a Java-based RAT alongside a Java runtime, the current activity used generic Microsoft-themed tenants and buckets, an Edge extension, and a Python backdoor paired with a Python runtime."

One thing worth taking away from this week's stories is how much damage can happen without a particularly clever attack. A forgotten server, a stolen credential, or a feature nobody thought to monitor can be enough. AI may be changing how some attacks are carried out, but many of the ways attackers get in are still surprisingly ordinary.

The harder part is knowing what deserves attention before it becomes a problem. That includes software sitting quietly in the background, services exposed for convenience, and accounts with more access than they need. Somewhere between keeping things running and adding new capabilities, it's easy to lose track of what you've already put online. This week offers several good reasons to look again.

[4] The Hacker News - North Korea-Linked PolinRider Poisons Over 7,600 GitHub Repositories to Spread Malware
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 20:43:41 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/north-korea-linked-polinrider-poisons.html

[TAM METİN - 1358 kelime]
The threat actors behind the PolinRider campaign have been observed planting a Python loader in GitHub repositories, as evidence emerges that the activity may have started as far back as February 2025.

"The loader hides in a small utils package or in conftest.py," SafeDep said in a report published this week. "When Python imports the file, the loader reads the newest transaction that an Ethereum wallet sent to itself. It decodes the input data of that transaction as JavaScript and runs it with Node.js."

The JavaScript code, for its part, reads a second wallet to locate the command-and-control (C2) server and then passes the next stage to a detached interactive Node.js environment by means of a node -i process.

The software supply chain security company said it identified the loader in 375 public repositories that belong to 260 accounts. There is no evidence that the loader has been distributed via PyPI or npm.

PolinRider is the name assigned to a malicious campaign linked to North Korean threat actors. Recent reports indicate that the activity employs various channels to distribute malware, including -

Fraudulent recruitment campaigns that make use of phishing emails and fake hiring portals like BlockNovas, AccuraSoftHire ("accurasofthire[.]com") and HuntRole ("huntrole[.]org") to trick prospective targets into running malware-laced projects under the pretext of a take-home assessment.

Publishing malicious packages across open-source ecosystems by hijacking maintainer accounts to plant obfuscated malware through fake font files (fa-solid-400.woff2, fa-solid-500.woff2, or fa-solid-300.llf), Visual Studio Code (VS Code) auto-run tasks, and Git history rewriting.

Using compromised developer machines to steal their GitHub tokens and force-push malicious commits into open-source repositories they have access to.

At a high level, the PolinRider loader retrieves an encrypted second-stage Node.js payload that scans the infected host for installed developer applications, such as GitHub Desktop, Cursor, VS Code, and Google Antigravity, and tampers with the most frequently used tools to execute attacker-controlled code and deliver infostealer malware.

It's worth noting here that PolinRider is assessed to be a sub-cluster operating in parallel with a long-running campaign codenamed Contagious Interview. While the latter is more of a social-engineering entry point that's used to compromise developer systems, PolinRider abuses the access to modify repositories and turn them into a distribution channel for malware.

"Cybercriminal groups deploy supply chain worms for opportunistic theft. On the other hand, North Korean state-sponsored threat actors use open-source software registries as a primary initial access vector targeting corporate environments," Palo Alto Networks Unit 42 said in a report published last month.

"Threat actors demonstrate how compromising a single maintainer account or registry scope converts open-source dependencies, resulting in widespread affected cloud environments."

Some of the decoy sites have been found to incorporate two other alternate delivery paths to distribute the loader -

Employ ClickFix-style lures to get victims to copy and paste malicious commands into the terminal app depending on the operating system as part of a "standard settings bundle for candidates and interview workflows."

"The [job] application asks for a short verification video," StepSecurity said. "Until the server marks a candidate as having installed a 'driver,' the camera check always fails with a message saying the system does not have a compatible camera environment."

"A separate 'Technical onboarding' page on the site gives a one-line curl command to download the 'driver.' On Windows, the downloaded installer fetches a script from the careers site that installs Node.js if it is missing, downloads a JavaScript payload, runs it in the background and deletes itself. The macOS and Linux versions do the same."

The JavaScript malware is designed to perform the following steps -

Read the latest transaction associated with an Ethereum wallet ("0xa322E5f3D311D3080e6f0121063e9aDC2490Ef1a").

Get its destination wallet address ("0x67B38e9b67B38E9b68656c6C6f6970626f742121").

Extract the C2 server address twice ("103.179.142[.]155") from the first eight bytes ("67B38e9b" and "67B38E9b") of the wallet address, using a technique referred to as NullReceiver, while the last 12 bytes represent the ASCII string "helloipbot!!."

Use the IP address to fetch two payloads, "0x/cls" and "0x/ls," over port 443.

The latest findings from SafeDep show that the repositories are injected with a 58-line Python loader that reads the "0x3b1574417524c11a7403c4b232CAcBb320CceBC6" wallet to extract JavaScript code that then reaches out to a second wallet ("0x1eEf0849EDa15d87f64e51C73C8442Cf3c8bCEF3") to obtain the destination wallet associated with the latest transaction ("0x5F87b5545F87b55468656C6C6f6970626f742121"), which is then used to parse the C2 server ("95.135.181[.]84").

A defining aspect of NullReceiver is the marker "68656c6C6f6970626f742121" in the made-up destination wallet address that consistently translates to "helloipbot!!."

"The /0x/cls script runs twice, once with eval in the current process and once in a new process," SafeDep said. "The /0x/ls script runs only in a new process."

Pushing compromised versions of legitimate npm packages such as @adminide-stack/utils, @adminide-stack/health-api-server, @adminide-stack/user-activity-browser, @adminide-stack/utils, @adminide-stack/forms-core, @adminide-stack/yantra-help-browser, server-hemera-mongo, cdeops, @cdmbase/wiki-browser, and @pubngo-stack/help-browser that use the same wallet address to fetch /0x/cls and /0x/ls from "95.135.181[.]151."

A fake recruitment campaign from HuntRole that convinces victims to complete a coding assessment, which uses a VS Code extension named "huntrole.assessment-tool" from the Visual Studio Marketplace to send system information and fetch and run a malicious JavaScript payload. The script queries the same wallet address to fetch /0x/cls and /0x/ls from "95.135.181[.]84."

Using a GitHub account named "Eternity0207" to open over 200 pull requests targeting open-source projects like AsyncAPI Generator, CircuitVerse, and Open Food Facts, and subsequently force-push a modified commit containing an obfuscated loader that uses the same wallet address to fetch /0x/cls and /0x/ls from "95.135.181[.]84."
    
According to one of the maintainers of AsyncAPI Generator, "Each time, the payload stayed on the branch for 30-40 minutes and was then force-pushed away, so the final diff looked clean." The unauthorized modifications allowed the loader to run when an unsuspecting developer runs "npm run build" (for AsyncAPI Generator), loads the test configuration (for Open Food Facts), or opens the folder in VS Code (for CircuitVerse). The GitHub account has since been suspended.

According to one of the maintainers of AsyncAPI Generator, "Each time, the payload stayed on the branch for 30-40 minutes and was then force-pushed away, so the final diff looked clean." The unauthorized modifications allowed the loader to run when an unsuspecting developer runs "npm run build" (for AsyncAPI Generator), loads the test configuration (for Open Food Facts), or opens the folder in VS Code (for CircuitVerse). The GitHub account has since been suspended.

According to estimates from Apiiro, PolinRider has been detected in at least 7,609 GitHub repos owned by 2,330 accounts, most of them belonging to developers. Some of the victims include Neutralinojs, InstaWP Connect, and fxhash. The loader has been pushed to 2,040 repositories between October 27 and November 6, 2026.

"The worm uses stolen GitHub access to rewrite commits the developer already made, keeping the author, message and date, and adds a loader hidden after hundreds of spaces in a build config," Apiiro researcher Shmulik Cohen said.

"Building the project runs it. In the newest wave, opening the folder in VS Code is enough. The loader reads its server from an Ethereum wallet, so taking a server down changes nothing: one transaction moves every loader to the next."

The development comes as OpenSourceMalware said it discovered a PolinRider attack from May 2025, predating the earliest documented victims by eight months. The attack targeted nine packages, using a hijacked maintainer account to push poisoned versions within a span of 90 minutes -

itsa-react-docviewer@16.1.2

itsa-react-button@16.0.11

itsa-react-maskedinput@16.1.2

itsa-react-checkbox@16.0.5

itsa-react-focuscontainer@16.0.10

itsa-react-input@16.1.2

Each of these packages have been found to leverage EtherHiding, a precursor to NullReceiver, to retrieve a JavaScript downloader called JADESNOW, which is then used to deliver InvisibleFerret, a Python-based backdoor used in Contagious Interview campaigns, along with a Socket.IO RAT and a Python-based stealer.

"The 2025 sample carries PolinRider's payload but not its documented spread behavior, which appears in the same maintainer's GitHub repositories in four windows between January and July 2026," security researchers Paul McCarty and Jenn Gile said. "The blockchain records behind it date attacker activity back to February 2025."

"The infection likely persisted on the maintainer's machine from 2025, because the 2026 pushes ran from it. That matches PolinRider's documented propagation script, which rolls back the clock, folds the payload into the tip commit, and force-pushes with the developer's own credentials."

[5] The Hacker News - Most Wanted: The Phishing Kits and Techniques Getting Past Security Controls
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 16:27:55 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/most-wanted-phishing-kits-and.html

[TAM METİN - 987 kelime]
Every phishing attack has to get past email security, web filtering, and safe browsing before it reaches an employee. Some kits manage that far more often than others, and those are the kits that security teams need to know about.

At Push Security, we measure this as breakthrough rate: The share of attacks that get past email security, web gateways, and safe browsing to reach the user.

How an attack breaks through

Every browser-based attack starts with a lure, and each control in its path sees only part of the attack:

Email security: Half of the attacks we see never touch email. The lure arrives through a search ad, a chat message, social media, a calendar invite, or a QR code. The rest come from trusted senders and services, so they pass reputation checks.

Web gateways: The pages sit on trusted hosting and are assembled in the browser. The domains rotate every few days, so reputation data lags behind them.

Safe browsing and scanners: Bot checks and cloaking show scanners a harmless page. A new domain isn't on any blocklist yet.

When the page loads in the user's browser and the user interacts with it, that's a breakthrough. Breakthrough rate counts those attacks, not attempts.

This changes how to read upstream data. A kit with a high breakthrough rate is one that upstream controls are missing. A low count in email or proxy logs doesn't prove that a kit is rare, because the kit may be getting past those controls without a trace.

Our upcoming webinar reveals the five kits with the highest breakthrough rates. Here's an early look at the number one kit.

DOMDIFFER: Google's real sign-in page, mirrored from the attacker's browser

From August to October 2026, one kit ranked first by breakthrough rate every month. DOMDIFFER, an AiTM phishing kit that targets corporate Google and Microsoft accounts, broke through 2-4x as often as the next kit each month. DOM mirroring, the mechanism it uses under the hood, also happens to be the newest and fastest-growing way for a kit to relay a sign-in.

We first saw DOMDIFFER in February 2026. DOMDIFFER is our name for the kit (it hasn't yet been linked to a known phishing-as-a-service brand). We believe it grew out of NakedPages, a reverse proxy kit, and the two kits share infrastructure in several places. DOMDIFFER went quiet in the spring, came back over the summer, and has held the top rating since August. Most of its detections in that time came from fake calendar event lures.

One real DOMDIFFER attack starts with a fake Google Voice notification about a new voicemail.

The link passes a bot check that turns scanners away. The victim then sees what looks exactly like Google's sign-in page. It isn't a screenshot or a static copy. It's Google's real page, mirrored from the attacker's browser.

On the surface, this looks like fairly standard phishing: A notification lure, a bot check gate, then a sign-in page. What makes DOMDIFFER particularly effective lies beneath the surface.

AiTM kits relay a sign-in in one of four ways: Reverse proxy, replica page, pixel streaming, and DOM mirroring. In DOM mirroring, the attacker's own browser does the real sign-in. It sends the page's structure (the DOM) to the victim's browser, which rebuilds a working copy. Because a real browser does the sign-in, the kit sends Google genuine browser signals and handles new sign-in steps without needing extra code.

Replica pages are still the most common class, but DOM mirroring is the newest and the fastest growing in 2026. Laurseraph, the main criminal pixel-streaming kit, has switched to DOM mirroring, and kits such as BlueKit and PhantomDOM use it too.

Our research post, From reverse proxies to DOM mirroring, explains the four classes in detail.

DOM mirroring is only part of why DOMDIFFER breaks through security controls. The rest comes from how the kit reaches its victims and stays out of sight of the controls along the way. In the webinar on December 2, Luke will take apart DOMDIFFER and the other four kits in the top five. He'll show how each one works under the hood, and the tricks that get them past controls more often than other kits.

Why controls fall short

Passkeys are worth rolling out, and they stop AiTM phishing where they're enforced. But AiTM is now one payload among several, and attackers are getting creative with how they combine them.

Kits use AiTM interchangeably with payloads that passkeys don't touch. In authorization attacks such as device code phishing, the victim signs in on Microsoft's real page and grants the attacker access. Other kits skip the sign-in page altogether and trick the victim into installing a remote access tool instead.

Kits pick the payload to match the visitor. A Windows visitor might get a remote access tool, a Microsoft account device code phishing, and a Google account AiTM. Some lures now carry two kits at once, so closing one path leaves another open.

Each of these gaps ends the same way: A page loads in the browser, and a user acts on it. In the webinar, Luke will show attackers use these tricks in real attacks.

What the webinar reveals

DOMDIFFER is one of five kits that broke through most often from August to October. On December 2, Luke Jennings will reveal the other four in a live threat briefing. Join the webinar to see:

The other four kits in the top five, and the techniques behind each one

How DOMDIFFER works under the hood, and the tricks that get it past controls

Real attacks from the top kits, from the lure to the stolen session

How kits now choose the payload to match the visitor's platform

Which techniques and kits should be on your radar heading into 2027

If you can't make it, register anyway and you'll get a copy of the recording.

Register for the webinar now.

[6] BleepingComputer - New NFS2ROOT Linux flaw can let attackers gain root privileges
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 03:06:58 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/new-nfs2root-linux-flaw-can-let-attackers-gain-root-privileges/

[TAM METİN - 555 kelime]
​A 12-year-old critical vulnerability in the Linux kernel's Network File System (NFS) server, tracked as CVE-2026-99362, allows remote attackers to gain root privileges.

Found by ANSSI's Jérémy Jean (who submitted a fix in August), the flaw has been dubbed NFS2ROOT by cybersecurity firm OpenSec, which shared a full remote-root exploit and a patch with the NFSD maintainers in late September.

The vulnerability has existed since Linux kernel version 3.16 (released in March 2014), and it stems from a heap buffer overflow weakness in the Linux NFS server (nfsd).

NFS2ROOT affects systems with a Linux kernel older than 6.12.117, 6.18.61, 7.3.2, or 7.4-rc1 if nfsd (v4.1 or 4.2) is loaded and running and TCP port 2049 is reachable over the Internet.

Successful exploitation lets remote threat actors without privileges corrupt kernel memory and gain root access to vulnerable Linux servers exposed online through low-complexity attacks that don't require user interaction.

In NFS2ROOT attacks, the threat actors can exploit this flaw by sending maliciously crafted requests that cause the NFS server to write data beyond an allocated memory buffer. This lets them read kernel memory, bypass KASLR (which protects kernel memory addresses), and place a callback that can execute a command in a released memory area.

When the attacker closes the NFS connection, the kernel executes the embedded command with root privileges, allowing the attacker to gain root control of the unpatched Linux server.

​Although there are no reports of CVE-2026-99362 exploitation in the wild, security teams should apply the latest kernel security updates from their Linux distribution vendor immediately to block potential attacks, then reboot the system to ensure the patched kernel is in use.

Those who can't apply a patch immediately should restrict access to the NFS server over TCP port 2049 to trusted clients or networks. Additionally, if NFS sharing is not required on the system, admins can stop and disable the nfs-server service in /etc/nfs.conf.

"Port 2049 sits open on file servers, NAS appliances, build farms and Kubernetes storage nodes. Since 2014, the Linux kernel listening on it has let anyone who can reach it write into kernel memory before logging in," OpenSec said. "Kernel hardening does not remove the overflow. Patch every host."

While the NFS2ROOT attacks have no network signatures, security teams can still monitor for potential signs of exploitation attempts by checking for kernel warnings from nfsd4_check_resp_size, shells spawned by kernel workers on a file server, bursts of short NFSv4.1 sessions from one source, and kernel oopses with nfsd4_*, svc_* or call_usermodehelper* frames, according to OpenSec.

NFS2ROOT is the latest in a long series of Linux privilege escalation security flaws disclosed and patched since the start of the year, including RefluXFS, CIFSwitch, Dirty Frag, Fragnesia, PinTheft, Copy Fail, Pack2TheRoot, and DirtyDecrypt/DirtyCBC.

Adaptive Security's AI agents analyze email intent, context, and behavior to uncover phishing, business email compromise, and invoice fraud.

See what's getting through with a 14-day read-only scan of Microsoft 365 or Google Workspace, with no MX changes.

Palo Alto Networks warns of Fragnesia flaw exploited in PAN-OS attacks

[7] BleepingComputer - OpenAI warns GPT-6.2 Astra will hit usage limits 40% faster because it fixed a bug
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 16:06:22 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/artificial-intelligence/openai-warns-gpt-62-astra-will-hit-usage-limits-40-percent-faster-because-it-fixed-a-bug/

[TAM METİN - 388 kelime]
OpenAI confirmed that it fixed a 'bug' that incorrectly discounted GPT-6.2 usage, which means you'll now hit GPT usage limits 40% faster than before.

In a post on X, Tibo Sottiaux, who works on Codex and ChatGPT at OpenAI, admitted to a billing mistake that incorrectly discounted GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol usage for most users.

OpenAI says it has fixed the issue but warned that the same tasks will now consume more of users' allowances.

"We've fixed this now, so the same work will use more of your plan's usage allowance," Sottiaux explained.

"Comparing to last week, you will reach your limits 40% faster on GPT-6.2 Astra and 25% faster on Sol."

However, OpenAI says both models will still offer better usage efficiency than their predecessors, GPT-6 Astra and GPT-6.1 Sol.

OpenAI says it won't charge users for the usage difference caused by the mistake, but it is offering something in return for the disruption.

"As a small token to apologize for the mistake, we'll be adding one banked reset for everyone on Go, Plus, Pro, and Business," Sottiaux said.

I can confirm that a new reset has been added to ChatGPT, and it expires a month later. So, if you go to ChatGPT Settings > Usage and click Reset, you can restore your exhausted usage limits.

As much as I appreciate OpenAI giving its users a free usage reset, it's important to understand that this isn't equivalent to getting 40% less usage.

You're still going to use your tokens much faster, and it's unclear when OpenAI will reduce the costs.

It is worth noting that the change affects GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol usage, while other models retain their existing usage calculations.

[9] BleepingComputer - Fake paid expert interviews impersonate brands to steal Google logins
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 12:15:27 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/apple/fake-paid-expert-interviews-impersonate-brands-to-steal-google-logins/

[TAM METİN - 678 kelime]
An ongoing phishing campaign dubbed "GoodGuys" impersonates major brands, including Google, Nvidia, Accenture, and SpaceX, to steal corporate Google and Okta credentials using fake invitations to paid expert interviews and webinars.

The attacks offer recipients up to $600 for a 45-minute interview in an attempt to get them to sign in with their work accounts to participate.

The campaign was discovered by cybersecurity firm Push Security, which has tracked it since October 13 and found that they impersonate eight companies: Accenture, American Express, Coca-Cola, Culver's, Google Cloud, Nvidia, SpaceX, and Spotify.

The threat actors abuse legitimate email and event platforms, including HireVue, SendGrid, Mandrill, and Cvent, to distribute phishing links.

In one attack, a phishing email sent through HireVue invited recipients to attend a Google Cloud webinar about deploying AI applications into production.

The threat actors use domains themed around interviews, webinars, and research studies, creating brand-specific subdomains to host the phishing pages, such as:

accenture[.]international-interviews[.]com

nvidia[.]feedback-study[.]com

google[.]webinar-cloud[.]com

spotify[.]interview-experts[.]com

Clicking the phishing links typically takes recipients to a CAPTCHA challenge before displaying a website impersonating the company that supposedly sent the invitation.

Each website features customized branding and lures, with the Nvidia phishing site offering $500 to participate in an AI feedback study and the Accenture site offering $600 for an expert interview.

The sites ultimately prompt visitors to sign in with their Google accounts to validate their invitations, allowing the attackers to steal their credentials. The phishing kit specifically targets corporate accounts and rejects email addresses associated with Gmail, Yahoo, and Outlook.

Unlike adversary-in-the-middle (AiTM) phishing kits that use reverse proxies to relay legitimate login pages, GoodGuys uses fake authentication forms controlled by an attacker-controlled server.

After victims enter their Google credentials, the phishing page can display various multifactor authentication (MFA) challenges, including requests for SMS codes, authenticator codes, and Google sign-in prompts requiring users to select a matching number on their phones.

The kit also includes fake Okta authentication forms and support for Okta Verify push notifications.

Push Security believes the threat actors manually attempt to log in using the stolen credentials, instructing the phishing page to display whatever MFA or security challenges Google or Okta show during authentication.

Similar techniques were used in vishing-based data theft attacks linked to the ShinyHunters extortion gang earlier this year to steal corporate single sign-on (SSO) credentials.

Once the process is completed, the phishing kit redirects victims to another website or displays a message thanking them for their application.

The researchers believe the phishing kit may have been developed using an AI coding assistant, based on hidden accessibility labels found in every analyzed version of the kit.

"The sign-in card in every build carries two hidden labels: "Sign-in simulation" and "GoodGuys sign-in simulation." They sit in accessibility text that only screen readers announce, so a visitor never sees them. We think the label is a leftover from how the kit was first built," explained Push Security.

"Calling the page a sign-in "simulation" for a made-up company is the kind of framing someone would use to get an AI coding assistant past its guardrails when asking it to build a phishing page. The label then stayed in every build we've collected."

The researchers also discovered a potentially related phishing campaign that uses fake Coca-Cola interview invitations and the Evilginx phishing kit to target Google Ads accounts.

While both campaigns used the same abused SendGrid click tracker and identical Coca-Cola branding assets, differences in their code and infrastructure prevented researchers from conclusively linking the attacks to the same threat actor.

[10] BleepingComputer - Remus malware steals Gmail messages via fake Google Sheets extension
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 10:23:28 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/remus-malware-steals-gmail-messages-via-fake-google-sheets-extension/

[TAM METİN - 527 kelime]
The Remus infostealer is stealing Gmail messages through a stealthy malicious Chrome extension, a capability the malware operators marketed as a zero-day exploit in Google's email service.

On November 8, the Remus infostealer developer started advertising the alleged Gmail zero-day flaw that allowed the malware to collect emails even if victims changed their passwords and revoked all sessions.

Threat intelligence company Hudson Rock investigated the claims but came to inconclusive results. The researchers noted that evidence from the malware customers matched the seller's pitch, although there was no technical proof that a zero-day vulnerability was being exploited.

GenDigital and Invoke RE researchers published their independent findings yesterday, explaining that the advertised “zero-day” was just a malicious Chrome extension installed on the victims' browsers.

The extension is designed to steal unread emails from signed-in Gmail accounts. It can also survive password changes and session revocations because it remains installed in the victim's Chrome browser.

Its access to Gmail stops when the active session ends, and resumes again when the victim signs back in.

GenDigital discovered the extension after reverse-engineering the Remus infostealer. The researchers found no trace of an email-stealing module, suggesting that the functionality is implemented via an add-on.

“In a nutshell, the loaded module is a native DLL whose sole purpose is to install a malicious extension into Google Chrome, which, notably, is the only browser it targets,” GenDigital explains.

“The extension itself is bundled directly inside the DLL, stored as an unencrypted ZIP archive in its .rdata section.”

GenDigital explains that Chrome versions 137 and later removed a command-line option that allowed malware to load extensions.

Remus takes a complex route to overcome this restriction, editing the victim’s Chrome profile to register the malicious extension and then enabling ‘Developer Mode’ in the browser, which is required to run unpacked extensions.

The extension uses the name “Google Sheets,” copies the description of Google Docs Offline, and requests access to all websites.

Once active in the browser, it checks for unread emails every minute across up to 10 account slots and retrieves them in full, in raw format.

The extension sends the email subject, message body, sender, and victim’s email address to its command-and-control server, along with a campaign identifier.

Failed uploads are queued and retried, while a local record helps the extension keep track of what has succeeded so it doesn’t send duplicates.

Although the extension also parses attachment filenames, it does not send those to its operators, so this feature may not be fully implemented yet.

To defend against this threat, users are advised to download software only from trusted sources and scrutinize “chrome://extensions” for rogue installations or unexpected activation of Developer Mode.

[11] BleepingComputer - SYS010 malware abuses browser management to spy on Gmail, Outlook
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 06:50:17 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/sys010-malware-abuses-browser-management-to-spy-on-gmail-outlook/

[TAM METİN - 859 kelime]
A new version of the SYS01 infostealer malware delivered through Meta ads is enrolling browsers into enterprise-style management to force the installation of an extension that steals sensitive data, including Gmail and Outlook emails.

The ads lure Windows users into downloading free animated wallpapers that work as advertised but also deploy malware in the background during installation.

The campaign targets Facebook and Meta business accounts and gives the operator access to recent Gmail and Outlook messages in Chrome, and the ability to permanently delete emails.

Bitdefender Labs tracks the campaign as SYS010 and says the malware is likely a new version of the SYS01 infostealer, observed since at least 2022 in malvertising activity on Facebook.

The name reflects the researchers' assessment that this variant represents a major improvement over its predecessor, leading them to tag it as version 2, represented in binary as 010.

Researchers identified a malicious SYS010 ad on October 16 and found that the campaign was still ongoing, with hundreds of ads created.

Company telemetry shows that requests to the threat actor’s servers came from several countries in  Asia, Europe, North America, Africa, and South America.

Meta Ad Library' transparency page shows that one advertisement alone reached 6,275 users in the European Union, targeting anyone above 18.

In a report today, Bitdefender says most requests came from the Philippines, Thailand, Bangladesh, Poland, Mexico, Algeria, Belgium, Mongolia, and the United States, but emphasizes these figures do not reflect confirmed infections.

Working wallpaper installs thieving add-on

The ads impersonate the legitimate Wallpaper Engine (a renamed, digitally signed version of the MeisterTask desktop app built on Electron) and Lively Wallpapers applications, directing users to malicious download sites, some of them using domains designed to appear official.

After installing the malicious Windows app, users get the advertised animated wallpaper functionality, which helps disguise the malware running in the background.

According to Bitdefender researchers, the attacker forces Chrome, Edge, and Firefox to install malicious browser add-ons by abusing management tools designed for organizations.

A script named eclient.php is responsible for installing the malicious extensions by modifying browser management policies.

In Chrome, it enrolls the browser in an attacker-controlled Google admin console, allowing forced installation of extensions.

In Firefox, it uses an ExtensionSettings policy to force-install an add-on while disabling Developer Tools and abuse reporting.

For Edge, it can enroll the victim's Windows device in an attacker-controlled mobile device management (MDM) server to enable forced extension installation.

These changes require administrator permissions, which is solved by triggering the User Account Control (UAC) prompt and repeating it every three seconds up to 99 times until the user approves the action.

Bitdefender determined that the malicious add-ons pose as document tools, Docs Helper in Chrome and Document Hub in Firefox.

They note that the fake Docs Helper extension for Chrome uses an icon resembling the Google Docs logo but is distributed outside the Chrome Web Store.

The extensions also collect information about Facebook Pages, advertising and business accounts that the victim can access, including spending and payment details.

Gmail and Outlook email access

Bitdefender’s research also notes that the Chrome extension can read recent messages in Gmail and Outlook accounts and send their content to the attacker, as well as permanently delete selected messages.

This capability could let attackers intercept login codes and erase security alerts before victims see them, Bitdefender warns.

Furthermore, access to the same inbox could also make it harder to recover compromised accounts.

Similar behavior has been observed recently in another infostealer named Remus, which also uses a malicious extension to read messages in Gmail inboxes.

Remus infostealer takes a different approach to installing the extension in Chrome: it registers the add-on directly in the victim's browser profile and enables Developer Mode to allow the unpacked extension to run.

Although malvertising is often considered a lesser risk, it can still cause significant damage, as stolen advertising accounts are typically used to fund malvertising campaigns under the identity of trusted businesses.

Affected companies incur unauthorized advertising charges, and may lose access to their social media accounts, and have customer conversations exposed.

Bitdefender’s recommendation is to avoid downloading software from social-media ads and to refuse administrator prompts with an unclear purpose.

Additionally, an unexpected "managed by your organization" message in a browser should be a red flag. Individuals using a work-managed device should check with their IT team, while such a message on a personal computer warrants further investigation.

The cybersecurity company advises businesses to review who has access to their Meta accounts, remove permissions that are no longer needed, and check for unfamiliar ads, payment changes, or spending.

[12] Security Affairs - Drupal Warns of New Critical Flaw Exploited in the Wild
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 19:45:57 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/204085/security/drupal-warns-of-new-critical-flaw-exploited-in-the-wild.html

[TAM METİN - 462 kelime]
Drupal Warns of New Critical Flaw Exploited in the Wild

Drupal fixes a critical flaw exploited in the wild that could let attackers take over vulnerable websites.

Drupal has warned of a new highly critical vulnerability in Drupal Core that attackers are actively exploiting to take over vulnerable websites.

Tracked as CVE-2026-136716, the flaw is related to improper handling of unusual or incomplete installation states. It is connected to CVE-2026-132165, also known as Fail2Install, which Drupal patched on November 18. However, the previous fix did not address every vulnerable installation scenario.

“SA-CORE-2026-017 recently fixed several vulnerabilities within the Drupal core installer. However, certain sites in unusual or incomplete install states remain vulnerable to site takeover following that advisory, including production sites. It is not apparent in the user interface if a site is in the affected vulnerable state, and sites that are already compromised may no longer be in the vulnerable state.” Drupal maintainers warned.

The issue affects websites where the Drupal installer (install.php) remains remotely accessible. Drupal stressed that the vulnerability does not affect sites where administrators have already properly secured the installer.

One particularly concerning aspect is that administrators cannot easily identify affected installations through the Drupal interface. Moreover, sites that have already been compromised may no longer appear to be in the vulnerable state.

Drupal confirmed that CVE-2026-136716 is being exploited in the wild and urged administrators to investigate possible compromises.

“This is being exploited in the wild. Any site that left its installer remotely accessible following SA-CORE-2026-017 may still have been compromised.” continues the advisory.

Recommended actions include reviewing logs for unauthorized access and unexpected changes involving the main administrative account, known as user 1. Administrators should also verify that its email address is under their control and consider resetting its password.

The vulnerability has been addressed in the following releases:

Drupal 11.4.x: Update to 11.4.14

Drupal 11.3.x: Update to 11.3.23

Drupal 10.6.x: Update to 10.6.23

Drupal 11.0.x through 11.2.x and 10.5.x and earlier are end-of-life and will not receive security fixes.

After updating, administrators should run update.php to fix affected installation states and ensure that the installer is properly secured on all existing and new websites.

Drupal’s own usage statistics provide further evidence of the exposure. Data published on November 15, 2026, show that thousands of websites still report running Drupal 8.x and tens of thousands use Drupal 9.x, both unsupported branches. Although these figures cover only sites reporting their versions through Drupal’s update system, they highlight the persistence of outdated installations and the need for organizations to migrate to supported releases.

This week, the U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) added Drupal Core flaw CVE-2026-132165 to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog:

Follow me on Twitter: @securityaffairs and Facebook and Mastodon

[13] Security Affairs - Linux/EasyShell: MalwareMustDie Identifies One of the Earliest Publicly Documented RATs Built for LoongArch Linux
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 14:42:59 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/204067/malware/linux-easyshell-malwaremustdie-identifies-one-of-the-earliest-publicly-documented-rats-built-for-loongarch-linux.html

[TAM METİN - 1111 kelime]
Linux/EasyShell: MalwareMustDie Identifies One of the Earliest Publicly Documented RATs Built for LoongArch Linux

MalwareMustDie analyzes Linux/EasyShell, a Rust implant targeting Linux and macOS, with LoongArch support and stealth features to evade detection.

This case began with a shell script found on a victim’s node, which the author credits to 0xrb. MalwareMustDie’s researcher first read it as one more Chinese miner-style uninstaller or downloader. It wasn’t. The script installs software, and what it delivers is a Rust beacon implant.

MalwareMustDie wrote four reverse engineering notes, one forensics note, and two OSINT notes, then merged them into one report.

That restraint shapes everything that follows. This careful approach guides the entire analysis. The stager is hosted at hxxps://192[.]3.31[.]148:8443 and installs a payload called polkitd on both Linux and macOS. On Linux, it is saved as /usr/bin/polkitd, while on macOS it is placed in ~/Library/Caches/polkitd. The stager randomly chooses one of eight possible file names. The same server hosts the malware and receives its beacon communications over TCP port 8443. It is hosted by ColoCrossing (AS36352) in the United States.

The server provides seven different builds: l32, l64, l128, a32, a64, darwin_amd64 and darwin_arm64. The researcher describes the Linux x86 version, l32, as a Rust-based HTTPS beacon with all its required libraries built into the executable. Its build paths contain references to the EasyShell framework, including strings such as easyshell-http-beacon-implant and impl-core. For this reason, the researcher identifies the malware as Linux/EasyShell.

The l128 build targets LoongArch, a CPU architecture, and was compiled with rustc 1.96.0, while the others use 1.95.0. According to the researchers, this is one of the earliest publicly documented LoongArch64 RAT implementations he could find in public reporting, identified within the EasyShell multi-platform Rust implant family.

“I would like to note that this is one of the earliest publicly documented LoongArch64 RAT implementations I could find in public reporting (as of 2026-11-21), identified within the EasyShell multi-platform Rust implant family. “Earliest” here only means that I found no earlier public analysis; the LoongArch build itself is a framework default (chapter 7 addendum).” states the report. “Hands-on access: shell, file theft, credential plugins, SOCKS and relay pivot High. No monetisation code. Operator intent unknown (18.1). The macOS and LoongArch builds are framework defaults offered by every EasyShell stager, not a choice of this operator (18.2).”

Two caveats weaken any story about a targeted LoongArch campaign. The report says the macOS and LoongArch payloads aren’t this operator’s choice, because every EasyShell server offers them by default. The author also doubts the LoongArch build would load on a stock Debian loong64 or Kylin host without matching links or a compatible loader, and says it may never have been tested on real hardware.

The author didn’t run any sample. The beacon is an HTTPS POST to /api/v2/sync with an X-Client-Id header and a Windows Chrome 131 user agent. According to the report, the implant can open a remote shell, run plugins and scripts, upload and download files, and zip folders. It also offers SOCKS5 and relay tunnels, an outbound proxy, screenshots, process listing and killing, and self-deletion.

Credential dumping, token elevation and browser password theft aren’t built in. They come from a separate “Extensions” plugin set. The implant also checks for sandboxes, looking at DMI data, hypervisor names, mouse activity and system resources. Then it renames itself like a kernel thread, for example [kworker/0:0].

A third-party Joe Sandbox run on November 21 gives a useful reality check. It executed another l32 copy on Ubuntu 20.04 for about five minutes. The copy had the same size, 3,769,372 bytes, but a different hash, and it made three TCP connections to the C2, forked a child that called prctl, uname and a thread sleep, and created an empty hidden file. The score was 2 out of 100 with no YARA or Suricata match, which says more about the malware waiting for someone else to show up than about its harmlessness.

That hash difference matters. The server writes random bytes into one slot of every payload it generates, so each victim gets a different file.

“YARA family rule MMD_Linux_EasyShell_Rust_Implant on memory dumps and new binaries (it still matches the builds without the crate name); do not rely on the hashes for other victims.” MalwareMustDie warns.

The first draft of the report dates from November 19, and an addendum on November 22 documented the per-download hashes and the developer repository.

On attribution, the author is careful. EasyShell is a Chinese red team C2 framework sold through trial and member programs, and its public repository matches the samples. It isn’t VShell and has no Kinsing or SystemTen link, although the stager is based on a VShell template. No operator is named, and the author says the tool’s build markers describe its build environment, not whoever runs this C2.

There’s one loose thread. A separate chapter describes an intrusion on a Chinese mobile-malware hosting server, and a single shell-history line there re-ran the EasyShell stager from the same C2. The author rates the link between the two operators as medium confidence. A note about a December 2025 incident that used the same stager template at a different IP is marked unverified.

For security teams, the l32 version does not have its own way to remain on an infected system, although its plugins may add this feature. The researcher suggests looking for processes that use names normally associated with kernel threads, such as [kworker/0:0] or [rcu_sched], but have a real executable file listed under /proc/pid/exe. Another warning sign is HTTPS POST traffic to /api/v2/sync with an X-Client-Id header.

On macOS, defenders should look for executable files in user Caches folders that lack a quarantine attribute, code signatures without a Team ID, and security permission requests (TCC prompts) that mention polkitd.

The report also includes YARA, Sigma and osquery detection rules. The researcher recommends starting in detection-only mode. These rules were tested against known malware strings, but not against a large collection of legitimate software to check for false positives.

If a system looks infected, there is one important rule: do not kill the fake kernel thread immediately. The researcher recommends first isolating and preserving the system before terminating any process.

The report ships YARA, Sigma and osquery rules, meant to start in detect-only mode. They were checked on strings, not through a full retro-hunt on legitimate software.

“Golden rule: do not kill the fake kernel thread first. Collect its process state and memory, freeze it, cut its network, then clean. Killing it first loses the live config, the session keys and the tunnel table.” concludes the report.

Follow me on Twitter: @securityaffairs and Facebook and Mastodon

(SecurityAffairs – hacking, LoongArch Linux)
