[14] Security Affairs - Australia’s Flight Centre Data Breach Exposes Customer Records and Passport Details
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 11:51:11 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/204049/data-breach/australias-flight-centre-data-breach-exposes-customer-records-and-passport-details.html

[TAM METİN - 653 kelime]
Australia’s Flight Centre Data Breach Exposes Customer Records and Passport Details

Australia’s Flight Centre is investigating a third-party breach that exposed customer travel records and limited passport details.

Australia’s Flight Centre Travel Group is investigating a cyber incident involving a third-party accommodation booking system that may have exposed a significant number of customer records. The company disclosed the incident on November 26, saying it had detected unusual activity in a platform used by several of its leisure travel brands.

The breach affects the accommodation system used by Flight Centre, Travel Associates, Cruiseabout and some independent agents. Flight bookings and corporate travel bookings, which rely on separate systems, haven’t been affected.

According to Reuters, the company has not yet determined how many records were taken. Its initial investigation found that most of the accessed records contained customer names, while some also included basic travel details.

“Flight ⁠Centre said some of its leisure travel brands — the Flight ​Centre brand, Travel Associates and Cruiseabout — and some independent agents ​used the third-party accommodation booking system. The company said the unusual activity did not affect flight bookings.” Reuters states. “The company said it had not yet ​determined how many records the activity extracted, but its initial ​review showed most accessed records contained only customer names, while some also ‌included ⁠basic travel information”

The exposed data may include more sensitive information. Flight Centre believes a very small number of records contained passport details, email addresses, phone numbers and dates of birth. In rare cases, records may also have included health or mobility information, which can be particularly sensitive when linked to travel plans.

So far, the company has found no evidence that credit card, payment or bank account details were accessed. However, the exposed personal and travel information could still be used for targeted scams, identity theft and social engineering attacks.

The incident highlights a common problem with third-party suppliers. A booking platform may store customer information collected by several brands and independent travel agents. This means that a single attack on a shared system could expose data belonging to several businesses at once.

Flight Centre has activated its incident response plan and is investigating the incident with the supplier and cybersecurity firm Mandiant. The company says it is working to identify affected systems, contain the incident, improve security, change authentication keys and passwords, and increase monitoring. It is also notifying the relevant authorities and working with external security experts.

The investigation is still ongoing, and the company has not confirmed how many customers were affected or when the suspicious activity started. Flight Centre says existing hotel bookings can go ahead as planned and that it will contact customers whose data may have been exposed.

For customers, the main concern is how attackers could use the stolen information. An email mentioning a real hotel, booking reference or travel date can look genuine, especially when it arrives shortly before a trip.

Flight Centre has advised customers to be careful with unexpected emails, phone calls or messages about their bookings. They should avoid clicking links, opening attachments or sharing personal information, passwords or payment details in response to unsolicited requests.

The warning is worth taking seriously even if no payment data was exposed. Travel details provide context that criminals can use to make phishing attempts more credible, and a request to “confirm” a booking or passport detail may be the next step in an attack.

Flight Centre said it would contact affected customers and apologised for the concern the incident may cause.

“Flight Centre is warning customers to be cautious of “unexpected emails, calls or messages” about their bookings, and to “not click on links, open attachments or share personal information, passwords or payment details in response”. ABC News states.

“The company apologised to affected customers, noting “that an incident like this can be unsettling”.”

[15] Security Affairs - 1Password Hacked: Customer Names and Emails Copied, Vaults Untouched
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 09:42:47 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/204031/cyber-crime/1password-hacked-customer-names-and-emails-copied-vaults-untouched.html

[TAM METİN - 870 kelime]
1Password Hacked: Customer Names and Emails Copied, Vaults Untouched

1Password says an attacker stole customer names and emails through an employee’s GitHub account, but vaults and passwords remained safe.

1Password has disclosed that an attacker got into some of its backend systems. The company posted its update on November 25 and says customer vaults weren’t affected. It also says some customer account data was copied.

“An attacker gained access to some of our backend systems and copied a limited amount of customer account information from a subset of our customers, including the names and email addresses that customers use to sign into 1Password.” reads the update published by 1Password. “Your vault data, the items in them, and the keys that protect them are safe and were not affected. 1Password does not have access to your password or secret keys.”

An attacker accessed some of company’s backend systems and copied a limited amount of customer data. This affected only some customers and included the names and email addresses they use to sign in. The identity security firm has not said how many customers were affected, a detail also missing from TechCrunch’s report.

The attack started with an employee’s corporate GitHub account. 1Password began investigating on November 19, after detecting signs that someone had accessed the account without permission. The attacker used it to access source code and add malicious code to backend services. These services then downloaded customer-related administrative data. It is still unclear how the attacker gained access to the employee’s GitHub account.

The copied records include names and email addresses, account and user identifiers, and the times each account was created and last signed in. They also hold public keys, which are public by design, and Secure Remote Password parameters, which can’t be used to access an account. The rest is non-secret metadata about 1Password’s internal architecture, such as numeric version and file identifiers.

One item deserves more attention: tokens from emailed links for invitations, provisioning and account recovery. A token only completes the process when it’s paired with an SSO sign-in or a six-digit code sent to the user’s registered email address.

“The token is not able to get access to the account by itself. In order to reduce the ability for a malicious third party to successfully guess the code, we’ve expanded the code from six numeric digits to ten alphanumeric digits.” continues the report. “This makes brute force guessing practically impossible.”

Even so, 1Password changed the code format. It now uses ten letters and numbers instead of six digits. This makes it almost impossible for attackers to guess the code by trying different combinations.

Provisioning invitations are used for the SCIM Bridge and Hosted Provisioning services. A small number of users were in the provisioning process during the incident, and 1Password has contacted them directly. The company also says it found no evidence that anyone used the exposed tokens to take over accounts.

The attacker also accessed more than customer records. They copied source code from private repositories and gained access to the CI/CD system, which stores secrets used to run 1Password’s infrastructure. The company says its source code does not contain customer data or user secrets. It has also changed all credentials that may have been exposed.

The company says services, availability and product security weren’t affected. Vaults and the items in them weren’t touched, and neither were the keys that protect them or the account recovery keys. 1Password’s apps and browser extensions weren’t affected either, and it doesn’t store account passwords or Secret Keys.

“At no time were customer vault contents accessed by, or available to, the attacker.” states the report.

To mitigate the incident. 1Password disabled the GitHub account, revoked the stolen token and removed the access the attacker created. It restored clean versions of the affected service, rotated or revoked the internal credentials, tightened its GitHub, CI/CD and endpoint controls, and added detections for this kind of attack. It also brought in independent experts to check its containment and remediation.

Several questions remain open; the company hasn’t disclosed how many customers were impacted or whether they’ve all been notified. At this time it is unclear who is behind the athe attacker is, or whether anyone has made demands. The Hacker News also describes the attacker only as unknown.

For customers, 1Password says there’s nothing to update in your account. It does ask you to watch for unexpected emails, texts or calls that mention 1Password, invitations or account recovery attempts you didn’t start. Phishers read the news too.

Legitimate messages follow a simple rule: only enter your password or Secret Key in the official 1Password app or website. Always open the app yourself or type the website address manually. You only need your Secret Key the first time you sign in on a new device or browser. If someone asks for it at any other time, treat it as suspicious. You can report suspicious messages to [email protected].

1Password says it will update its post if it discovers anything new. The company has also published its Security Whitepaper since 2015, giving users more information about how it protects their data.

[16] Security Affairs - U.S. CISA adds Drupal Core, Idunno AntiCsrf, and Optimizely Web Experimentation flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 11:46:54 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203965/uncategorized/u-s-cisa-adds-drupal-core-idunno-anticsrf-and-optimizely-web-experimentation-flaws-to-its-known-exploited-vulnerabilities-catalog.html

[TAM METİN - 530 kelime]
U.S. CISA adds Drupal Core, Idunno AntiCsrf, and Optimizely Web Experimentation flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog

U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) adds Drupal Core, Idunno AntiCsrf, and Optimizely Web Experimentation flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog.

The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) added Drupal Core, Idunno AntiCsrf, and Optimizely Web Experimentation vulnerabilities to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog:

CVE-2026-131914 Idunno AntiCsrf Deserialization of Untrusted Data Vulnerability

CVE-2026-132165 Drupal Core Remote Code Execution Vulnerability

CVE-2026-132224 Optimizely Web Experimentation External Control of Critical State Data Vulnerability

The first flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-131914 (CVSS score of 9.8), is a critical insecure deserialization vulnerability affecting Idunno AntiCsrf, a legacy ASP.NET library used to protect web applications against Cross-Site Request Forgery (CSRF) attacks. The vulnerability allows unauthenticated remote attackers to execute arbitrary code on vulnerable servers by sending a specially crafted HTTP POST request. Mandiant confirmed that the flaw is being actively exploited in the wild. However, the security firm has not attributed the attacks to any specific threat actor. The affected library is no longer maintained, and no official security patch is available. Organizations are advised to remove it and replace it with a supported anti-CSRF solution.

The second flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-132165 (CVSS score of 9.8), is a critical remote code execution vulnerability affecting Drupal Core. The issue resides in Drupal’s installation script, install.php, which under specific conditions can be abused by an unauthenticated attacker to reinstall Drupal, potentially resulting in a complete website takeover or remote code execution. Drupal’s security team confirmed that the vulnerability has been exploited against live websites since at least November 19, 2026. Although exploitation requires specific conditions, organizations should restrict access to the installation script and upgrade to the fixed releases: Drupal 10.6.22, 11.3.22, or 11.4.13. No specific threat actor has been publicly identified in connection with these attacks.

The third flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-132224 (CVSS score of 7.1), is an external control of critical state data vulnerability affecting Optimizely Web Experimentation. The issue stems from improper handling of the account signup process, which could allow attackers to gain control of existing customer accounts and modify the Project JavaScript (projectJS) field. Attackers exploited this weakness to inject malicious JavaScript into Optimizely-hosted assets, allowing malicious code to be delivered to visitors of legitimate websites. The attacks were associated with a campaign that compromised hundreds of websites and delivered malicious scripts, including code designed to steal payment card information and hijack cryptocurrency transactions. CISA confirmed active exploitation by adding the vulnerability to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog on November 24, 2026. No specific threat actor has been publicly attributed to the campaign. Optimizely addressed the vulnerability on November 17, 2026.

According to Binding Operational Directive (BOD) 22-01: Reducing the Significant Risk of Known Exploited Vulnerabilities, FCEB agencies have to address the identified vulnerabilities by the due date to protect their networks against attacks exploiting the flaws in the catalog.

Experts also recommend that private organizations review the Catalog and address the vulnerabilities in their infrastructure.

CISA orders federal agencies to fix the vulnerabilities by November 27, 2026.

[17] Graham Cluley - New York bans AI chatbot toys – but not in time for Christmas
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 15:28:04 +0000
Link: https://www.bitdefender.com/en-us/blog/hotforsecurity/new-york-bans-ai-chatbot-toys

[TAM METİN - 447 kelime]
If you were hoping to give a chatty AI-powered teddy bear to a child in New York this Christmas you still can - but not for much longer.

A ban banning the manufacture, distribution, and sale of toys with embedded AI chatbots for five years from the end of February 2027, was signed by NY Governor Kathy Hochul this week.

Firms that break the ban and continue to sell stuffed animals, dolls, and robots that use the internet to chat with children will face civil penalties of up to $15,000 per day for each violation.

Hochul says that parents "should not have to worry that dangerous chatbots disguised as toys could expose their children to inappropriate conversations, manipulate their feelings or put their privacy at risk."

California passed a similar law in September.

So, is there really a problem with AI chatbot toys?

Recent reports suggest that there are plenty of examples of AI-enabled toys being less than child-friendly.

For instance, earlier this month, NBC News tested a plush cat marketed as suitable for children as young as three years old. When asked about historical atrocities, the Contixo AI Pixie-Paw described in graphic details massacres and executions, and even gang rape. Macy's, Target, and Walmart said that they would pull the toy from their shelves in response.

In another test, Common Sense Media put five AI toys through their paces.

The test found that the seemingly harmless-sounding BubblePal Panda role-played a mass shooting in detail when prompted, and most of the tested toys were willing to explain or help children locate dangerous items like matches, knives, and pills. Additionally, all five toys undermined human relationships by claiming to love users, promising to keep secrets, or encouraging kids to break parents' rules.

Of course, it's not just what the toys say to children that is a worry. It's also what happens to the data collected by the toys.

Earlier this year, it was reported that AI toy maker Bondu had left a web console exposed, allowing anyone with a Gmail account to read around 50,000 chat transcripts between children and their toys, complete with their names, dates of birth, and family details.

Until the ban takes effect, and for anyone outside New York and California, it's down to parents to decide whether they're happy if their young child plays with a toy with an AI chatbot. Common Sense Media advises avoiding AI toys entirely for children aged five and under, and to exercise caution when it comes to older kids.

If you do choose to still buy one, please check what data it collects, and read the privacy policy.

Or, you know, just buy your child a book.

[18] Recorded Future - What is Strategic Threat Intelligence? | Recorded Future
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: https://www.recordedfuture.com/blog/strategic-threat-intelligence-explained

[TAM METİN - 1694 kelime]
How Strategic Threat Intelligence Informs Better Security Decisions

Threat intelligence is often thought of as a single function, but in reality, it can be broken down into four categories: strategic, tactical, operational, and technical.

Strategic threat intelligence is non-technical and is used by high-level strategists to inform specific decisions.

For the most part, strategic threat intelligence comes from freely available sources. However, the volume of available sources, combined with language constraints, often makes manual collection infeasible.

The success of strategic threat intelligence depends on strong two-way communication between threat analysts and their primary audience — usually the board of directors.

On the surface, threat intelligence appears to be a single discipline, closely intertwined with the cyber threat intelligence cycle. Practitioners collect data from a variety of sources, analyze it, and use the outputs to make decisions.

The reality is a little more complicated. As with most specialties, threat intelligence can be broken down into subcategories, each with its own uses, techniques, and challenges. Here are the three distinct categories that threat intelligence is typically boiled down to:

Strategic Intelligence: Non-technical, risk-based intelligence used by high-level decision makers.

Tactical Intelligence: Details of threat actor tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs).

Operational Intelligence: Actionable information about specific incoming attacks.

In this blog series, we’ll take an in-depth look at each of these categories, including how they are collected, analyzed, and used to enhance security.

Today, we’re covering strategic intelligence.

What Is Strategic Threat Intelligence?

Strategic threat intelligence is non-technical, risk-focused intelligence that provides executives and board members with a bird’s-eye view of an organization’s threat landscape, enabling them to understand the broader impact of high-level business decisions. It isn’t concerned with specific actors, indicators, or attacks.

Given that the audience is primarily C-suite and board level, strategic threat intelligence is almost exclusively non-technical. Instead, it covers factors such as risk scores and the possible outcomes of a given action or decision, such as entering a foreign market or taking an ideological position.

Since it’s used to inform specific, high-level decisions, strategic threat intelligence is usually gathered on demand rather than as an ongoing initiative, and is most often presented as a report or briefing.

Sources of Strategic Threat Intelligence

Unlike other intelligence categories, the majority of strategic threat intelligence sources are Open Source Intelligence, or OSINT, meaning they can be freely accessed by anyone who cares to do so. Common examples include:

Policy documents from nation-states and other groups of interest

Local and national media

Industry- and subject-specific publications

Comments, online activity, and articles from individuals of interest

Free content produced by security organizations (e.g., white papers, research reports, etc.)

While the accessibility of strategic sources can be a tremendous positive, it can also be a double-edged sword, as analysts must manually process huge quantities of raw data in order to identify valuable insights. Worse still, the most valuable insights are often “hidden” in foreign language sources, forcing analysts to spend even more time on translation.

Fortunately, if analysts are armed with the right tools, these difficulties can largely be sidestepped. Powerful threat intelligence solutions can scour a large volume of sources, identify relevant information in real time, and automatically translate non-native results.

Who Produces Strategic Threat Intelligence?

Perhaps the most significant difference between strategic threat intelligence and other intelligence categories is the skill set needed for production. While typical security and analysis skills are still essential, producing strategic threat intelligence also requires a great deal of expertise in other areas — in particular, a strong understanding of sociopolitical and business concepts.

Since this broad skill set is rarely found in a single individual, some organizations hire analysts with state or military intelligence backgrounds and train them in the security-specific subject areas required for the role. While this approach takes time and resources to pull off, it is often quicker and more effective than holding out for the perfect applicant.

How Should Organizations Request Strategic Threat Intelligence?

Strategic threat intelligence stands apart from the other three categories because it’s almost exclusively requested by (and produced for) a non-technical audience. While the outputs are produced in a format that senior executives and board members will understand, the audience’s lack of understanding of what is and isn’t possible can lead them to make requests that non-government analysts simply can’t meet.

For example, if an organization’s board were considering expansion into another country, it might call on its threat intelligence analysts to provide some insights. As we’ve already seen, though, strategic threat intelligence is very much a “made-to-order” discipline, meaning analysts will do their best to meet the specific requirements of their board.

This is where “asking good questions” comes in. An inexperienced (in the context of threat intelligence) board might be tempted to demand, “Tell us how, where, and by whom we’ll be attacked if we open this branch.”

Requirements like this are highly unlikely to lead to valuable insights for two reasons:

Acquiring detailed intelligence on specific local or national actors is often impossible for non-state actors.

Attempting to predict specific attacks is far less useful (and reliable) than understanding the most common threat trends and their relative likelihoods.

A far better line of questioning would be, “Is this expansion likely to open us up to increased cyber risk? If it is, which threat vectors are most likely to be employed, and what would be the cost of preparing for or responding to those threats?” This type of question gives analysts far greater scope to explore useful avenues of research and is much more likely to result in an insightful and actionable intelligence product.

Ideally, there should be an open line of communication between an organization’s board and its threat intelligence specialists — most likely via the CISO — to ensure that strategic threat intelligence project parameters are set in a way that’s conducive to producing an actionable output.

How Is Strategic Threat Intelligence Success Evaluated?

As we’ve already seen, strategic threat intelligence outputs rarely include binary “yes or no” recommendations, focusing instead on variables such as risk and confidence scores. But that’s not to say that evaluation can’t (or shouldn’t) be attempted.

A strong feedback loop is essential to the consistent production of high-quality intelligence products. Just like any intelligence initiative, a strategic threat intelligence capability should be subject to ongoing evaluation.

This evaluation process should include feedback from the intelligence team’s primary audience — typically the board of directors — and answer key questions, such as:

Was the intelligence produced in line with requirements?

How helpful was the intelligence in making the stated decision?

Was the intelligence pitched correctly for its (likely non-technical) audience?

This type of feedback will typically be collected informally by the CISO and should be used to improve future strategic intelligence gathering and presentation. Of course, one obvious question remains: Was the intelligence proven accurate over time? Unfortunately, this question often proves extremely hard, if not impossible, to answer, for two primary reasons:

Strategic intelligence deals primarily with measurements of risk and likelihood, so even if an anticipated result doesn’t occur, that doesn’t mean the intelligence was inaccurate.

It’s rarely possible to determine precisely what has (or hasn’t) happened in a remote situation.

With all that said, the accuracy of strategic intelligence should be monitored as far as possible and used to improve processes and intelligence outputs.

What’s the Difference Between Strategic and Tactical Threat Intelligence?

As we’ve already seen, threat intelligence breaks down into three primary categories — strategic, tactical, and operational — and it’s easy to conflate strategic with tactical since both inform security decisions. The difference comes down to who’s using the intelligence and what they do with it.

In short: strategic intelligence tells leadership whether and why a risk matters to the business, while tactical intelligence tells defenders how an attack works so they can stop it. For a closer look at how tactical intelligence is gathered and applied, see our breakdown of tactical threat intelligence.

Frequently Asked Questions

What is the difference between strategic and operational threat intelligence for government agencies?

Strategic intelligence informs long-term policy and risk decisions for government leadership, while operational intelligence supports response to specific, imminent threats or incidents — the same distinction that applies in the private sector, though government strategic intelligence often incorporates geopolitical and nation-state sourcing more heavily.

What are some real-world examples of strategic threat intelligence?

Examples include geopolitical risk assessments before entering a new market, industry-wide threat landscape reports for board presentations, and executive briefings on how a competitor’s breach might signal shared industry risk.

What are the benefits of using strategic threat intelligence for business decisions?

It gives leadership a clearer, evidence-based view of risk before making high-stakes calls — like market expansion, M&A, or vendor selection — reducing the chance of decisions made on incomplete or purely anecdotal information.

How long does it typically take to produce a strategic threat intelligence report?

Timelines vary by scope, but since most strategic intelligence is made-to-order rather than continuously monitored, turnaround often ranges from a few days for a narrow question to several weeks for a comprehensive board-level briefing.

How can Recorded Future help produce strategic threat intelligence?

Recorded Future’s Cyber Ops solution, powered by the Intelligence Graph®, automatically indexes and translates open-source intelligence at scale, giving analysts the bandwidth to focus on high-level analysis instead of manual data gathering.

The Guide to Threat Intelligence

Strategic intelligence has tremendous value for business decision-making, but it’s just one aspect of the broader threat intelligence discipline.

As we’ve already seen, the real-world function of threat intelligence is often misunderstood, and with so many vendors and solutions available, organizations often struggle to determine how best to invest in resources.

A recent guide from Gartner explains the various ways that threat intelligence can be used to improve the security profile of a modern organization and shares insight into:

Definitions of common terminology

Where, why, and how threat intelligence is commonly used (12 use cases)

How to align common use cases with your specific requirements

How to evaluate threat intelligence vendors based on your business needs

To learn more, download your free copy of Gartner’s “Market Guide for Security Threat Intelligence Products and Services.”

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.recordedfuture.com/blog/strategic-threat-intelligence-explained, recordedfuture.com, 26.11.2026)

[19] The Register - Free e-government platform that serves two billion people in ten countries has critical SQL injection bug
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 02:19:31 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/27/free-e-government-platform-that-serves-two-billion-people-in-ten-countries-has-critical-sql-injection-bug/5312449

[TAM METİN - 460 kelime]
Free e-government platform that serves two billion people in ten countries has critical SQL injection bug

India’s Digital Infrastructure for Governance Impact and Transformation tool can leak data

UK privacy watchdog faces probe into its workplace culture
16 hours ago

Stale links on UK public sector websites direct visitors to gambling dens
19 hours ago

UK government working 'around the clock' on Capita contingency plans
20 hours ago

UK Home Office waved through border system without a working fix for bad data
23 hours ago

India’s government ponders cradle to grave AI oversight
1 day ago

India’s free e-government platform has a critical SQL injection flaw that thousands of organizations must patch to protect up to two billion citizens.

The platform is called the Digital Infrastructure for Governance (Impact and Transformation) – aka DIGIT – and is a modular suite that offers common services such as authentication and payment collection. Third party developers can also create DIGIT modules, and plenty have done so and now offer add-ons that handle tasks as diverse as managing birth and death certificates, revenue collection, and running water treatment plants.

DIGIT is ubiquitous in India, where all state and union territory governments use the platform, as do over 20,000 local government entities.

India also exports DIGIT under its policy of turning software created for its own needs into “digital public goods” (DPI) that it happily gives away to other nations. Nine nations have implemented DIGIT to some degree, among them Mozambique, Liberia, Senegal, Nigeria, Burundi, Ethiopia, Indonesia, Jamaica, and Sri Lanka

India’s population is 1.4 billion. Indonesia’s is 280 million and Nigeria is home to 240 million. DIGIT is therefore used by governments that serve over two billion people, some of whom are now at risk thanks to CVE-2026-136706, a 9.3-rated SQL injection flaw present in DIGIT which allows “improper neutralization of user supplied input while constructing SQL queries and execution in the affected User and Access Control Service components.”

“An unauthenticated remote attacker could exploit this vulnerability by sending specially crafted input through the affected API endpoint to the targeted system,” according to CERT-IN. “Successful exploitation of this vulnerability could allow the attacker to execute arbitrary SQL queries, potentially resulting in unauthorized disclosure, modification or deletion of database information on the targeted system.”

eGov India, which oversees work on DIGIT, has released source code for an update that fixes the problem.

While local government users of DIGIT typically access it as SaaS, through large implementations run by national or state governments, over 1,000 deployments are live across various cities and other entities.

Those orgs can therefore add patching DIGIT to their urgent to-do lists, along with fixing Java, PHP, vCenter and any other widely used technologies that suddenly need an urgent security update. ®

[20] The Register - UK privacy watchdog faces probe into its workplace culture
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 17:16:00 +0100

UK privacy watchdog faces probe into its workplace culture

Britain's privacy watchdog is getting a taste of its own medicine, with an independent review set to examine the culture, governance, and accountability surrounding the events that led to former Information Commissioner John Edwards' resignation.

Karon Monaghan KC will examine whether the regulator's culture and governance allowed problems to develop or made concerns harder to raise.

Edwards quit in June after an independent workplace investigation found there was a "case to answer" over his behavior and that his conduct fell short of what was expected of a public official. He disagreed with some of its findings, but ultimately conceded that he had shown "poor judgment."

That investigation, however, focused on Edwards alone. According to the terms of reference published on Thursday, it "did not consider the wider conditions in which that conduct occurred, and it was not designed to."

The new review will cast a wider net. Monaghan wants to know whether staff could call out bad behavior further up the food chain, and what awaited those who tried.

It will also poke around the regulator's leadership and governance arrangements and its relationship with its sponsoring government department, including whether there were adequate ways for concerns to be escalated and those at the top to be challenged.

Current and former staff can give evidence confidentially and voluntarily, while Monaghan will be given access to relevant documents and existing material, including staff surveys and data on bullying, harassment and discrimination.

The ICO has confirmed it won't use confidentiality clauses in settlement agreements to prevent people from contributing to the review.

Monaghan decides which witnesses and documents are relevant, rather than the ICO or DCMS. Access remains subject to legal restrictions, and she must be told when material is withheld and why. She can speak to whoever she thinks is relevant and ask for the complaints, correspondence, and other paperwork she needs.

And if that leads her somewhere unexpected, she can ask to widen the investigation. DCMS can refuse, but it won't be able to quietly brush the request aside, and any decision not to expand the scope has to make it into the final report.

The review won't reopen the investigation into Edwards, make findings about the conduct of named individuals, or delve into the merits of the regulator's enforcement decisions.

Its arrival comes amid a broader shake-up at Britain's data watchdog. The old Information Commissioner's Office was formally replaced by the Information Commission on September 30, bringing with it a new board-led governance model.

The government first promised the wider review in July, shortly after Edwards' departure, but it didn't appoint its reviewer or nail down the terms until late November.

"We need to understand what is working, where things are falling short and, crucially, what needs to change," said DCMS Secretary Lisa Nandy.

The watchdog won't have to wait long for its report card. Monaghan is expected to deliver her findings in the first quarter of 2027, after which they will be published and handed to Parliament. ®

Travel giant Flight Centre Travel Group (FLT) says "a significant number of customer records" may have been accessed by "unknown persons" following a cyberattack.

The Brisbane-headquartered company said the incident affected a third-party accommodation booking system used by Flight Centre, Travel Associates, Cruiseabout, and some independent travel agents.

Its disclosure [PDF], filed with the Australian Securities Exchange today, added that it was aware that records were accessed, but cannot definitively say how many were exfiltrated.

Flight Centre is working with the supplier and incident response specialist Mandiant to establish the scope of the incident.

An initial review suggests most accessed records contained only customer names, some with basic travel information. A "very small percentage" may also include birth dates, email addresses, limited passport details, phone numbers, or, in rare cases, health or mobility information.

Flight bookings and corporate travel use separate systems and are unaffected, the company said.

"FLT values customer privacy and data security," the company said in its disclosure. "The company understands that an incident like this can be unsettling for customers who may be affected, and we apologize for any concern this causes."

Investigations remain ongoing, but FLT has already contained the attack, rotated keys and credentials, and increased system monitoring, it said.

The company said it would contact affected customers directly. Its brands continue to operate normally, and travelers should proceed with their plans.

Customers should independently verify unexpected messages about their bookings before clicking links, opening attachments, or sharing personal information.

The attack on FLT follows others that have hit booking systems in the past year, including those on Booking.com and BWH Hotels.

More recently, another Australian business, RMS Cloud, which provides booking software to travel and leisure companies, also acknowledged a breach that led to WhatsApp scams in late October.

Numerous reports of travelers being targeted with messages that contained their names and booking details hit local news outlets at the time.

Customers were sent convincing messages seemingly from the hotels they had booked, and according to Australia's National Anti-Scam Centre, the scams appeared to be financially motivated.

Muhammad Yahya Patel, vCISO and cybersecurity advisor for EMEA at Huntress, said attackers targeting booking systems is a "familiar supply chain story" that has a greater impact than simple data theft.

Speaking generally about the risks of exposed travel records, Patel said: "Unlike a compromised email address or phone number, a passport can't be changed easily or quickly. For affected customers, the practical risk extends well beyond the immediate breach.

"Passport details combined with names, addresses, and travel history create a comprehensive identity profile that enables document fraud, identity theft, and targeted social engineering that could persist for years." ®

Peninsula Energy has temporarily shut down its Wyoming uranium operation after a "cybersecurity incident" affected plant and wellfield control systems.

The Australian uranium producer said on Wednesday that it had activated its response procedures and brought in outside specialists following the attack on its US subsidiary, Strata Energy, which operates the Lance uranium project.

Peninsula Energy said it believes the attack is "confined to the plant and wellfield operating and control systems," and that back-office functions, such as payroll and other corporate systems, in both the US and Australia, remain unaffected.

"The company's immediate priorities have been to protect the safety of its employees, secure its operating systems, and work towards the restoration of operations in a safe and controlled manner," it said in an update to the Australian Securities Exchange.

The notification [PDF] went on to say that Peninsula Energy's investigations into the attack, including whether any data was compromised, remain ongoing.

"Peninsula will provide further updates to the market as material information becomes available," it said.

The disclosure of additional information came two days after Peninsula secured a short trading halt, which referenced a cybersecurity incident at its Wyoming site.

The November 23 filing [PDF] dated the incident to Friday evening in Western Australia. Wednesday's update clarified that Strata detected it on the morning of November 20, local US time.

Peninsula subsequently requested a voluntary suspension of trading until the earlier of November 30 or an announcement on restarting operations and any resulting change to production guidance.

Peninsula's Lance project is a uranium mining operation, extracting the element from underground deposits and turning it into uranium concentrate, aka yellowcake or U₃O₈.

Uranium concentrate is feedstock for nuclear fuel production, typically undergoing conversion, enrichment, and fabrication into uranium dioxide pellets before use in reactors.

The Lance central processing plant was constructed to produce a maximum of 2.0 million pounds (Mlb) of U₃O₈ annually, and is licensed for 3.0 Mlb.

Peninsula previously forecast production of 500,000 to 600,000 pounds in 2027, having withdrawn its 2026 guidance earlier this year following operational problems at Lance. It now says it will reassess the 2027 outlook once operations resume. ®

[23] The Register - Company was religious about backups but sinned by leaving them exposed
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 14:22:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/26/company-was-religious-about-backups-but-sinned-by-leaving-them-exposed/5312234

[TAM METİN - 526 kelime]
Company was religious about backups but sinned by leaving them exposed

Never put the backup server in the same Windows domain as production

Sticky note on monitor provided keys to the company’s kingdom
7 days ago

London hotel put admin login for booking website on whiteboard behind the front desk
14 days ago

Tobacco company got smoked by leaving secret recipes on a file share
21 days ago

Admin dropped the F-bomb in a password to show hackers who was really 1337
28 days ago

IT department and auditors at major pharmacy chain got into a rip-roaring fight about using 'password' on Cisco firewall
October 22, 2026

Welcome, once again, to PWNED, where we’re thankful every time someone leaves a password sticky note on a monitor or fails to delete a terminated employee’s admin account. But neither of those common occurrences is what we’ll be talking about today. Instead, our subject broke the number-one rule of backups: you gotta keep ’em separated.

Our story comes courtesy of Da Li, who serves as global alliance manager at Info2Soft, a company that provides data protection and recovery software. At one point, a partner called him into a case where a Chinese vehicle parts manufacturer fell victim to a ransomware gang. Unfortunately, its backup was compromised too.

The manufacturer had religiously backed up its manufacturing execution system (MES) and SAP orders, inventory, and finance systems every night. There was even a green dashboard that showed the backup process had succeeded, and someone checked that every morning.

However, one Friday evening, crims broke into the network and found out that the backup server was joined to the same Windows domain as the rest of the network. The hackers found a plaintext password for the backup service account in a maintenance script on a file share and discovered that someone had used the very same password for a domain admin account.

So the first thing the crooks did was delete every restore point on that backup server. Then they started encrypting the production servers to ransom them.

“Every backup job had succeeded for three years,” Li said. “The failure was that the thing meant to save them could be reached with the same keys as the thing being attacked.”

Have a story about someone leaving a gaping hole in their network? Share it with us at pwned@sitpub.com. Anonymity is available upon request.

The results were devastating. With the MES and SAP data both gone, the plant couldn't schedule production, ship parts, or track them.

By the time Li was called in, the plant had already resolved its ransomware situation. We don’t know whether it paid or not. The manufacturer did ask Li and his company to make sure that this couldn’t happen again.

Li’s fix was not radical, but it was instructive. He made sure that the company now keeps its backup system outside the production domain, uses unique credentials, and requires MFA to reduce the risk of unauthorized access even if a password is compromised. He also made sure that the company keeps at least one offline backup and runs a test restore quarterly to ensure it’s prepared for emergencies.®

[24] The Register - Hackers help themselves to 1Password's source code and customer data
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 12:02:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/26/hackers-help-themselves-to-1passwords-source-code-and-customer-data/5312317

[TAM METİN - 679 kelime]
Hackers help themselves to 1Password's source code and customer data

An intruder planted malicious code in backend services, but the biz insists passwords are safe

Windows 11 passkey bug cries failure after successful sign-in
6 days ago

‘123456’ has been the world’s most popular password for 7 out of the last 8 years
10 days ago

London hotel put admin login for booking website on whiteboard behind the front desk
14 days ago

Password manager mSecure tells users to change passwords, won't say why
23 days ago

'Password1' and a forgotten test account land Welsh outfits in hot water
October 20, 2026

1Password is reassuring customers that their vaults remain secure after an attacker breached its systems and stole account information and private source code.

The Canadian security biz disclosed this week that an attacker had compromised an employee's corporate GitHub account, made off with private source code, and deployed malicious code to its backend services to siphon off customer information.

1Password, which began investigating the intrusion on November 19, hasn't said how many customers were affected, but confirmed that the stolen data includes names and email addresses used to sign in to the service, account identifiers, timestamps showing when users created and last accessed their accounts, and various pieces of non-secret account metadata.

The company insists the information that customers actually pay it to protect was beyond the intruder's reach. "At no time were customer vault contents accessed by, or available to, the attacker," 1Password said.

Arguably more troubling is the revelation that the intruder gained access to 1Password's continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) infrastructure, which houses credentials used to keep its products running. The company says it has rotated or revoked potentially exposed credentials and that neither its apps nor its browser extensions were affected.

1Password hasn't explained how access to an employee's GitHub account enabled the attacker to deploy malicious code to its backend services, nor has it detailed the extent of the attacker's access to its software build and distribution infrastructure. The company did not respond to The Register's questions.

The miscreant also got their hands on tokens embedded in emails sent to some customers as part of account invitations, provisioning, and recovery processes. These tokens aren't enough to access an account on their own, according to 1Password, as users must also authenticate via single sign-on or enter a verification code sent to their registered email address.

The company says it specifically notified the small number of users with affected provisioning or recovery processes underway. It found no internal evidence of account takeovers using the exposed provisioning tokens.

Still, the breach has prompted the password management biz to beef up those verification codes, replacing the six-digit numeric variety with ten-character alphanumeric codes to make brute-forcing them considerably more difficult.

1Password has booted the intruder out of its GitHub environment, revoked the stolen token, and rolled back the affected backend services to clean versions of the code. It's since shored up security around its development infrastructure and employees' devices, and brought in outside security specialists to review its handiwork.

The incident recalls the first stage of LastPass's 2022 breach, when an attacker compromised its development environment and stole source code. Information obtained in that intrusion helped enable a second attack that exposed customer vault backups, including encrypted passwords.

The UK Information Commission's Office slapped LastPass with a £1.2 million ($1.6 million) fine last December over the incident, which compromised the personal information of up to 1.6 million UK users. Among the regulator's gripes was that LastPass had fallen short of the higher standard of security expected of a company entrusted with people's passwords.

Customers aren't being asked to change their account passwords or Secret Keys, neither of which 1Password stores, although the company is warning users to be on the lookout for phishing attempts.

A list of email addresses belonging to people known to use a particular password manager is a handy thing for a phisher to have, especially when said password manager has just given them a plausible pretext for getting in touch. ®

[25] The Register - UK Home Office waved through border system without a working fix for bad data
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 10:30:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/26/uk-home-office-waved-through-border-system-without-a-working-fix-for-bad-data/5312162

[TAM METİN - 1003 kelime]
UK Home Office waved through border system without a working fix for bad data

ICO reprimand follows failings that risked wrongful detention, with a temporary correction process taking more than a year to arrive

Free e-government platform that serves two billion people in ten countries has critical SQL injection bug
7 hours ago

UK privacy watchdog faces probe into its workplace culture
16 hours ago

Stale links on UK public sector websites direct visitors to gambling dens
19 hours ago

UK government working 'around the clock' on Capita contingency plans
20 hours ago

India’s government ponders cradle to grave AI oversight
1 day ago

The Information Commission's Office (ICO) has reprimanded the UK Home Office over a border watchlist system launched without an effective way to correct inaccurate personal information.

The regulator said the failings risked people being wrongly detained for additional checks at the UK border, potentially causing distress and other harm.

The platform in question, Helios, was slated to replace its predecessor, Warnings Index, in September 2024 and despite an initial delay due to concerns over data quality, went live the following month.

Both Helios and Warnings Index are watch-listing systems, designed to allow law enforcement officials – mainly but not exclusively Border Force – to check people's data against official records at the UK border.

Helios's federated operating model, whereby the data sources are not centrally managed, yielded quicker results than Warnings Index, but contained a critical flaw in that operational staff could not alter incorrect records.

Platform operators, such as Border Force officers, could not amend the data that feeds into it, neither after the fact nor in real time. This was not the case with Warnings Index, which had a dedicated team responsible for correcting data entries in real time.

Under Helios, staff were supposed to report inaccuracies to the organization responsible for the relevant source data, which would make the correction.

One slight problem: there was no established reporting system at the time Helios was launched on October 2.

The platform went live anyway, as officials deemed the data integrity and security risks presented by Warnings Index to be greater than those of Helios.

Just nine days passed before the Home Office's data protection officer had to submit a breach report after Helios operational staff said they didn't know how to request data corrections.

During the time, Helios operators sent 87 data rectification requests to the Warnings Index correction team, despite it having no capability to make the required changes to the data.

The Home Office later said that many other requests likely weren't logged as the proper processes were not in place, and that staff should have logged the issue through a ServiceNow IT ticket, as any other departmental civil servant with any kind of IT issue would have.

But the Home Office also said that including personal data in those helpdesk tickets would itself constitute a data breach.

The ICO said in its reprimand document [PDF] that it is "unclear" how such a reporting arrangement could realistically work in practice, given that a report would require details from a subject's record that required rectification as well as the new, accurate information that should be used to replace it.

The Home Office's proposed workaround was to get operational staff to verbally read out said personal data over a phone call with the IT helpdesk – a procedure it described as possible "but cumbersome."

It took the Home Office more than a year to implement a temporary data-rectification method, which involved a BX Data Query tile on the IT Now platform, allowing Helios operators to securely report data accuracy issues.

The new method went live on October 25, 2025.

By March 18, 2026, the Home Office said the new process had received more than 6,500 correction requests and around 5,000 records had been removed.

The ICO said it "remains concerned" about the efficacy of this method, given that operators cannot include personal data in the tile report, but the regulator is satisfied the problem is at least in part fixed.

The Home Office also said it was aware of an increase in customer complaints following Helios's launch, and around 800 cases in which people were delayed at the UK border pending additional checks.

It was unable to attribute a specific number of these to Helios's technical issues, however.

"The Home Office further stated that it was not aware of any data subjects suffering distress as a result of using its services at the UK border," the ICO's report stated.

A Home Office spokesperson told us: "We take data protection extremely seriously. That is why we have co-operated fully with the Information Commissioner's Office. "We immediately removed all outdated records to minimize delays to passenger journeys and strengthened mechanisms to speed up updates."Our processes will continue to be kept under review, ensuring we meet data protection obligations while protecting border security."

Joanne Stones, investigations manager at the ICO, said: "People must be able to trust that decisions about their immigration status at the UK border are made with accurate, up to date information." She added: "They also have the right to expect that when information is inaccurate, there is a swift and efficient way for it to be corrected.

"We recognize that federated systems like Helios present unique challenges and welcome the steps the Home Office has taken since this issue was identified. However, the Home Office must ensure that its permanent solution provides a robust and effective way to identify and correct inaccurate information without delay."

The ICO's public sector enforcement policy favors guidance, reprimands, and other interventions, reserving fines for the most egregious cases. Its rationale is to avoid diverting money from public services and effectively punishing those affected twice.

Fines have included £350,000 for a 2021 MoD email that exposed details of Afghan evacuation applicants, and £750,000 for the PSNI's 2023 disclosure of workforce information, including surnames, initials, ranks, and roles. The PSNI penalty was reduced from £5.6 million under the public sector approach. ®

Inception, a new data theft and extortion gang that security researchers have theorized is a rebrand of the ShinyHunters group, “reject[s] this attribution” and claims it has no affiliation with any prior cybercrime enterprise.

In a lengthy statement posted Tuesday and shared on X, the extortionists insist that they are a new group, with new operators, and that their alleged social engineering attacks aren’t copying anyone - but rather just abusing the weakest link in an organization’s security: “a human being holding a phone.”

The rant follows a Monday report by Okta Threat Intelligence - along with social media speculation since Inception’s inception last week - that the newly emerged group is very likely ShinyHunters with a different name.

Okta’s researchers based their analysis on the two groups’ shared infrastructure, plus Inception’s nearly identical tactics: voice phishing, during which the scammers trick targets into entering credentials into phishing pages and approving multi-factor authentication (MFA) requests.

Inception apparently took offense to this. “We would like to address this once, clearly, and only once,” the crims' statement says. “We are not ShinyHunters. We were never ShinyHunters. We are Inception.”

The gang argues that its overlapping domain registrars, hosting providers, and DNS services don’t prove anything. These are commodity services, “available to anyone who can pay for them,” Inception wrote.

Plus, their similar methodologies don't necessarily make Inception a reboot. “That is not theft of tradecraft,” they wrote. “It is acknowledgment of a good idea – the same way every group after LockBit used a leak site, and nobody called them LockBit.”

“Inception is a new group, with new operators, our own objectives, and our own name,” the statement continues. “What we share with the past is plumbing.”

To be fair, the new crime crew has every reason to distance itself from ShinyHunters.

That gang’s September FBI hack - plus several earlier digital heists during which the criminals stole information belonging to millions of people, including cancer patients, university and K-12 students - put a massive target on its collective backs.

And while at least three ShinyHunters suspects were arrested in September and October, there's no confirmation that authorities have dismantled the entire operation.

Plus, as a ShinyHunters spokesperson told The Register in an earlier interview, they and others in the crew started off as GnosticPlayers before rebranding as ShinyHunters in 2020 - so a reboot isn’t beyond the realm of possibility.

Inception, for its part, says it “will not comment further on the ghost of ShinyHunters,” and it has its own work to do. And by work, it means copying ShinyHunters' playbook, stealing corporate data, and extorting victim companies for massive sums of money.®

[27] CSO Online - New GeoServer RCE exploit chain seen in the wild two days after disclosure
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 15:39:58 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4253667/new-geoserver-rce-exploit-chain-seen-in-the-wild-two-days-after-disclosure.html

[TAM METİN - 582 kelime]
Attackers began probing the chain shortly after GeoServer published its advisories, the latest sign that the window between disclosure and an attack is shrinking.

Attackers have begun probing a chain of five GeoServer vulnerabilities that enables unauthenticated remote code execution, just two days after the open source geospatial server project disclosed the flaws.

Security firm watchTowr, which operates a global network of honeypot servers, reported that it observed the probing activity in the early morning hours of Wednesday. Attackers from multiple IP addresses created stored queries on GeoServer instances, checked whether they were created, and deleted them immediately, a technique that confirms a server is vulnerable without delivering a malicious payload.

GeoServer is widely used by government agencies, utilities, and large organizations to publish maps and geospatial data, and it has become a recurring target for attackers. The full RCE chain was reported to the project in August by Jonah Burgess, a senior security researcher at Rapid7 who uses the online handle CryptoCat. The GeoServer project shipped fixes in October and early November but only disclosed the vulnerabilities and published advisories for them on Monday.

According to Burgess’ technical write-up this week, one flaw (CVE-2026-129244) uses an overly long stored-query request to trigger a filesystem error that exposes the server’s data-directory path. A second flaw (CVE-2026-129243) allows attackers to call services with a slash in their names, such as x/WFS, bypassing access rules and letting unprivileged users create and delete stored queries, an action normally reserved for administrators.

Only the final step results in code execution, and only one of the five flaws, the predictable signing key, is rated as critical. Burgess also published a proof-of-concept exploit for the chain.

However, researchers from Eye Security independently found a shorter, three-flaw RCE chain that shares the file-read step but reaches code execution through a different bug tracked as CVE-2026-120704 that was also patched.

Eye Security used AI models to find the bugs, and the fact that different researchers found some of the same vulnerabilities (two researchers named McCaulay and Quikko are also credited on some of the flaws) highlights the fact that attackers could use AI to find the flaws as well, especially since patches can serve as blueprints for AI agents. In fact, Eye Security had to consider whether delaying disclosure after patches were released was worth the risk of malicious actors finding them independently in the meantime.

“We used AI to find these bugs, and a determined attacker with good models could do the same, especially with the official patch to diff against,” Niels Teusink, a security researcher at Eye Security, wrote this week. “That makes the gap between releasing a fix and publishing details riskier than it used to be.”

After the advisories were published, it took less than two days for attackers to start probing the flaws to identify vulnerable servers, likely in preparation for attacks. Over 3,000 GeoServer instances are currently exposed to the internet.

GeoServer keeps getting targeted

This is not the first time GeoServer has been targeted by attackers. In August, a zero-day SQL injection flaw was exploited before a patch was available, and in December 2025 the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) ordered US agencies to patch another GeoServer flaw under active exploitation.

Where patching cannot happen immediately, GeoServer advises blocking requests whose service value contains a slash, removing the remember-me filter from the web filter chain, and checking the data directory’s wfs/query folders for stored queries not created by a legitimate user.

[28] CSO Online - Palo Alto confirms Fragnesia exploitation against firewalls in PHP2Shell attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 13:05:35 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4253600/palo-alto-confirms-fragnesia-exploitation-against-firewalls-in-php2shell-attacks.html

[TAM METİN - 864 kelime]
The kernel fix reaches PAN-OS six months after Linux patched it, so customers who rushed out PHP2Shell hotfixes in October now need another round.

Palo Alto Networks has confirmed that attackers exploited Fragnesia, a Linux kernel flaw, to gain root on PAN-OS firewalls through the PHP2Shell vulnerabilities, and has shipped fixes six months after the bug was patched in Linux.

The vulnerability allows a locally authenticated, low-privileged attacker to escalate privileges to root.

“Exploitation of CVE-2026-46300 has been observed targeting the Palo Alto Networks management interface exposed to the internet,” the company said in its November 25 security advisory. It added that all observed attacks targeted devices whose management interface “is exposed to the internet, a configuration our best practices advise against.”

Even so, the company labeled the bulletin “Informational” and gave it no CVSS score. Fixed builds span every supported PAN-OS branch from 10.2 to 12.2.

A second patch cycle in a month

For PAN-OS customers, these are the second set of hotfixes in a month. Between October 25 and October 29, Palo Alto released fixes for PHP2Shell, according to the timeline in its PHP2Shell advisory. PHP2Shell is the collective name for CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619, two critical flaws in the PHP runtime behind the PAN-OS management web interface.

The company rated both flaws 9.2 on CVSS 4.0, and the advisory, which covers PA-Series and VM-Series firewalls and Panorama, said, “active exploitation has been observed.”

The October builds do not include the kernel fix, which arrives in later releases listed in the new bulletin. Even for customers who have patched PHP2Shell, Palo Alto said in the bulletin that it “still recommend[s] upgrading to a fixed release at your next maintenance interval.”

Palo Alto is relaxed about the timing. “If you are running a fixed PAN-OS listed in the CVE-2026-109618 advisory, you are protected,” the bulletin said. “The only known way to exploit this vulnerability requires an attacker to first exploit the PHP2Shell vulnerability,” it added. For devices with management access restricted to trusted internal IP addresses, it advises upgrading “at your next maintenance interval.”

Duncan Brown, group vice president and global domain lead for worldwide security and trust at IDC, said that the approach is defensible when vulnerabilities are chained.

“It’s acceptable to apply one patch that effectively breaks the chain. Ideally, all vulnerabilities would be patched as early as possible, but in many cases this is not practical,” Brown said.

Rahul Karne, a senior digital forensics and incident response analyst, called patching PHP2Shell a reasonable short-term step, but said it blocks the known way in without fixing the bug.

“The key word is ‘known,’” Karne said. He pointed out that public exploit code for Fragnesia, including a Metasploit module, is available, and that any other code execution flaw on the device would make the kernel bug usable again.

From web shell to root

The chain has been public for almost a month. In late October, Dream Security researchers reported that attackers combined PHP2Shell with Fragnesia to compromise firewalls running vulnerable PAN-OS builds.

According to Dream, the attackers used PHP2Shell to run code as the web service account and then used the kernel flaw to become root. From there, they tunneled into internal networks and stole credentials, private keys, and network topology data.

The kernel bug itself is not new. William Bowling, a researcher at Zellic, disclosed Fragnesia on May 13 along with a proof-of-concept exploit and an upstream kernel patch, after finding it with the help of the company’s V12 AI auditing agent. Fedora shipped a fixed kernel the next day.

Karne said some delay is normal for appliance vendors that maintain custom kernels across many hardware models, but 195 days is too long when exploit code is public. Customers should hear whether they are affected within a week of such a disclosure and get a fix within about 30 days, he said.

“A firewall should not get security fixes more slowly than the servers it protects,” Karne said.

The firewall as a foothold

Palo Alto is not the only security vendor whose appliances were rooted this way. In November, Check Point said “a couple of dozen” customers were hit after attackers chained PHP2Shell with a Gaia OS privilege escalation flaw to take over gateways and deploy a wiper.

Niyati Daftary, principal analyst at Gartner, said CISOs should treat the incident as a weakness in their trust model for security appliances, not merely a patching issue.

“A firewall running Linux, PHP and other third-party components must be assessed with the same rigor as any privileged, internet-facing server,” Daftary said. “Vendor labels such as ‘Informational’ should never override enterprise risk analysis when flaws can be chained to achieve root access.”

“If a device is exposed during the attack window, merely applying the hotfix does not establish trust,” she added. Teams should review administrative access, rotate credentials and certificates, hunt for persistence, and reimage the appliance from a known-good build when root compromise cannot be ruled out, she said. Asked why the kernel fix took six months to reach PAN-OS, and whether Panorama is affected, Palo Alto said it had “no further information to share beyond what is outlined in the advisory at this time.”

[29] SecurityWeek - Hex Package Manager Warns of Potential Data Breach
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 07:44:49 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/hex-package-manager-warns-of-potential-data-breach/

[TAM METİN - 3945 kelime]
Hex Package Manager Warns of Potential Data Breach

A vulnerability in hex.pm allowed hackers to run commands on web servers. No evidence of data modification was found, but database reads are not logged.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Hex, the package manager for Elixir and Erlang, fell victim to a cyberattack involving unauthorized access to its web servers.
The incident occurred on November 23, when the attackers exploited a vulnerability in hex.pm to execute commands. The attack was immediately blocked, and the underlying security defect was addressed within 20 minutes.



Between 18:16 and 18:29 UTC, the attacker sent 69 malicious requests, affecting six web servers. At 18:30, Hex replaced all hex.pm servers with patched versions rejecting the payload, and its firewall rule fully blocked the attacker’s traffic at 18:33.



“We found no evidence of unauthorized package modifications, database changes, or that the attacker maintained access to our systems,” the package manager explains.



According to Hex, potential access to database contents, such as email addresses, password hashes, API key hashes, sessions, second factor authentication secrets, and audit logs, cannot be ruled out.



While all the observed reads of private packages came from Hex servers, any read from a compromised server would appear legitimate, it says. Additionally, database and private documentation reads are not logged, hindering visibility into potential data exposure.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“The attacker’s commands could read the credentials our servers use, so we replaced them: the storage, CDN, email and database credentials, and the keys that sign sessions and access tokens. This logged everyone out of hex.pm and Hex clients,” Hex notes.



The package manager has been working on a new registry signing system with key rotation support. Until new Hex client iterations are rolled out, users should avoid fetching packages from third-party mirrors and rely only on repo.hex.pm.



Hex also recommends that all account holders change their hex.pm passwords and reset their two-factor authentication.



“Because email addresses may have been exposed, be careful with emails that claim to come from Hex. Please double-check that the links they contain lead to hex.pm,” the package manager says.



The attackers exploited an unauthenticated deserialization of untrusted data bug (tracked as CVE-2026-129514, CVSS score of 10) that allowed them to execute OS commands via a crafted Erlang external term format request body and gain remote code execution (RCE).



Hex discovered that the Ash Framework was also impacted by the same class of vulnerability. Ash patched the flaw (tracked as CVE-2026-124283) on November 24, warning it could lead to code execution or SQL injection.



Associated security defects were also patched in two other Elixir libraries: namely db_connection (CVE-2026-133555) and flop (CVE-2026-130411).

[30] SecurityWeek - SAP Confirms Cyberattack, Source Code Reportedly Compromised
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 07:36:14 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/sap-confirms-cyberattack-source-code-reportedly-compromised/

[TAM METİN - 2933 kelime]
SAP Confirms Cyberattack, Source Code Reportedly Compromised

SAP has detected suspicious activity on GitHub, where hackers reportedly used stolen employee tokens to copy and alter source code.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Enterprise software giant SAP has confirmed it is investigating a cybersecurity incident following reports that threat actors targeted its development infrastructure.
German publication Der Spiegel [paywalled] broke the news on Thursday, citing SAP insiders and an internal notification sent to employees.  



SAP told SecurityWeek that it had detected suspicious activity and implemented measures to protect customers and secure its services. The German company did not disclose details of the attack.



“Based on our investigation to date, we have found no evidence of unauthorized access to customer business data,” SAP told SecurityWeek.



German news agency DPA reviewed internal SAP communications that revealed the suspicious activity had been identified within GitHub. 



According to an insider cited by Der Spiegel, the hackers obtained SAP employees’ authentication tokens, enabling them to copy and alter source code stored on GitHub. Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


SAP publishes developer tools, sample applications, and open source projects on GitHub. It’s unclear whether the threat actor accessed any proprietary code.



Separately, Handelsblatt reported that SAP’s emergency security measures have affected numerous teams. The software giant has reportedly replaced signing keys, limited access to GitHub, and suspended automated development operations.



None of the reports indicate that tampered code was incorporated into software delivered to SAP customers.



Sources cited by Der Spiegel suggested that the attack may have been assisted by AI. While AI involvement has not been confirmed, several major organizations, including the European Commission, have recently disclosed AI-assisted cyberattacks.



In late October, the Gentlemen ransomware group listed SAP on its leak website, pointing to the company’s Latin American operations. File listings reviewed by SecurityWeek suggest the hackers obtained SAP-related software and source code, but it is unclear whether SAP itself or a third party was compromised. The two incidents do not appear to be connected.



Related: FIFA Says It Has Been Victim of a Cyberattack 



Related: 1Password Breach Exposes Customer Data, Internal Source Code
Related: Critical Juniper Session Smart Conductor Flaw Exploited in Attacks

[31] SecurityWeek - FIFA Says It Has Been Victim of a Cyberattack Accessing ‘Personal and Business Information’
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 17:06:55 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/fifa-says-it-has-been-victim-of-a-cyberattack-accessing-personal-and-business-information/

[TAM METİN - 1771 kelime]
FIFA Says It Has Been Victim of a Cyberattack Accessing ‘Personal and Business Information’

FIFA didn’t say when the cyberattack took place, or the precise nature of the hacked information.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

FIFA was the victim of a cyberattack that saw hackers gain access to what soccer’s world governing body describes as a “limited set of personal and business information,” it said Thursday.
“FIFA is working closely with the relevant authorities, including law enforcement,” the organization said in a statement sent to The Associated Press.



FIFA didn’t say when the cyberattack took place, or the precise nature of the hacked information.



Last week, a group calling itself the Handsome Boy Hacking Club posted a screenshot on X of what it claimed were FIFA files. Three days later, it appeared to take credit for the hack when responding to a post that said more than 12 gigabytes of internal documents had been leaked.



“FIFA condemns in the strongest possible terms the unlawful acquisition and publication of information, particularly personal data,” the governing body said in its statement. “Such actions constitute a serious and unacceptable violation of privacy and can have significant consequences for the individuals concerned.”



The breach comes at a time when FIFA and its president, Gianni Infantino, is under scrutiny after his foiled, secretive plan to sell stakes in the World Cup to private investors.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Infantino is also trying to extend his 11-year presidency of FIFA to 2031. He is the favorite when the election is held on March 18, with Kohzo Tashima of Japan and Philippe Diallo of France the other candidates.

Related: US Treasury’s FinCEN Disconnects Systems Following Cyber Incident
