[32] SecurityWeek - Critical Juniper Session Smart Conductor Flaw Exploited in Attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 13:04:06 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/critical-juniper-session-smart-conductor-flaw-exploited-in-attacks/

[TAM METİN - 2558 kelime]
Critical Juniper Session Smart Conductor Flaw Exploited in Attacks

Tracked as CVE-2026-103406, the vulnerability allows remote attackers to execute arbitrary commands as root.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Juniper Networks and the US cybersecurity agency CISA this week warned that threat actors have been exploiting a critical vulnerability in Session Smart Conductor.
Tracked as CVE-2026-103406 (CVSS score of 9.8), the security defect is described as a remotely exploitable OS command injection bug.



It affects the Session Smart Conductor’s AutomatedProvisioner service and could allow attackers to execute arbitrary commands with root privileges via a specially crafted Salt minion ID.



A centralized management and policy solution, Session Smart Conductor is used for the orchestration and management of Session Smart Routers across networks. The Salt minion ID is a router’s unique ID when registering with the Salt master (Session Smart Conductor).



CVE-2026-103406 was patched on October 28 along with 17 other flaws in Session Smart Router and Session Smart Conductor. On Tuesday, Juniper updated its advisory to flag the security defect as exploited.



“At least one instance of malicious exploitation of CVE-2026-103406 has been reported to the Juniper SIRT. Customers are encouraged to upgrade to a fixed release as soon as possible and in the meantime take steps to mitigate this vulnerability,” reads Juniper’s update.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


On Wednesday, CISA added the bug to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog, urging federal agencies to patch it within three days, as mandated by BOD 26-04.



Although BOD 26-04 only applies to federal civilian executive branch agencies, all organizations are advised to review CISA’s KEV list and address the relevant security weaknesses as soon as possible.



Juniper patched CVE-2026-103406 in Session Smart Router and Session Smart Conductor versions 7.2.3, 7.1.7, and 7.0.5.



CISA also added five Mitel MiCollab flaws, a Vates Xen Orchestra issue, and three Danfoss AK-SM 800A series vulnerabilities to the KEV list.



Related: Drupal Installer Patch Left a Gap Attackers Are Already Exploiting
Related: Two Newly Disclosed GeoServer Vulnerabilities Under Attack
Related: Hackers Exploit Unpatched Flaw in Defunct Idunno AntiCsrf Library
Related: Palo Alto Networks Patches Fragnesia Flaw Used to Root Firewalls

Tracked as CVE-2026-103406 (CVSS score of 9.8), the security defect is described as a remotely exploitable OS command injection bug.



It affects the Session Smart Conductor’s AutomatedProvisioner service and could allow attackers to execute arbitrary commands with root privileges via a specially crafted Salt minion ID.



A centralized management and policy solution, Session Smart Conductor is used for the orchestration and management of Session Smart Routers across networks. The Salt minion ID is a router’s unique ID when registering with the Salt master (Session Smart Conductor).



CVE-2026-103406 was patched on October 28 along with 17 other flaws in Session Smart Router and Session Smart Conductor. On Tuesday, Juniper updated its advisory to flag the security defect as exploited.



“At least one instance of malicious exploitation of CVE-2026-103406 has been reported to the Juniper SIRT. Customers are encouraged to upgrade to a fixed release as soon as possible and in the meantime take steps to mitigate this vulnerability,” reads Juniper’s update.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


On Wednesday, CISA added the bug to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog, urging federal agencies to patch it within three days, as mandated by BOD 26-04.



Although BOD 26-04 only applies to federal civilian executive branch agencies, all organizations are advised to review CISA’s KEV list and address the relevant security weaknesses as soon as possible.



Juniper patched CVE-2026-103406 in Session Smart Router and Session Smart Conductor versions 7.2.3, 7.1.7, and 7.0.5.



CISA also added five Mitel MiCollab flaws, a Vates Xen Orchestra issue, and three Danfoss AK-SM 800A series vulnerabilities to the KEV list.

Related: Palo Alto Networks Patches Fragnesia Flaw Used to Root Firewalls

================================================================================

[33] SecurityWeek - Mitel MiCollab Zero-Days Exploited in Suspected AI-Assisted Attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 11:35:25 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/mitel-micollab-zero-days-exploited-in-suspected-ai-assisted-attacks/

[TAM METİN - 5435 kelime]
Mitel MiCollab Zero-Days Exploited in Suspected AI-Assisted Attacks

Accenture says hackers exploited MiCollab flaws before Mitel’s advisory, likely aided by AI, in a suspected extortion campaign.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Threat actors exploited several Mitel MiCollab vulnerabilities as zero-days in a data theft campaign, according to Accenture’s Apex Labs. CISA on Wednesday added five MiCollab flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog. 
In one attack observed by Accenture, hackers gained privileged access to a MiCollab server and quickly reached the targeted organization’s identity infrastructure, ultimately stealing sensitive business information.



The MiCollab business collaboration platform enables employees to communicate through calls, messages, and virtual meetings. Accenture noted that the product’s frequent internet exposure and integration with Active Directory make it particularly attractive to hackers.



Mitel on November 16 announced patches for five vulnerabilities affecting the product’s audio, web and video conferencing component, including arbitrary file upload, XML injection, unauthorized access, command injection, and authentication bypass. They can all be exploited by unauthenticated attackers, and two critical bugs allow arbitrary command execution. 



The security holes are tracked as CVE-2026-133275, CVE-2026-133303, CVE-2026-133311, CVE-2026-133323, and CVE-2026-133324.



Accenture found evidence that the vulnerabilities were exploited in the wild before Mitel published its advisory. The company determined that threat actors deployed a web shell on a victim’s systems on November 15.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


According to Accenture, the attackers executed commands on a MiCollab server just 12 minutes after the first request, and gained root privileges within half an hour.



The hackers harvested additional credentials and, roughly 20 hours after their first observed request, gained access to internal backup and database servers.



The investigation uncovered evidence that some of the malicious activity may have been driven by AI tools, with the attacker’s infrastructure communicating with two unnamed commercial AI API services. 



“Because the telemetry is sampled, Apex Labs cannot determine precisely how the threat actor used the services. However, the observed activity is consistent with potential uses such as data triage, operational support for exploitation or web-shell activity, and preparation of extortion material,” Accenture noted.



It added that communications between the attackers’ infrastructure and the AI services resumed — after an eight-hour silence — at precisely the same second that the hackers sent an authentication bypass request to the targeted MiCollab server.



The company also found evidence of compromise at a second victim, with a MiCollab server communicating with the same attacker’s infrastructure since November 9. However, Accenture could not establish how the second server was hacked, as it had been affected by older vulnerabilities as well.



The researchers have not linked the attacks to a known cybercrime group, but believe they may have been part of an extortion campaign. 



Mitel has released patches, but its advisory — last updated on November 24 — does not mention in-the-wild exploitation of the vulnerabilities.



Accenture warned organizations that attackers may have compromised their MiCollab servers before the fixes were installed and urged them to check for indicators of compromise (IoCs).



CISA added all five vulnerabilities to its KEV catalog, instructing federal agencies to address four of them by November 28; the last one has a December 9 deadline. 



Threat actors regularly target Mitel products. CISA’s KEV catalog currently includes a dozen such vulnerabilities. 



Related: Drupal Installer Patch Left a Gap Attackers Are Already Exploiting
Related: Two Newly Disclosed GeoServer Vulnerabilities Under Attack
Related: VMware Updates Patch Critical ESX, vCenter Vulnerabilities

The investigation uncovered evidence that some of the malicious activity may have been driven by AI tools, with the attacker’s infrastructure communicating with two unnamed commercial AI API services. 



“Because the telemetry is sampled, Apex Labs cannot determine precisely how the threat actor used the services. However, the observed activity is consistent with potential uses such as data triage, operational support for exploitation or web-shell activity, and preparation of extortion material,” Accenture noted.



It added that communications between the attackers’ infrastructure and the AI services resumed — after an eight-hour silence — at precisely the same second that the hackers sent an authentication bypass request to the targeted MiCollab server.



The company also found evidence of compromise at a second victim, with a MiCollab server communicating with the same attacker’s infrastructure since November 9. However, Accenture could not establish how the second server was hacked, as it had been affected by older vulnerabilities as well.



The researchers have not linked the attacks to a known cybercrime group, but believe they may have been part of an extortion campaign. 



Mitel has released patches, but its advisory — last updated on November 24 — does not mention in-the-wild exploitation of the vulnerabilities.



Accenture warned organizations that attackers may have compromised their MiCollab servers before the fixes were installed and urged them to check for indicators of compromise (IoCs).



CISA added all five vulnerabilities to its KEV catalog, instructing federal agencies to address four of them by November 28; the last one has a December 9 deadline. 



Threat actors regularly target Mitel products. CISA’s KEV catalog currently includes a dozen such vulnerabilities. 



Related: Drupal Installer Patch Left a Gap Attackers Are Already Exploiting
Related: Two Newly Disclosed GeoServer Vulnerabilities Under Attack
Related: VMware Updates Patch Critical ESX, vCenter Vulnerabilities

[34] SecurityWeek - Drupal Installer Patch Left a Gap Attackers Are Already Exploiting
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 09:12:26 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/drupal-installer-patch-left-a-gap-attackers-are-already-exploiting/

[TAM METİN - 2561 kelime]
Drupal Installer Patch Left a Gap Attackers Are Already Exploiting

Drupal warns that its previous installer patch left some websites vulnerable to takeover, with attackers already exploiting the gap.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Drupal says last week’s patch for an exploited installer vulnerability did not protect every website, and attackers are now leveraging the gap to hack vulnerable sites.
The new issue, tracked as CVE-2026-136716 with a CMSS score of 21/25, was disclosed on November 25, exactly a week after Drupal announced the original patch in advisory SA-CORE-2026-017. Drupal describes the new flaw as an access bypass issue that can lead to site takeover.



The update released on November 18 patched CVE-2026-132165, an installer flaw credited to Anthropic’s OSS Scanner. The affected installer ships with Drupal core and enables users to set up a new site.



Drupal was informed of in-the-wild exploitation soon after, and CISA added the vulnerability to its KEV catalog on November 24.



The advisory for CVE-2026-136716 explains where the earlier fix fell short.



“SA-CORE-2026-017 recently fixed several vulnerabilities within the Drupal core installer. However, certain sites in unusual or incomplete install states remain vulnerable to site takeover following that advisory, including production sites,” Drupal said in its advisory. Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


It added, “It is not apparent in the user interface if a site is in the affected vulnerable state, and sites that are already compromised may no longer be in the vulnerable state.”



Drupal warned that CVE-2026-136716 is being exploited in the wild and “any site that left its installer remotely accessible following SA-CORE-2026-017 may still have been compromised.”



Websites where the installer had already been secured are not vulnerable to attacks. Patches are included in versions 11.4.14, 11.3.23 and 10.6.23. Affected production sites must run an update hook after upgrading to fully address the vulnerability.



Drupal has not shared any information on the attacks, and it’s unclear who is responsible for the exploitation. Users have been advised to check logs for signs of compromise.



Related: Two Newly Disclosed GeoServer Vulnerabilities Under Attack
Related: Hackers Exploit Unpatched Flaw in Defunct Idunno AntiCsrf Library
Related: Palo Alto Networks Patches Fragnesia Flaw Used to Root Firewalls

[35] SecurityWeek - US Treasury’s FinCEN Disconnects Systems Following Cyber Incident
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 13:39:21 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/us-treasurys-fincen-disconnects-systems-following-cyber-incident/

[TAM METİN - 2358 kelime]
US Treasury’s FinCEN Disconnects Systems Following Cyber Incident

FinCEN has extended deadlines for filers affected by the disruptions while it is investigating the incident.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

The Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN), the US Treasury Department’s financial intelligence unit, has disconnected some of its systems while investigating a cyber incident.
“FinCEN is investigating a cybersecurity incident that resulted in unauthorized access to a limited subset of its systems. As a proactive measure, FinCEN has taken some systems offline,” the agency said in a notice on the Bank Secrecy Act (BSA) E-Filing System website.



The website is operational, but some FinCEN services may be temporarily unavailable, and the agency has extended the deadlines for qualifying filers affected by the disruption.



“FinCEN is granting temporary, limited exceptive relief in response to a service disruption that is hindering filers’ ability to adhere to reporting obligations,” the agency said in a November 24 order (PDF).



The agency has not shared further details on the nature of the incident and made no mention of a potential data breach, but said it was working to return all systems to normal.



It is unclear who was behind the attack and if any data was accessed or stolen. SecurityWeek has emailed FinCEN for additional information and will update this article if the agency responds.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


FinCEN disclosed the incident amid a wave of cyberattacks and outages impacting US government agencies, including the CIA, which had an unclassified system breached.



The Department of Defense’s National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence were also affected by the open source intelligence system breach, The New York Times reported on Tuesday.



Last week, the National Security Agency temporarily took offline an internal network used to communicate classified information, and the FBI is looking into an apparent hack of a national law enforcement database.

[36] Help Net Security - In 2027, “the agent did it” stops being an excuse
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 06:30:14 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/27/sailpoint-2027-ai-identity-security-predictions-report/

[TAM METİN - 657 kelime]
In 2027, “the agent did it” stops being an excuse

Enterprises will come under growing pressure to establish accountability for autonomous AI agents in 2027, with regulators, corporate boards and insurers demanding evidence of how organizations govern automated decisions, manage access privileges and prevent operational disruption, according to SailPoint’s 2027 Identity Security Predictions.

The company’s Horizons of Identity Security 2026–2027 research found that 79% of enterprises have deployed AI agents in production, while just 2% have implemented identity security controls designed for them, creating what SailPoint describes as a 40:1 agent security gap.

Autonomous agents could trigger billion-dollar operational failures

SailPoint predicts that a major public company will experience a multibillion-dollar supply chain disruption caused by misconfigured autonomous agents with excessive access permissions.

The company describes a potential incident called an “Authorized Outage,” in which misconfigured agents delete inventory databases, terminate supplier contracts and redirect shipments. The speed of automated execution could overwhelm intervention procedures, resulting in financial losses, contractual disputes and regulatory investigations.

Such an incident could accelerate the adoption of separation-of-duties requirements for AI systems. SailPoint expects enterprises to establish independent governance environments that prevent agents from modifying their permissions, disabling security monitoring or authorizing privilege escalation.

“While it will be possible to delegate a complex workflow to an autonomous agent, it will never be acceptable to delegate accountability, because agent accountability is enterprise responsibility,” said Mark McClain, CEO of SailPoint.

Legal standards and executive accountability

The company anticipates the emergence of a mandatory US governance standard for autonomous agents, involving the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), the Federal Trade Commission (FTC) and state attorneys general.

The predicted framework would require businesses to demonstrate that automated activities comply with legal obligations, fiduciary responsibilities and operating procedures.

Identity governance records could play a role in helping enterprises establish accountability and demonstrate regulatory compliance. Organizations would need to document the relationships between agents, their human owners, assigned permissions and the actions performed throughout their operational lifecycles.

Supply chain attacks and machine credentials

Connections between autonomous agents, third-party applications and business partners are expected to expand the attack surface. Protocols such as the Model Context Protocol (MCP) introduce dependencies that could expose downstream organizations through compromised credentials and connectors.

Major enterprises will begin restricting external agents’ access to their APIs unless vendors and business partners can provide cryptographic proof of the agents’ identities, ownership and authorized access scope.

Credential theft is another area of concern. The company expects passkeys to account for more than 45% of human logins in 2027, prompting cybercriminals to increasingly target API keys, OAuth tokens and machine secrets.

Its research found that 67% of enterprises have secured human access, and 24% govern agentic security. Agents represent 22% of non-human accounts, with machine identities outnumbering human identities by 45 to one.

Zero trust moves toward continuous intent verification

Autonomous systems can generate subtasks and modify workflows during execution, creating challenges for role-based access controls built around predefined responsibilities.

SailPoint expects zero trust architectures to incorporate continuous intent assurance, requiring verification of an agent’s identity, associated human owner and business purpose before each API call.

Organizations are expected to adopt just-in-time access mechanisms, granting permissions when required for a task and removing them upon completion. These measures could reduce exposure associated with persistent administrative privileges.

Identity governance becomes a business performance metric

Upcoming post-quantum cryptography requirements and EU AI Act obligations are expected to increase board-level scrutiny of identity management.

Inventories of human, machine and cryptographic identities will influence corporate valuations, credit ratings and eligibility for cyber insurance. Visibility into credentials and their ownership will support compliance assessments and technology migrations.

Investment policies are also expected to undergo changes. CIOs and CFOs could begin requiring measurable returns and documented security controls before approving funding for agent deployments.

The transition could bring thousands of unsanctioned AI agents operating through employee credentials into centralized management, establishing oversight of their permissions, activities and contributions to business operations.

[37] Help Net Security - Product showcase: Norton helps Claude users uncover the telltale signs of fraud
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 06:00:59 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/27/product-showcase-norton-claude-scam-detection/

[TAM METİN - 613 kelime]
Product showcase: Norton helps Claude users uncover the telltale signs of fraud

The Norton connector for Claude brings the company’s Genie AI-powered scam detection capabilities into the chatbot. Users can submit suspicious emails, text messages, screenshots, and links for analysis, as well as ask questions about cybersecurity, privacy, and identity protection.

The integration does not require a Norton account or a separate application.

Setting up the connector

I tested the Norton connector using Claude on an iPhone.

The integration provides three tools. The first checks links, messages, emails, and phone numbers for scams. The second provides cybersecurity threat guidance, incident response recommendations, and safety education, while the third offers Norton product support, troubleshooting, and setup guidance.

Users can manage permissions for each tool individually.

Testing scam detection

To test the integration, I used several examples, including a fraudulent text message impersonating a government agency, a legitimate promotional email, and a suspicious cloud storage subscription renewal notice.

The first was a Croatian-language text message claiming to come from Croatia’s Ministry of the Interior. It informed the recipient of an unpaid speeding fine, referred to legal provisions, and threatened further consequences.

I shared a screenshot of the message with Claude directly from the iPhone’s Photos app and requested a scam assessment using the Norton connector.

Norton classified the message as an extortion scam with high confidence. The result appeared in a dedicated card within the conversation, displaying the scam classification, confidence level, diagnosis, detailed explanation, and recommendations.

The analysis identified government impersonation, official-sounding language, references to legislation, and escalating urgency as indicators of fraud. It explained how scammers use these tactics to pressure recipients into making payments without verifying the claims.

Norton advised against clicking links, replying to the message, or making a payment. It also recommended checking for outstanding traffic fines through the official website of the Croatian Ministry of the Interior.

Analyzing a legitimate email

For the second test, I submitted a screenshot of a Black Friday promotional email offering a 40% discount on new subscriptions.

Norton classified the email as safe with high confidence, describing it as a legitimate promotional offer for a password manager subscription. The analysis identified the use of standard marketing language and the absence of suspicious urgency or requests for sensitive information.

Despite the positive verdict, Norton recommended verifying the promotion by visiting the company’s official website instead of clicking buttons embedded in the email.

The results included suggested follow-up questions, allowing users to continue the conversation and learn more about phishing, government impersonation scams, legitimate promotional emails, and password managers.

Identifying a cloud storage phishing attempt

In another test, I submitted a suspicious cloud storage subscription renewal notice that warned of a payment failure and potential service interruption.

Norton classified the message as phishing, identifying generic branding and urgency tactics as warning signs.

The analysis identified several characteristics commonly associated with phishing attempts, including the absence of a specific cloud storage provider’s name, pressure to resolve a payment issue, and an official-looking subscription ID that could not be verified.

Unlike the earlier high-confidence classifications, the explanation acknowledged that the message could not be definitively confirmed as malicious based on the available information.

Norton recommended avoiding links in the message and instead accessing the cloud storage provider’s official application or website directly to verify account status, payment methods, and subscription details.

If no corresponding subscription could be found, the tool recommended deleting the message and treating it as a phishing attempt.

The Norton connector integrates scam detection and cybersecurity guidance into Claude’s conversational interface. Its structured responses identify suspected scams, explain the characteristics behind each classification, and recommend next steps, helping users recognize similar patterns in future communications.

[38] Help Net Security - US seizes nearly $17 billion in global war on scam rings
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 05:30:28 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/27/doj-scam-crackdown-800-scammers/

[TAM METİN - 668 kelime]
US seizes nearly $17 billion in global war on scam rings

The US government has gone after more than 800 alleged scammers in what it calls its largest-ever crackdown on scams targeting Americans.

The Department of Justice announced that the effort covers more than 400 cases in 85 federal districts, including new charges, guilty verdicts and sentences, and 30 defendants were extradited to the United States.

American prosecutors and agents joined police in more than 10 countries to raid scam call centers and compounds abroad. The push began after Executive Order 14390 was signed on March 6, 2026.

“Predatory scams against the American people will not be tolerated,” Attorney General Todd Blanche declared.

In one case, gang members in Georgia prisons allegedly used smuggled phones to call strangers, tell them they had missed jury duty and demand a “fine” to keep them out of jail.

Fourteen people tied to the scheme were charged in three indictments in federal and state courts. Prosecutors charged 17 more in state court with smuggling the phones into the state’s prisons. Investigators seized websites used to run the scam, and the US Postal Inspection Service took over three domains and sent their visitors to a seizure banner.

The Federal Communications Commission went after the devices themselves. On November 19, it approved a rule that allows prisons to use jamming technology against contraband phones.

“For years, hundreds of thousands of contraband cellphones have entered our prisons allowing offenders to perpetrate scams on ordinary Americans,” said FCC Chairman Brendan Carr.

“By allowing jamming in prisons for the first time, we’re taking an aggressive, but responsible approach to eliminate inmates access to contraband phones,” Carr added.

Elderly Americans’ data sold to scammers

Several cases involved people who trade in the personal information of older Americans. A list broker in Mexico knowingly sold the names and contact details of elderly Americans to dozens of scammers.

In a separate sweepstakes scheme aimed at elderly victims, a dozen defendants were charged, including Jamaican nationals. Other targets included a Peruvian call center that told Spanish-speaking Americans they would be arrested or deported unless they paid for English language-learning products.

A Nigerian national was extradited from the UK for allegedly trying to steal the military pay of US service members.

Scam compounds shut down abroad

Prosecutors made the overseas operations a priority. “We will not allow these scammers to hide behind their screens or escape justice by committing these crimes abroad,” vowed A. Tysen Duva, Assistant Attorney General for the Criminal Division.

Under Operation Blackout, the FBI seized nearly $17 billion in illicit funds, dismantled more than 29 scam compounds and helped bring home over 2,200 trafficked workers. The bureau warned more than 11,000 Americans that they were being targeted and estimates it prevented over $690 million in losses.

“The message to these scam syndicates is clear: if you target Americans, justice will find you,” FBI Director Kash Patel warned.

The State Department helped freeze over $1 billion in stolen cryptocurrency and take down more than 1.4 million fraudulent websites and accounts. The Federal Trade Commission returned almost $3 billion to consumers.

David Woodcock, who heads the SEC’s Division of Enforcement, argued that the schemes have changed less than the tools behind them. “The technology may be new, but the frauds are familiar.”

About 10% of US adults experienced a scam in the past year, either themselves or through someone in their household, Gallup found in research the DOJ cited. Nearly three in four victims said it hurt their mental health or well-being, and Americans lost an estimated $68 billion to scams in 2025.

“Too many Americans have suffered from cyber fraud, particularly from scam operations emanating from Southeast Asia, and expanding across South Asia, the Middle East, Africa, and the Pacific. But the true cost is not in dollars alone. Behind every statistic is a human story – a family bankrupted, a retiree left destitute, or a young person driven to suicide,” noted Deputy Assistant Secretary of State David Bedard.

[39] Help Net Security - AI raises new questions about what penetration testing proves
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 05:00:32 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/27/crest-ai-threat-led-penetration-testing/

[TAM METİN - 685 kelime]
AI raises new questions about what penetration testing proves

AI is becoming an established part of threat-led penetration testing (TLPT), with 81% of surveyed providers using the technology beyond experimentation, according to research from CREST.

The study, AI and the Future of Threat-Led Penetration Testing: Capability, Control and Credible Assurance, draws on responses from 26 providers and insights from two industry roundtables involving cybersecurity professionals and institutional stakeholders, with a focus on financial services.

AI expands threat emulation capabilities

Over the next 12 months, 65% of respondents expect AI to support 40% or more of their TLPT tasks.

Reporting and assessment is the most common area of live AI use, followed by reconnaissance and threat-informed preparation. The technology can influence engagement planning, intelligence prioritization and the selection of attack scenarios.

Of the providers using AI for threat actor emulation, 86% report improved accuracy and realism. Reported benefits include faster intelligence analysis, more effective mapping of adversary TTPs, and support for the development of tools and adaptive attack paths.

Biases in AI models and training data could introduce generic patterns into threat assessments, making it harder to distinguish between adversaries. Practitioners must evaluate whether generated techniques are relevant to the organization, consistent with the threat actor’s behavior and aligned with the engagement’s objectives.

AI-enabled impersonation raises ethical concerns. Voice cloning, synthetic video and personalized content can be used to test verification procedures and escalation controls. Their use requires justification, authorization and safeguards proportionate to the potential intrusion or harm to individuals targeted during exercises.

Autonomous testing remains limited

None of the 11 regulated TLPT providers that answered questions about execution authority permit AI to perform testing activities autonomously. One commercial provider reported allowing autonomous execution.

Scope and operational control, along with client data and confidentiality, were each ranked among the top three delivery risks by 68% of respondents.

Uses selected as most clearly requiring explicit client approval (Source: CREST)

Network restrictions, access permissions, monitoring and stop conditions can help contain unexpected system behavior. Providers need to maintain records of automated actions, practitioner interventions and decisions affecting the engagement.

Human approval procedures can introduce delays into simulated attacks, potentially affecting the assessment of an organization’s ability to detect and respond to threats. The report recommends documenting changes to testing conditions and considering how they affect conclusions about cyber resilience.

Client data protection drives approval requirements

Some 92% of respondents believe that submitting client information to external or cloud-hosted AI services requires explicit client approval.

Autonomous testing actions were identified as requiring explicit client approval by 62% of respondents. Data governance considerations include processing and storage locations, retention policies, model training practices, subcontractor involvement and access rights.

Organizations commissioning penetration tests need visibility into the systems and integrations surrounding AI models. Changes to technology vendors, permissions or data flows during an engagement may require additional approval.

Technical expertise remains essential

Validation of AI-generated technical output was rated as an essential practitioner skill for the coming year by 92% of providers. Secure data handling, manual exploitation and an understanding of AI limitations and failure modes also rank highly.

“TLPT, particularly in production environments, has always been high risk and in need of the strongest assurance. AI is amplifying this and is already changing how it is delivered,” said Nick Benson, CEO of CREST.

The use of AI to develop offensive tools creates a supervision challenge for cybersecurity teams. Training, peer review and secure development practices are necessary to ensure practitioners have the expertise to assess generated code and anticipate its potential consequences before deployment.

Procurement and disclosure requirements set to increase

Looking ahead, 88% of providers anticipate greater use of AI-generated offensive tooling, with the same percentage expecting increased demand for testers with AI-specific skills.

Some 85% of respondents expect AI-related procurement requirements to increase, while 81% anticipate more client requests for disclosure of AI use over the next year.

Some financial institutions are developing internal testing capabilities, potentially changing demand for external cybersecurity services. Independent assessment, complex scenario design, threat relevance and professional judgment may become more important differentiators for external providers as AI capabilities become more accessible.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/27/crest-ai-threat-led-penetration-testing/, helpnetsecurity.com, 27.11.2026)

[40] Help Net Security - Preinstalled Keenadu malware turns up on 220,000+ Android devices
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 12:57:58 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/26/keenadu-malware-android-devices/

[TAM METİN - 491 kelime]
Preinstalled Keenadu malware turns up on 220,000+ Android devices

A new Android phone can come out of the box with malware already in its firmware, and the number of such devices detected each month has grown more than 2.5 times over the past year, according to Kaspersky.

Keenadu, one of the two families behind most of these infections, landed on more than 220,000 devices in the first ten months of 2026. Together with the older Triada family, it turned up on 88% of devices found with preinstalled malware this year. Triada was first discovered about ten years ago, and about twenty distinct variants of it appeared in 2026.

“The latest Triada versions have two key distinguishing features: the ability to inject malicious functionality into any app on the device, system or user-installed, and a modular architecture that lets attackers tailor the malware to their current targets with additional payloads,” researchers explained.

The Keenadu backdoor versions Kaspersky examined stayed silent for about two and a half months before their command-and-control servers sent the order to activate. Kaspersky believes the delay was meant to help the malware avoid detection.

Keenadu borrows this same modular design from Triada, letting attackers push extra modules onto a device and inject them into target apps. In most known Keenadu attacks, the malware was used for ad fraud, in particular to generate fake clicks.

Planted in the supply chain

Malware of this kind reaches a device through a supply chain attack. In some cases the manufacturer installs it on purpose. In others, a legitimate brand that assembles the final product receives malicious firmware from a contractor acting in bad faith, or a component used in the build process has been compromised.

Once the malware is in the system partition, it is “technically impossible for either antivirus tools or the OS itself” to remove it, Kaspersky warned. Some of these Trojans are hard or impossible to get rid of even by reflashing the device.

Most cases involve phones and tablets, though Kaspersky has found other preinstalled malware families in different hardware, with Dwphon in the firmware of children’s smartwatches and Vo1d in TV boxes.

The cost for companies

Companies that issue these devices to staff can end up with slower phones, ads running on them, some visible to the user and some hidden, hijacked accounts, stolen cryptocurrency and leaked corporate data, the security firm noted. Infected devices can be enlisted into proxy networks or DDoS botnets, and attackers who disable devices in daily use can disrupt a company’s operations.

Test samples before buying in bulk

Kaspersky advises companies to inspect and pilot-test sample units before ordering a large batch, while stressing that this step does not replace security software on each device.

“Even if the tests come out fine, an unscrupulous supplier may have deliberately sent clean units for inspection. Protection must also be continuous because, for example, the device update system could come under attack,” the researchers concluded.

[41] Help Net Security - US agency tracking money laundering is latest casualty in string of federal breaches
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 11:05:01 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/26/fincen-breach-systems-offline/

[TAM METİN - 366 kelime]
US agency tracking money laundering is latest casualty in string of federal breaches

An intrusion at the US Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) has forced the agency to take some systems offline and extend reporting deadlines for financial institutions.

The bureau serves as the US government’s financial intelligence unit, collecting suspicious activity reports and other Bank Secrecy Act filings from banks and other financial institutions and sharing the intelligence with law enforcement.

“FinCEN is investigating a cybersecurity incident that resulted in unauthorized access to a limited subset of its systems,” reads an alert posted on the agency’s BSA E-Filing website. “As a proactive measure, FinCEN has taken some systems offline.”

The agency has shared few details, saying nothing about who might be behind the attack or which systems were accessed.

Deadlines pushed back

The BSA E-Filing website is still up, though FinCEN warned that “some services may be temporarily unavailable.” The agency said it “is working diligently to return all systems to normal operations as soon as possible.”

On November 24, FinCEN issued an order granting temporary, limited relief to filers hit by what it describes as “a service disruption that is hindering filers’ ability to adhere to reporting obligations.”

Judging by the order’s scope, the disruption appears to have hit Secure Data Transfer Mode (SDTM), one of the channels financial institutions use to submit BSA reports. The services companies rely on to file beneficial ownership information reports, and that individuals use to update their FinCEN identifiers, seem to be affected as well.

Anyone with eligible filings or updates due between November 18 and December 29 has until December 30 to get them in.

The growing scope of government intrusions

FinCEN’s disclosure followed a November 24 report by The New York Times, according to which the CIA and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence (ODNI) suffered a cybersecurity breach involving some unclassified information.

The FinCEN intrusion is the latest in a string of security incidents at US government agencies. A breach has been reported at the Department of Agriculture, the FBI has disclosed two separate breaches, and the Pentagon and the Department of Homeland Security have dealt with cyber incidents of their own.

[42] Help Net Security - Data recovery firm co-founder accused of cashing in on ransomware victims
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 09:59:07 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/26/ransomware-recovery-fraud-florida-firm-co-founder-charged/

[TAM METİN - 425 kelime]
Data recovery firm co-founder accused of cashing in on ransomware victims

The co-founder of a Florida data recovery firm has been indicted on charges that he secretly paid ransomware gangs while telling victims his company never did.

Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, a 52-year-old Colombian national, was arrested in Las Vegas on October 22 and has been charged by a federal grand jury in the District of Rhode Island with three counts of wire fraud, each carrying a maximum of 20 years in prison, the US Department of Justice said.

According to the indictment, the company told ransomware victims it had “sophisticated decryption technology” that could recover their data without a ransom being paid.

Prosecutors allege he had no such technology and instead negotiated with the attackers behind his clients’ backs, paying them and then charging his clients a higher amount so he could keep the difference. He is accused of using the proceeds to buy several properties in Florida.

“As alleged, the defendant preyed on ransomware victims by falsely marketing a solution he did not possess, secretly negotiating with cybercriminals, and profiting from the difference,” said First Assistant U.S. Attorney Charles C. Calenda for the District of Rhode Island.

The case against Palacio leans on an undercover operation run in June 2026, when FBI agents posed as a company hit by ransomware and as the ransomware actor holding its data.

Palacio allegedly paid the fake attacker about $6,000 in Bitcoin for the decryption key, then charged the undercover company about $13,260, more than twice the amount he handed over.

Ted E. Docks, Special Agent in Charge of the FBI Boston Field Office, described it as a “short-sighted shakedown scheme” in which Palacio is accused of “lying, cheating, and negotiating with cybercriminals to line his own pockets.”

“As alleged, Mr. Palacio Escobar is the proverbial wolf in sheep’s clothing, representing both himself and his company as experts in data recovery, when all the while he was paying the perpetrators of these ransomware attacks, under the guise of helping his clients,” added Docks.

“Anyone who tries to target victims of crime for personal gain should know that justice will eventually find them,” Docks concluded.

Palacio isn’t the first ransomware specialist this year to be prosecuted for turning on the victims who hired him. In July, Angelo Martino, a former ransomware negotiator at incident response firm DigitalMint, was sentenced to 70 months in prison for passing confidential details from his negotiations, including victims’ insurance policy limits, to BlackCat (ALPHV) operators, who used them to push up ransom demands.

[43] The Record - UK’s long-awaited National Cyber Action Plan delayed until December
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/britain-delays-national-cyber-action-plan-again

[TAM METİN - 987 kelime]
UK’s long-awaited National Cyber Action Plan delayed until December

Britain has again delayed the launch of its National Cyber Action Plan, the government’s long-awaited strategy for defending the wider economy against state-backed and criminal hackers, according to multiple people familiar with the matter.

The document, originally conceived as a national cyber strategy, was first due to be published by the end of 2025. After initial delays it was rebranded as an action plan and was close to publication in July this year before being postponed amid Keir Starmer’s resignation.

Officials, who had planned to publish the document in mid-November, now aim to unveil it on December 9 according to several sources, speaking to Recorded Future News on condition of anonymity.

The reasons for the latest delay are unclear. A government spokesperson declined to comment on the timing but said ministers remained committed to publishing the plan.

The latest delay follows an influential cross-party House of Lords committee warning that the government “appears complacent” about “potentially catastrophic risks,” including cyberattacks from Russia, China and Iran.

In a report published last week, the National Resilience Committee found Britain was dangerously underprepared for crises which could cascade across the economy, with disruption spilling over multiple sectors and disrupting the everyday lives of millions of people.

Baroness Northover, a Liberal Democrat member of the committee, told Recorded Future News that the country could not afford more uncertainty or drift. “This situation is unacceptable at a time of significant and escalating risk,” she said. “We must act urgently. The cost of inaction is enormous.”

“Further absence of an overarching National Cyber Action Plan, aimed at defending the economy against state-backed and criminal hacking, is not just frustrating, it is serious,” she said.

“The UK has got to keep pace with threats. Delay and avoidance is simply not an option and underlines the Committee’s point that the UK remains seriously exposed and underprepared for very significant events, and our vulnerability is current, active and acute on many fronts.”

Successive delays to cybersecurity legislation and policy announcements have contributed to a perception that the issue is a low political priority, despite its growing importance to Britain’s economic and national security.

The Cyber Security and Resilience Bill, an update to the country's critical-infrastructure cyber laws, took more than four years to reach Parliament and is now not expected to be enforced until 2028 — a decade on from the NIS Regulations it was written to replace.

The core provisions of the CSRB had already been completed back in 2022 under Rishi Sunak, whose government incorrectly described the laws as “updated” before failing to include them in that year's King's Speech setting out the government’s parliamentary agenda and leaving the draft bill ultimately unintroduced to parliament.

When Starmer's government first moved to bring the bill forward in September 2025, it was then delayed again amid a cabinet reshuffle.

Separately, a set of ransomware proposals — mandatory reporting for all victims, a licensing regime for extortion payments and a ban on ransoms for critical infrastructure operators — was due to go out to consultation in mid-2024, before being scuppered when Sunak called a general election.

Officials argue that the delays belie the substantial amount of work underway in Westminster.

The government is expected to bring forward a new National Security Bill next year to reform the Computer Misuse Act — although some fear the reforms as proposed would fail to protect security researchers — as well as finally set the ransomware proposals on the statute books.

In last month’s budget, the government committed £120 million ($159 million) over four years to the AI Security Institute and a further £40 million ($53 million) to establish a National Agentic Security Unit to address the threats posed by AI agents acting autonomously.

Another £70 million ($93 million) was earmarked for an AI-powered vulnerability hunting center at the National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), with £60 million ($79 million) allocated to the first phase of Cyber Shield, a national defense system that would use AI agents to find and fix security weaknesses across government and critical infrastructure networks.

Potentially more consequential for the technology industry was a commitment, buried among the budget announcements, that the government would consult next year on “where liability for cyber breaches should sit.”

Ministers have also launched a voluntary Cyber Resilience Pledge, seeking commitments from British businesses to put cybersecurity on the boardroom agenda and better manage their supply-chain risks. The government describes the pledge as a central pillar of the National Cyber Action Plan.

National Cyber Action Plan

The plan’s contents have not been officially disclosed. Recorded Future News understands it will include three pillars focusing on Threat, Growth and Resilience.

The clearest public indication of the government’s approach came in a lecture to the Royal United Services Institute in June by the NCSC’s chief executive Richard Horne.

Horne called for a full court press across what he termed the “near, mid and far spaces” of cyberspace — a framing people familiar with the plan expect to shape the areas it focuses on.

He defined the near space as the defense of individual organizations, the far space as offensive action against adversaries and the “mid space” as the shared “cloud, technology and telecommunications infrastructure,” most of which he said was “in private hands.”

In that space, he said, the government would partner with providers to “harden the mid space and disrupt attacker activity.”

The idea of an action plan is to specifically set out how and when specific activities will be undertaken, rather than simply describing the overall strategy. Sources who spoke to Recorded Future News says the critical details would be in the plan’s implementation schedule.

is the UK Editor for Recorded Future News. He was previously a technology reporter for Sky News and a fellow at the European Cyber Conflict Research Initiative, now Virtual Routes. He can be reached securely using Signal on: AlexanderMartin.79

[48] BSI - Offensive agentische KI verschärft Bedrohungslage
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 10:00:00 +0100
Link: https://www.bsi.bund.de/DE/Service-Navi/Presse/Pressemitteilungen/Presse2026/261126_agentische-KI.html

[TAM METİN - 335 kelime]
Offensive agentische KI verschärft Bedrohungslage

Ort Bonn
Datum 26.11.2026

Software-Baukästen wie Artex, CAIRN oder PentAGI können KI-Agenten orchestrieren und damit Cyberangriffe weitgehend automatisieren. Diese sogenannten Agentic Offensive Frameworks können mit selbstgewählten KI-Modellen betrieben und auch mit frei zugänglichen (open weight) KI-Modellen weitgehend selbstständig Ziele angreifen. Ein Zugriff auf aktuelle Frontier-Modelle ist dafür nicht notwendig. Damit fallen deutlich niedrigere Kosten für derartige Angriffe an, wodurch eine erheblich größere Verbreitung agentischer KI-Angriffe ermöglicht wird. Das Bundesamt für Sicherheit in der Informationstechnik (BSI) hat im Rahmen des AI Safety and Security Institute Deutschland (AISI Deutschland) dieses Vorgehen untersucht und stellt aktuell erneut eine deutliche Verschärfung der Bedrohungslage im Cyberraum fest.

Claudia Plattner: "Die KI-Revolution im Bereich der Cybersicherheit setzt sich fort. Wenn autonome Cyberangriffe mit Hilfe agentischer KI zum Schnäppchenpreis zu haben sind, müssen wir uns möglicherweise auf eine Lawine neuer Cyberangriffe einstellen. Weiterhin gilt, dass grundlegende Cybersicherheitsmaßnahmen schnellstmöglich und konsequent umgesetzt werden müssen. Behörden, Unternehmen und Organisationen müssen dringend ihre Resilienz weiter erhöhen, indem sie Sicherheitsmaßnahmen umsetzen und Notfallpläne erarbeiten und üben."

Offensive agentische Frameworks können Pentesting-Tools nutzen, um Schwachstellen zu finden und auszunutzen. Dabei können mehrere Phasen eines Angriffs automatisiert werden. Sie steuern KI-Agenten, die selbstständig Ziele angreifen und dabei ihr Vorgehen eigenständig anpassen können, etwa anhand von Fehlermeldungen. Die Angriffe zeichnen sich dadurch aus, dass in Maschinengeschwindigkeit eine Vielzahl verschiedener bekannter Techniken ausprobiert werden kann. Das BSI kann diese Fähigkeiten aufgrund eigener Erkenntnisse im Rahmen des AISI Deutschland bestätigen.

Das BSI empfiehlt Unternehmen, die noch an den Grundlagen der Cybersicherheit arbeiten, die Aktivierung eines Sofortprogramms mit der Priorisierung von Quick Wins. Dabei sollten die Reaktionsfähigkeit bei Sicherheitsvorfällen und das Absichern der einfachsten Angriffswege im Vordergrund stehen. Weitere Informationen finden Sie im Beitrag 
    Hoher Reifegrad von Agentic Offensive Frameworks verschärft Bedrohungslage.

Pressekontakt:
Bundesamt für Sicherheit in der Informationstechnik 

Pressestelle

Tel.: 0228-999582-5777
E-Mail: presse@bsi.bund.de

Internet: www.bsi.bund.de
BSI in Social Media

Bundesamt für Sicherheit in der Informationstechnik

[49] Industrial Cyber - Booba ransomware spreads from US water treatment plant to local government network, exposing IT/OT network security gap
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 07:39:03 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/threats-attacks/booba-ransomware-spreads-from-us-water-treatment-plant-to-local-government-network-exposing-it-ot-network-security-gap/

[TAM METİN - 860 kelime]
Booba ransomware spreads from US water treatment plant to local government network, exposing IT/OT network security gap

New data from ThreatDown reported that Booba ransomware hit an East Coast water treatment plant in November and then spread to the network of the local government that oversees it. The attack was staged and launched from the plant’s side of a split network, managed by a separate provider with no ThreatDown coverage. ThreatDown’s managed detection and response (MDR) team locked down 48 machines on the local government’s side after the first alerts. It saved 45 of them, while three were already encrypted. The company said there is no evidence the incident disrupted water operations.

“The ransomware was Booba, which only appeared this summer and already accounts for 44% of the ransomware compromises our analysts have handled over the last 90 days. Our MDR team locked down 48 machines at once and saved 45 of them,” ThreatDown researchers wrote in a blog post this week. “Water systems are a target our team rarely sees, but they’ve become a growing one. In July alone, CISA saw attackers go after more than 100 internet-exposed water and wastewater systems, and Minnesota reported a coordinated attack on more than 30 of its community water systems. Some of those attacks led to boil water notices and forced operators to run systems by hand. There’s no evidence of anything like that here.”

ThreatDown’s analysts see ransomware arriving from machines with no agent more and more often. “When our agent is on the machine where an attack starts, they have a chance to stop it while it’s still being staged, before anything is encrypted. A machine without our agent gives them nothing to see. And ransomware doesn’t check who manages a machine before it moves.”

“The network was split in two, something our analysts learned only when they called the customer. The local government’s side, home to its records and personal data, was under the watchful eye of ThreatDown MDR,” the post detailed. “The water treatment plant’s side, with no ThreatDown oversight, was managed by a separate provider, and that’s where the attack was staged and launched. On the plant’s side, every computer went offline temporarily while its provider remediated on site. On the local government’s side, the first sign of the attack was the encryption itself, at the exact line where our coverage began. Three machines past that line, it stopped.”

The post highlighted that Booba turned up in 44% of the ransomware compromises our team has handled over the last 90 days. “Our analyst thinks its ease of use may explain the spread. Outside our caseload, the surge shows up too. SOCRadar, which tracks it as the Booba Project, had counted 81 claimed attacks by Nov. 17, with local governments among the targets.”

“We weren’t chasing smoke here,” ThreatDown’s analyst said of the first alerts. “We were seeing flames.”

But whether the fire was the point is a question the evidence doesn’t answer.

ThreatDown has had no forensic access to the plant’s systems. “This attack got in through the part of the network we couldn’t see. We stopped it three machines into the part we could. For anyone running systems a community depends on, it’s worth knowing exactly where that line falls,” it added.

No indicators point to a nation-state actor, and Booba is a criminal ransomware brand. Still, water systems have drawn state-backed attackers before, including the group that breached a small water authority in 2023, according to a multiagency advisory.

Two or three alerts arrived in quick succession, flagging ransomware and files being encrypted. One machine had nearly every file renamed to [dot]booba, and the encryption was coming from another computer on the same network that had never had our agent installed.

With nothing to investigate, the team isolated 40 workstations and eight servers in one sweep from the Nebula console. Three machines were already encrypted. The other 45 were saved, and no further alerts followed. Next, they sent commands to try to roll back the three encrypted machines. When the customer asked to start reconnecting machines, our analysts advised waiting until the plant’s side was confirmed clean. They couldn’t vouch for a part of the network they couldn’t see.

“Our team’s assessment is that without MDR, every machine on the local government’s side would have been encrypted, records and personal data included,” according to the post. “Recovery would have meant reimaging every system, restoring from backups if usable ones existed, and running a full incident response.”

Recently, the National Rural Water Association (NRWA) reported growing demand for cybersecurity assistance among small and rural water and wastewater utilities in the U.S., with nearly 100 utilities contacting its Water Watch Center since the initiative was announced in August 2026. Three months after its launch, utilities in 26 states, from Maine to California, were seeking support, while the first participating systems had already entered 24/7 cybersecurity monitoring. The joint initiative between NRWA and DEF CON Franklin provides free, around-the-clock cybersecurity monitoring, threat intelligence, basic cyber hygiene guidance and incident response support to under-resourced water and wastewater systems that often lack dedicated cybersecurity personnel or budgets to hire them.

[50] Industrial Cyber - BSI publishes first standards supporting EU Cyber Resilience Act, gives manufacturers product security risk framework
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 07:37:52 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/regulation-standards-and-compliance/bsi-publishes-first-standards-supporting-eu-cyber-resilience-act-gives-manufacturers-product-security-risk-framework/

[TAM METİN - 714 kelime]
BSI publishes first standards supporting EU Cyber Resilience Act, gives manufacturers product security risk framework

The British Standards Institution (BSI), the U.K.’s national standards body, this week published the first two standards supporting the European Union’s Cyber Resilience Act (CRA), giving manufacturers a common framework for assessing and managing cybersecurity risks in products with digital elements. BS EN 40000-1-1 sets shared cybersecurity terminology and definitions, while BS EN 40000-1-2 lays out principles for product risk management and lifecycle activities. Both are meant to help manufacturers, suppliers and conformity assessment bodies apply the Act’s requirements.

They are the first two of four horizontal standards that apply to all products with digital elements. BS EN 40000-1-3, covering vulnerability handling, is due in early 2027, followed by BS EN 40000-1-4, on generic security requirements, later in 2027. Around 26 vertical standards, mainly from ETSI and CEN/CENELEC, will then add requirements for specific higher-risk products such as browsers, VPNs, routers and smart home devices.

The CRA establishes mandatory cybersecurity requirements for products with digital elements, including connected hardware and software such as smartwatches, cameras, connected baby monitors, toys and cloud-enabled products. The standards come ahead of the CRA’s full application on Dec. 11, 2027, when connected products placed on the EU market must meet the legislation’s requirements, including CE marking. Reporting obligations have applied since September 2026, while full requirements, including CE marking, take effect Dec. 11, 2027, for connected products placed on the EU market. The rules also apply to roughly 100,000 U.K. businesses that export to the EU or supply EU manufacturers.

BSI said two further horizontal standards are planned for 2027, covering vulnerability handling and generic security requirements, alongside around 26 sector-specific standards expected to address higher-risk products such as browsers, virtual private networks, routers and smart home devices. The organization said manufacturers should begin product cybersecurity risk assessments ahead of the compliance deadline.

“We know cyber criminals are getting ever more sophisticated in their methods, and AI is only exacerbating that. That means manufacturers making and selling connected products need to be vigilant, which is what the EU’s mandatory cybersecurity requirements are designed to support,” Laura May Bishop, Sector Lead, AI and Cybersecurity, BSI, said in a media statement. “These first standards are designed to help companies make sense of the requirements, offering a common language and a clear, consensus-based method for assessing and managing product cybersecurity risk.”

U.K. experts have played an active role in shaping these standards, and their publication gives manufacturers here a domestic route to the guidance they need to keep access to the EU market. With the rest of the series following in 2027, starting with a robust risk assessment now will put organizations in a stronger position ahead of the deadline.

Vocabulary (BS EN 40000-1-1) sets out the shared terms and definitions used across the series, so that manufacturers, suppliers and conformity assessment bodies can work from the same language. Principles, product risk management, and lifecycle activities (BS EN 40000-1-2) give manufacturers a consistent, consensus-based method for the product risk assessment on which every other CRA requirement is built. It also sets out the principles and activities that apply across a product’s full lifecycle.

BSI’s recent research into the cost of AI failure suggests security is rising up the business agenda. 31% see cybersecurity as currently the biggest risk as AI reshapes operations in their organization and 48% say it’s the capability they expect to become more important in the year ahead. Paired with security being the biggest concern around agentic AI adoption, the requirements of the Act bring clarity over expectations for manufacturers of products with digital elements.

The standards are relevant to manufacturers placing products with digital elements on the EU market, including product security and engineering leads, compliance, regulatory and quality managers, software developers and open-source maintainers, procurement and supply chain managers, and conformity assessment bodies and consultants.

Back in July, the European Commission published its first non-binding guidance to help manufacturers, software developers, and businesses prepare for CRA implementation. The document offered practical clarification on how the regulation applies ahead of key compliance deadlines, helping organizations understand their cybersecurity obligations before mandatory vulnerability and incident reporting requirements took effect in September and ahead of the CRA’s full cybersecurity rules becoming applicable in December 2027.

[51] Industrial Cyber - CISA flags critical vulnerabilities in Danfoss, Schneider Electric and AutomationDirect industrial products
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 07:36:59 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/cisa/cisa-flags-critical-vulnerabilities-in-danfoss-schneider-electric-and-automationdirect-industrial-products/

[TAM METİN - 1433 kelime]
CISA flags critical vulnerabilities in Danfoss, Schneider Electric and AutomationDirect industrial products

The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) published three ICS (industrial control systems) advisories this week covering hardware from Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series, Schneider Electric EcoStruxure Power Build Rapsody software, and AutomationDirect C-More EA9 Programming Software. Typically, this equipment is deployed across global critical infrastructure sectors, covering commercial facilities, energy, critical manufacturing, and water and wastewater systems.

In an advisory, CISA has flagged a critical stack-based buffer overflow flaw (CVE-2026-129713, CVSS v3 9.8) in Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series devices running version 5.4.0 or earlier. “Successful exploitation of this vulnerability could allow an attacker to execute arbitrary code with webserver privileges.”

The agency added that a stack-based buffer overflow in an unauthenticated CGI handler of the Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series controller allows remote attackers to send specially crafted HTTP/S POST requests, leading to memory corruption, webserver restart, and hypothetically potential arbitrary code execution with webserver privileges. Authentication is not required to exploit this vulnerability.

Carlo Meijer of Midnight Blue reported CVE-2026-129713 to CISA. This vulnerability will be addressed in an upcoming Danfoss AK-SM 800A Series software release (version 5.5.x). In addition, Danfoss will remove the ability for users to disable Strict Mode in future software releases.

Until a software update is available, Danfoss recommends that asset owners and operators follow the guidance provided in the product documentation and do not expose devices directly to the Internet or assign them public IP addresses; restrict remote access to affected devices by using a secure VPN or other protected network architecture; configure affected devices to operate in Strict Mode, which provides enhanced security protections; and use strong, unique passwords for all user accounts and administrative access.

In another advisory, CISA detailed a vulnerability in Schneider Electric’s EcoStruxure Power Build Rapsody, software used to enter or import single-line diagrams and generate an extensive bill of materials for a switchboard, covering all devices, connection items, and mounting components. Affecting the global energy, critical manufacturing, and commercial facilities sectors, the flaw is a use-after-free vulnerability (CWE-416), tracked as CVE-2026-119055 and carrying a CVSS v3 score of 5.3. A local attacker could exploit it by tricking an end user into opening a specially crafted malicious SSD file, potentially achieving arbitrary code execution. Left unremediated, it could also cause memory corruption and heap- or stack-based buffer overflows.

Schneider Electric CPCERT reported this vulnerability to CISA. Eight language-specific builds are affected: French (2.9.1.0 and earlier), Spanish (2.8.5.0301 and earlier), Portuguese (2.8.7.0101 and earlier), Belgian French (2.8.8.0201 and earlier), Belgian English (2.8.3.0201 and earlier), International English (2.8.4.0401 and earlier), International French (2.8.6.0000 and earlier), and Dutch (2.8.2.0000 and earlier).

Schneider Electric has released a vendor fix, available through its website. The patched versions are French 2.9.12.0101, Spanish 2.9.5.0000, Portuguese 2.9.7.0000, Belgian French 2.9.8.0000, Belgian English 2.9.3.0000, International English 2.9.4.0000, International French 2.9.6.0000, and Dutch 2.9.6.0000.

In another advisory, CISA identified a heap-based buffer overflow vulnerability in AutomationDirect’s C-More EA9 Programming Software. Tracked as CVE-2026-127280, the flaw carries a CVSS v3 score of 7.8 and affects version 6.80 and earlier. AutomationDirect has released a vendor fix. Users should update the software to version 6.81, available through the company’s software downloads page.

C-More EA9 Programming Software V6.80 does not adequately validate data read from a project file before using it in heap memory operations. A crafted file causes a heap buffer overrun during file open, resulting in heap corruption and instability within the application. An attacker can exploit this vulnerability by convincing a user to open a malicious project file delivered through common distribution channels such as email, file sharing, or removable media. “Successful exploitation of this vulnerability could allow attackers to corrupt application memory and cause instability or a crash, potentially leading to arbitrary code execution, if a user is convinced to open a malicious file.”

rgod of TrendAI Zero Day Initiative reported this vulnerability to CISA.

Deployed across the global critical manufacturing, energy, and water and wastewater sectors, the affected products come with a clear message from the advisory: asset owners and operators who cannot update to the latest software or firmware right away should apply compensating controls to reduce risk until the update can be performed. These measures are temporary and do not replace the update, which remains the primary recommended remediation. They should be applied as appropriate to the vulnerability, the assessed risk, operational requirements, and the characteristics of each environment, and implemented under the established management-of-change process. Before making any isolation, access-control, or network-configuration changes, organizations should evaluate the potential impact on safety, availability, and essential operational functions.

On the network side, the advisory calls for isolating affected engineering workstations and OT systems from external and nonessential networks, including the internet and corporate IT networks, where operationally feasible. Vendor, contractor, and other remote access to the affected OT environment should be disabled or tightly restricted until the vulnerability is remediated.

Where remote access is operationally required, organizations should use a hardened jump host, enforce multi-factor authentication, apply least-privilege access, and limit connections to authorized, time-bound sessions. Device communication should run over trusted, dedicated OT networks, and higher-criticality systems may warrant dedicated or physically separated infrastructure where practicable. Where logical segmentation is used, the controls should be properly configured, secured, and monitored.

Access to the affected systems should be limited, both physically and logically, to authorized personnel, with least-privilege principles applied to administrative, engineering, maintenance, and service access. On the host side, application allowlisting should be configured where supported so that only trusted, pre-approved applications can run and unauthorized software is blocked. Antivirus or endpoint detection and response (EDR) tools should be used where supported and compatible, and host-based and network firewalls should permit only required communications. Unnecessary services, ports, protocols, and communication pathways should be disabled. Where a service or protocol cannot be turned off for operational reasons, access should be restricted to trusted systems and authorized users wherever possible.

Visibility and recovery matter as well. System, security, and network logs should be enabled and reviewed regularly to spot suspicious or unauthorized activity, and communications to and from affected systems should be monitored for unexpected connections or shifts in network behavior where appropriate. Organizations should keep secure, tested, and preferably offline backups of PLC programs, device configurations, engineering workstation configurations, and any other information needed to restore operations, and should periodically confirm that those backups can actually be restored.

Critical OT functions should also be able to keep running without unnecessary dependencies on external networks, cloud services, remote-access services, corporate IT services, or other nonessential systems. If removable media is needed to move files into an isolated or restricted environment, it should be controlled and trusted, and scanned for malicious content before use.

The advisory also urges continuous evaluation of the risks of running vulnerable or outdated firmware or software, with compensating controls adjusted as the threat environment, operational requirements, or available vulnerability information changes. These controls are meant to reduce exposure while remediation is pending, and organizations should prioritize applying the vendor-provided update as soon as it can be safely tested and deployed in the operational environment.

CISA is urging users to take defensive measures to reduce the risk of exploitation, starting with limiting network exposure so that no control system device or system is accessible from the internet. Control system networks and remote devices should sit behind firewalls, isolated from business networks. Where remote access is unavoidable, the agency advises more secure methods such as Virtual Private Networks (VPNs), while cautioning that VPNs can carry vulnerabilities of their own and should be updated to the latest version. A VPN is also only as secure as the devices connected through it.

CISA reminded organizations to conduct a proper impact analysis and risk assessment before deploying any defensive measures. Its control systems security recommended practices section on the ICS webpage offers several cyber defense products for reading and download, including Improving Industrial Control Systems Cybersecurity with Defense-in-Depth Strategies. The agency also encourages organizations to adopt its recommended strategies to defend ICS assets proactively. Further mitigation guidance appears in the technical information paper ICS-TIP-12-146-01B, Targeted Cyber Intrusion Detection and Mitigation Strategies.

Organizations that observe suspected malicious activity should follow their internal procedures and report findings to CISA, which tracks and correlates them against other incidents.

To guard against social engineering, CISA also advises users not to click links or open attachments in unsolicited emails, and points to its guidance on recognizing and avoiding email scams, phishing, and social engineering attacks.

CISA noted that no known public exploitation specifically targeting these vulnerabilities has been reported to the agency so far.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://industrialcyber.co/cisa/cisa-flags-critical-vulnerabilities-in-danfoss-schneider-electric-and-automationdirect-industrial-products/, industrialcyber.co, 27.11.2026)
