[52] Industrial Cyber - Volant Manticore expands Iran-linked cyber espionage from Cyprus to Saudi Arabia, targeting critical sectors
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 10:06:16 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/ransomware/volant-manticore-expands-iran-linked-cyber-espionage-from-cyprus-to-saudi-arabia-targeting-critical-sectors/

[TAM METİN - 985 kelime]
Volant Manticore expands Iran-linked cyber espionage from Cyprus to Saudi Arabia, targeting critical sectors

New research from Check Point uncovers Volant Manticore, a previously undocumented Iran-nexus cyber espionage group active since at least September 2025. After initially focusing on Cyprus, the group expanded to Saudi Arabia this September, a rapid pivot that underscores how swiftly Iranian cyber operations adapt to shifting geopolitical priorities across the Middle East and Europe. More broadly, the discovery shows Tehran’s cyber reach extending well beyond its traditional targets.

Interestingly, Check Point Research assesses that Volant Manticore is likely a separate group of former MuddyWater operators, combining legacy MuddyWater tradecraft with its own developing tools and infrastructure.

“The relationship between the two groups is an important part of the discovery,” Check Point researchers wrote in a Tuesday blog post. “Volant Manticore uses older MuddyWater tradecraft, but its custom implants, infrastructure and targeting distinguish it from the latest MuddyWater operations. Check Point Research assesses with medium confidence that the group consists of former MuddyWater operators who now operate as an independent threat cluster.”

Historically linked to Iran’s Ministry of Intelligence and Security (MOIS), Volant Manticore is the first Iranian group Check Point Research has observed making Cyprus a primary espionage target, focusing on telecommunications, aviation, and critical infrastructure. In September 2026, the group shifted its focus to Saudi Arabia, with increased phishing activity targeting the healthcare sector. The group uses the KuboShell backdoor and Homa Framework, alongside remote management tools and credential-harvesting campaigns.

Check Point added that Cyprus has deepening defense and energy ties with Israel and Greece and plays a role in Western military operations in the region, increasing its potential intelligence value to Iranian operators. “The investigation began after researchers identified a fake CV belonging to ‘Athena Marinou,’ an AI-generated travel and tourism professional supposedly based in Cyprus. A link presented as a download for the full CV instead directed the recipient to an archive containing KuboShell, a newly identified backdoor. Check Point Research assesses that the phishing attempt targeted a Cyprus-based airline whose business centers on flights between Cyprus and Israel.”

Researchers also connected the group’s infrastructure with an earlier January 2026 campaign. That operation used a malicious military-themed Word document and was likely aimed at a Cypriot government entity, potentially connected to the defense sector. Taken together, the campaigns show a persistent focus on organizations that could provide intelligence related to Cyprus’s government, infrastructure, and regional relationships.

Volant Manticore relies heavily on phishing and social engineering, adapting its lures to the organizations and industries it targets. The Cyprus airline campaign used a fake CV, while the activity targeting Saudi Arabia included healthcare-themed files such as ‘AI Healthcare Seminar.’ Other campaigns used malicious documents or credential-harvesting infrastructure designed to imitate corporate email and authentication services.

“Once access is established, the group has several options,” according to the research. “KuboShell acts as an initial backdoor and uses the InterPlanetary File System (IPFS), together with the Pinata service, for its command-and-control communication. Researchers observed KuboShell being used for initial reconnaissance before additional capabilities were deployed. The group then uses the Homa Framework, a multi-stage post-compromise framework whose final-stage client closely resembles MuddyWater’s older MuddyC2Go framework.”

Volant Manticore also uses legitimate remote monitoring and management software, including NetLock RMM, N-able RMM and MeshCentral. This mixture of custom malware and legitimate administrative tools provides several potential routes for maintaining access to compromised organizations.

The methods observed in Volant Manticore’s campaigns give security teams several concrete areas to monitor. The group has used fake CVs, healthcare-themed archives and malicious Word documents as initial lures, making recruiting teams and employees who regularly handle unsolicited external files particularly important targets for security awareness and monitoring. Its deployment of NetLock RMM, N-able RMM and MeshCentral also highlights the need for organizations to maintain an inventory of approved remote administration tools and investigate unexpected installations or connections.

Researchers also identified infrastructure designed to imitate corporate email and authentication services, underscoring the importance of strong multi-factor authentication and monitoring for unusual authentication activity to limit the impact of compromised credentials. The group’s use of KuboShell for reconnaissance before deploying additional payloads highlights the need to detect unexpected PowerShell activity, network discovery and queries relating to administrative access, which may indicate that an initial compromise has progressed.

Organizations operating in aviation, telecommunications, healthcare, government and critical infrastructure should also incorporate geopolitical developments into their threat monitoring, particularly when their operations or business partners are connected to regions experiencing heightened tensions.

The group represents more than just another newly discovered Iranian threat actor—it signals a broader strategic shift. While Israel remains a primary target, Tehran is distributing its cyber operations across the Middle East and Europe to support wider geopolitical goals.

Volant Manticore’s trajectory illustrates this clearly. Cyprus remained a persistent target due to its regional position and strategic ties, but when tensions escalated between Saudi Arabia and the Iran-backed Houthis, the group pivoted rapidly to Saudi organizations. Signs of rushed tooling, reused infrastructure, and broad phishing suggest its operational priorities shifted faster than its developers could keep up.

For defenders, the takeaway is clear that cyber risk moves at the speed of geopolitics. An organization doesn’t need to be a direct party to a conflict to become a target; its geography, sector, infrastructure, or supply-chain partnerships alone can put it in a state-linked actor’s crosshairs.

In conclusion, the Check Point researchers mentioned that Volant Manticore shows how Iran’s cyber ecosystem continues to reshape itself. What appears to be a group of former MuddyWater operators is developing its own malware and infrastructure while retaining parts of the tradecraft its operators used previously.

“Its targeting may ultimately be the more important signal. The move from a sustained focus on Cyprus to intensified activity against Saudi Arabia demonstrates how quickly espionage priorities can change in response to regional developments,” they added. “Future geopolitical developments could drive further shifts in the group’s targeting priorities.”

[53] Industrial Cyber - EU proposes Quantum Act to strengthen industrial capabilities, secure supply chains, advance post-quantum readiness
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 10:05:00 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/regulation-standards-and-compliance/eu-proposes-quantum-act-to-strengthen-industrial-capabilities-secure-supply-chains-advance-post-quantum-readiness/

[TAM METİN - 1159 kelime]
EU proposes Quantum Act to strengthen industrial capabilities, secure supply chains, advance post-quantum readiness

The European Commission proposed on Wednesday a Quantum Act aimed at translating Europe’s research strengths in quantum technologies into industrial capabilities, strengthening critical supply chains and preparing the region’s digital systems for post-quantum cryptography. The proposed legislation is built around three pillars: research excellence, quantum technology deployment, and systems and supply security. Through the Quantum Europe Framework Initiative, the Commission intends to coordinate EU and national programs, improve access to funding and shared infrastructure, and support the development of quantum computers, simulators and other industrial applications.

The Quantum Act is the European Union’s enabling framework for a quantum ecosystem that already hosts around one-third of the world’s quantum companies. Its goal is to turn Europe’s scientific strength into industrial and commercial capability, accelerate deployment and adoption of quantum technologies, and strengthen preparedness for a quantum-safe future.

To get there, it sets a common EU framework that supports the full quantum lifecycle, from research to deployment, and builds on existing EU programmes. The framework backs company scale-up and market uptake, improves resilience across the quantum value chain, and enhances strategic coordination. It also promotes large-scale initiatives such as quantum grand challenges to tackle major technological and engineering bottlenecks, and encourages milestone-based development, procurement, user access, competence centres, supply-chain resilience and post-quantum cryptography preparedness.

The proposal addresses structural challenges, including difficulties in translating research into market-ready products and services, limited opportunities for start-ups and scale-ups to reach industrial scale, and dependencies on concentrated or non-EU sources for critical components, technologies and services. Through a common EU framework, the legislation seeks to align efforts across member states, support industrialisation and deployment, improve access to infrastructure and finance, and identify and mitigate strategic dependencies.

“Quantum technologies will shape the future of industry, security and innovation, and they will have a profound impact on our societies,” Henna Virkkunen, executive vice president for tech sovereignty, security and democracy, wrote in a media statement. “Europe needs to accelerate now. With the Quantum Act, we are putting in place the key elements to turn our world-class quantum research into industrial capacity, strengthen critical supply chains and prepare for the cyber risks linked to quantum computing. Europe has already built strong foundations in quantum; now we must turn them into industrial scale, resilient supply chains and secure deployment.”

The legislation is intended to strengthen Europe’s competitiveness, technological sovereignty and resilience while addressing the security risks associated with advances in quantum computing. In particular, sufficiently powerful quantum computers could undermine widely used cryptographic systems, potentially affecting public administrations, critical infrastructure, sensitive communications, digital identities and financial services. The Quantum Act therefore seeks to sustain the EU’s scientific and technological leadership, strengthen industrial deployment capacity, and improve resilience and economic security across strategically important quantum value chains and infrastructure. It also supports a coordinated transition to post-quantum cryptography, alongside preparedness monitoring and targeted procurement safeguards to protect Europe’s digital systems.

The Commission is also working with the European Investment Bank Group, member states and other stakeholders to establish a Sovereign Tech Equity Fund to support technologies considered critical to Europe’s technological sovereignty and economic security. The proposal supports the EU’s ambition to become a global leader in quantum technologies by 2030, building on more than €11 billion in investments by the EU and its member states, as reported in the 2025 EU Quantum Strategy.

The Regulation establishes a Quantum Europe Framework Initiative, a common Union framework for quantum research, innovation and industrialisation. Member States are required to adopt or align national quantum strategies with its agreed priorities and measures. The Initiative is intended to support excellence across the full technology lifecycle, from research and scientific discovery to engineering, testing, validation, industrialisation, scale-up, deployment and market uptake.

The Initiative covers seven operational objectives, including leadership in quantum science and technology; breakthroughs in critical technological and engineering bottlenecks; building and deploying large-scale strategic quantum infrastructures; and bringing mature quantum technologies to deployment and market. It also helps accelerate quantum growth and adoption in the Union; attract and strengthen the Union’s quantum talent base; and accelerate research, testing, validation and deployment of post-quantum cryptography.

The Commission notes that powerful quantum computers could break existing cryptographic methods used to protect sensitive communication and data. The proposal therefore combines support for quantum innovation with security preparedness. It supports research, coordinated migration, standards and risk-based procurement of post-quantum cryptography. It also foresees the possibility for the Commission to impose stronger requirements to accelerate the transition to post-quantum cryptography, if technological advancements require to do so. Preparation needs to start before such computers become available, because migration takes time and data requiring long-term confidentiality may already be collected for later decryption.

Commenting on the release of the Quantum Act, Ali El Kaafarani, CEO and founder of PQShield, wrote in an emailed statement that it’s reassuring that the proposed Quantum Act recognises that Europe’s quantum ambitions will depend on the security and resilience of the technology supply chains underpinning them.

“With post-quantum standards and migration timelines now in place, 2027 should bring much greater scrutiny of supplier readiness,” according to Kaafarani. “I expect to see clearer procurement requirements, more cryptographic RFIs, and more organisations asking suppliers when and how they will support PQC. Those demand signals matter because they push quantum resilience further down the supply chain, from end users and equipment manufacturers to the silicon itself.”

He added, “Many of the systems being designed today will still be operating a decade from now, so supporting PQC cannot simply be a future software update. Secure boot, firmware updates, device identity, communications and roots of trust all need to be considered now. And readiness has to mean more than supporting the right algorithm: implementations need to be secure, resistant to side-channel attacks, and crypto-agile enough to adapt as standards and threats evolve. If the Quantum Act drives that level of scrutiny across Europe’s technology supply chain, it can help turn research excellence into systems that are not only competitive, but resilient by design.”

Last June, the European Union moved to strengthen its cybersecurity posture by setting out a path to post-quantum cryptography. Backed by the European Commission, Member States issued a roadmap and timeline for moving to this newer form of encryption, built on algorithms designed to withstand decryption by quantum computers. The move grew out of a strategy developed by the NIS Cooperation Group and followed the Commission’s Recommendation, reflecting a growing sense of urgency as quantum computing capabilities accelerate.

The Cyber Security Agency of Singapore (CSA) issued last month quantum-safe migration guidance recommending that organizations exposed to quantum-related cybersecurity risks establish migration plans by 2027 and aim to complete the transition by 2035. The guidance calls for Critical Information Infrastructure (CII) systems to complete migration by 2031, reflecting Singapore’s risk-based approach to preparing for advances in quantum computing that could eventually undermine widely used cryptographic methods protecting digital communications, transactions and sensitive information.

[54] Industrial Cyber - FDD urges US Congress to designate space systems as critical infrastructure sector, citing cyber and counterspace threats
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 10:03:27 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/critical-infrastructure/fdd-urges-us-congress-to-designate-space-systems-as-critical-infrastructure-sector-citing-cyber-and-counterspace-threats/

[TAM METİN - 2784 kelime]
FDD urges US Congress to designate space systems as critical infrastructure sector, citing cyber and counterspace threats

The Foundation for Defense of Democracies (FDD) has called on the U.S. Congress to designate space-based systems as the country’s 17th critical infrastructure sector, warning that absence of a dedicated federal risk-management framework leaves satellites, ground stations, launch facilities, command-and-control systems and specialized space supply chains exposed to growing threats. The move comes as space systems have quietly become the backbone of modern American life, and destruction or disruption of these systems would have cascading consequences for U.S. national security, economic security, public safety, and daily life.

In a monograph published on Tuesday, titled ‘Space Critical Infrastructure: The Final Frontier,’ FDD researchers Emmerson Overell and Annie Fixler said space infrastructure underpins services essential to national security, economic security, public safety and daily life, including global positioning, navigation and timing (PNT), telecommunications, financial transactions, weather forecasting, intelligence collection and military operations. The researchers said the federal government recognizes 16 critical infrastructure sectors, but space has neither a designated sector risk management agency nor a sector-specific risk management plan.

The report identified growing counterspace capabilities from China and Russia, increasing reliance on commercial space providers for federal and military operations, and expanding dependence on satellite-enabled services as factors making the fragmented U.S. approach increasingly inadequate. Potential adversary activities include jamming satellite communications, falsifying navigation signals, conducting surveillance through proximity operations and destroying satellites, potentially disrupting intelligence collection, command and control, missile warning and military targeting.

The executives identified that while some systems are indirectly covered through existing sectoral designations, no single federal entity was responsible for assessing how risks to satellites, ground stations, launch systems, command-and-control links, space data services, and commercial providers interact as one interconnected system. “This incomplete coverage leaves gaps in U.S. defenses. Launch facilities, spacecraft and orbital constellations, ground stations, tracking and command systems, manufacturing facilities, and specialized space supply chains do not fit neatly within the risk-management plans of the communications, defense industrial base, or critical manufacturing sectors or those of the other sectors that rely on these assets. That leaves essential parts of the ground-to-orbit ecosystem without a risk framework.”

The 28-page monograph highlighted three trends that make this fragmented approach increasingly untenable. First, China and Russia are fielding counterspace capabilities to jam satellite communications, falsify navigation signals, maneuver near U.S. satellites to spy on them, or destroy satellites. The U.S. Space Force has repeatedly warned, ‘Intensifying strategic competition presents a serious threat to U.S. national security interests in, from, and to space.’ In a crisis or conflict, U.S. adversaries could delay intelligence collection, disrupt command and control, degrade missile warning, and complicate offensive targeting.

Second, commercial providers’ integration into federal and military space operations makes private-sector systems increasingly central to national security missions.

Third, the American public, economy, and military increasingly depend on space-enabled services, including GPS navigation, timing for financial transactions, weather forecasting, and telecommunications. In 2022, Samuel Visner, board chairman of the Space Information Sharing and Analysis Center (Space ISAC), an industry-led threat-sharing organization, warned that ‘space systems are conceivably a target’ for infrastructure attacks. The growth of the industry’s importance has only made it a more attractive target.

The global space economy is projected to triple from US$630 billion in 2023 to $1.8 trillion by 2035, according to a 2024 World Economic Forum report. SpaceX began launching operational Starlink satellites in 2019, and by early 2026 had more than 10,000 active Starlink satellites in low-Earth orbit.

Overell and Fixler note that government-owned satellites remain essential, but commercial providers help fill gaps in coverage, capacity, speed, and resilience. Commercial satellite communications give military users more bandwidth, wider geographic reach, and backup pathways when government networks are unavailable, overloaded, or targeted by U.S. adversaries. Commercial imagery and data providers supplement federal collection systems. Commercial launch providers can expand access to orbit and increase the pace at which the U.S. can deploy, replace, or augment space systems.

The Pentagon acknowledges value of commercial capabilities. U.S. Space Force’s vision for the next 15 years calls for augmenting future GPS with diverse allied and commercial navigation and timing systems, and envisions satellite communications built around a hybrid, self-healing architecture rather than small numbers of easily targeted satellites. A self-healing network is resilient, designed to reroute data around jammed, degraded, or compromised components. The Defense Innovation Unit’s Hybrid Space Architecture program seeks secure global internet connectivity across commercial, civil, military, allied, and partner systems in multiple orbits.

Overell and Fixler identify that satellites quietly hold up far more of American life than most people realize. GPS timing and navigation guide aircraft, ships, trucks and trains, help emergency crews find callers, and stamp financial transactions down to the millisecond, while utilities lean on the same precision to keep power generation and transmission in sync. Satellite links also stretch phone and data coverage beyond the reach of ground networks; farmers use satellite imagery and weather data to manage crops and irrigation, and forecasters and disaster responders depend on orbital eyes to track storms, wildfires and floods and to coordinate relief. The military leans on space just as heavily for missile warning, intelligence, surveillance and targeting, which is why Gen. Stephen Whiting told House lawmakers in March 2026 that losing these capabilities would leave the joint force ‘immediately and materially impaired.’

The Space Force’s April 2026 Future Operating Environment 2040 assessment sketches how fast the damage would compound: a single day without space services could disrupt weather alerts, ground aircraft and halt markets, while weeks or months could bring fuel and hospital supply shortages, clogged ports, missed harvests and loss of power-grid synchronization. Yet no single federal agency is looking at the whole picture. The FAA, for instance, helps aviation operators prepare for GPS interference but does not protect the satellites, ground stations, command links, launch sites and manufacturing base behind those services, and existing sector risk plans focus on their own assets rather than the full ground-to-orbit chain, leaving space without dedicated, sector-wide risk management.

Overell and Fixler highlighted that space governance in the U.S. is a patchwork, and the gap is structural rather than a matter of anyone failing at their job. Each agency tends to its own slice: NASA runs civil missions, the Pentagon and National Reconnaissance Office handle national security systems, the FCC and NTIA split spectrum duties, the FAA oversees launches and reentries, Commerce covers remote sensing and space traffic coordination, and CISA offers voluntary cybersecurity support while Space ISAC runs a private threat-sharing watch center. What nobody owns is the full ground-to-orbit picture: how a flaw in one satellite, ground station or command link could ripple across the whole ecosystem, or who leads when a disruption crosses several agencies’ jurisdictions.

They assess that the practical fallout is easy to imagine. A cyberattack on a commercial provider’s ground network that knocks out both military and civilian services would likely trigger a scattered response, with CISA, the FCC, the Defense Department and law enforcement or intelligence agencies each acting within their own lanes, and the operator fielding multiple reporting channels and overlapping information requests.

Agencies are generally doing what Congress told them to do, but those mandates target single functions like exploration, defense, spectrum, launch safety or cybersecurity, not the resilience of interconnected civil, commercial, military and intelligence systems. Risks that land between mandates can go unowned, and lessons rarely travel across the ecosystem, a point underscored by a November 2026 GAO report finding that Space Force supply-chain risk efforts were not sufficiently coordinated with other national security space stakeholders.

Beijing is building the means to turn America’s reliance on space into a weakness. SPACECOM reported in March 2026 that China operates more than 500 intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance satellites, which help the People’s Liberation Army track and target U.S. and allied forces with long-range precision weapons. The Pentagon says China is also developing missiles that kill satellites, along with jammers, ground-based lasers and tools to interfere with satellite signals, sensors and control networks, while Chinese cyber actors burrow into U.S. space and defense networks, likely to collect intelligence and stake out access for attacks in a future conflict.

Early in a war, that could mean severed military communications, false navigation data fed to ships and aircraft, blinded satellite imagery and delayed U.S. decisions. China’s behavior in orbit is growing bolder as well: five experimental spacecraft maneuvered in coordinated patterns in 2024, a display a senior Space Force official likened to ‘dogfighting in space,’ and the 2022 towing of a defunct satellite by Shijian-21, along with 2025 docking and refueling capabilities, hint at technology that could shove U.S. satellites out of position or sustain operations against them for longer.

Russia, meanwhile, has already shown that counterspace attacks land on Earth. About an hour before the February 2022 invasion of Ukraine, it hit Viasat’s KA-SAT network, exploiting faulty settings in ground equipment to reach the management system and disable tens of thousands of modems across Ukraine and parts of Europe.

Moscow warned at the UN in October 2022 that commercial satellites supporting military operations could become legitimate targets, and by July 2026 it was reportedly deploying electronic warfare systems to disrupt the Starlink links that control Ukrainian drones. Its GPS jamming around the Baltic, Black Sea and Mediterranean has diverted flights and degraded navigation accuracy for civilians too, and the destructive end of the toolkit remains very much alive: a 2021 anti-satellite missile test created more than 1,500 trackable debris pieces, and SPACECOM warned in March 2026 that Russia is developing a nuclear anti-satellite weapon whose detonation could wipe out thousands of satellites and leave low-Earth orbit unusable for years.

“China and Russia view space as an operational domain where U.S. advantages can be weakened before or during conflict,” Overell and Fixler wrote. “SPACECOM warned Congress in March 2026 that threats across ground infrastructure, orbit, and cyberspace are no longer future possibilities but current operational realities.76 During a crisis or conflict, cyberattacks, signal jamming, or physical attacks on space systems could delay intelligence collection, disrupt military coordination, weaken missile warning, and make navigation and targeting less reliable.”

FDD recommended that Congress formally establish space-based systems as the 17th critical infrastructure sector and designate NASA as the lead federal agency for sector-wide risk management, public-private collaboration, threat intelligence sharing and resilience planning. The designation would clarify federal responsibilities and strengthen cybersecurity and engineering resilience without creating a new regulatory regime or displacing existing authorities.

The report urges a generous definition that stretches across the whole ground-to-orbit chain: satellites, ground stations and mission operations centers, telemetry, tracking and command systems, launch-related systems, satcom and user terminals, PNT services, remote sensing and weather data, mission software and cloud infrastructure, supply chains, and commercial systems serving critical civilian or national security functions. The logic is simple. A satellite can be perfectly healthy in orbit while a compromised ground station or command link still knocks out essential services, so risk has to be assessed as a system-of-systems problem rather than a pile of disconnected assets.

The second recommendation hands NASA the role of non-regulatory Sector Risk Management Agency, for the simple reason that space is its core mission and it has decades of experience designing, operating and evaluating complex systems across the civil-commercial divide. The pitch to industry is deliberately modest: better threat sharing, clearer federal points of contact, stronger resilience planning and more help in a crisis, with no new regulation attached. Every other agency keeps its existing authorities, and NASA’s job is to connect them rather than replace them.

The report is candid that NASA is not built for this today, since its security work has centered on its own missions, so it asks Congress to fund the job separately, starting at $15 million a year for 25 full-time staff, to be reassessed as the sector matures. Hiring should favor space engineering, cybersecurity, mission reliability, incident coordination, supply chains and dependency mapping.

Third, Congress should require NASA to publish a sector-specific risk management plan within a year of designation. The plan would define the sector, map critical nodes and dependencies, assess cyber, engineering and supply-chain vulnerabilities, and chart where commercial operations lean on federal systems. It would also take stock of existing channels such as Space ISAC, CISA’s partnerships and FCC-NTIA spectrum coordination, so that NASA finds the gaps and overlaps instead of duplicating work.

The practical payoff is clear escalation procedures: an operator hit by cyber interference, ground-network intrusion, jamming or false navigation signals should know whom to call, how information moves across government and which agency leads. The plan would also cover degraded-operations planning around a “space zero day,” a sudden loss or compromise of space services before anyone understands the cause or scope, so that officials know in advance which services come back first and what backups exist.

Fourth, NASA would be directed to set up functional subgroups, because national security satellites, commercial communications networks and civil missions have different security needs and legal authorities that no single process could handle. The report sketches three as a baseline. A National Security Space Subgroup would bring in DOD, the intelligence community (with the NRO coordinating), NTIA and DHS.

A Commercial and Civil Space Subgroup would cover launch, civil missions, data services and ground infrastructure, with Commerce, Transportation, the FCC, DHS and NASA at the table. A Communications Subgroup would focus on satcom, spectrum-dependent services, command links and PNT dependencies, with the FCC, NTIA and CISA keeping their regulatory and risk roles. Each would map dependencies, set resilience priorities and plan incident response, feeding NASA’s sector plan, and Congress would ask GAO to review every federal entity with a stake in space to make sure the cross-sector interests are covered.

Fifth, Congress should have NASA lead development of cybersecurity and operational resilience standards for federal and commercial systems supporting critical functions, working with DHS, Commerce, DOD and operators. The aim is alignment, not a new layer of rules. Existing contractor and DOD cybersecurity requirements would be reviewed to see where they suffice, where they duplicate one another and where space-specific mission assurance is missing, meaning whether systems can keep running, fail safely or recover quickly under cyber, electronic or operational disruption. The baseline would cover secure telemetry, tracking and command, authentication, encryption of command and data links, software and firmware integrity, secure updates, anomaly detection, incident reporting, supply-chain risk, backup PNT, degraded-mode functionality, redundant communications, recovery timelines, public messaging and resilience-by-design engineering.

The enforcement mechanism is procurement. Federal customers such as DOD, the intelligence community and NASA would check existing contracts against the baseline, close gaps in future buys and test that purchased systems can protect essential services and recover. Operators outside federal contracting would be encouraged to adopt the standards voluntarily, with NASA and CISA offering technical help and exercises.

The sixth recommendation is the one that tests all of it, by calling on Congress to require regular space zero-day wargames and degraded-operations exercises that bring federal agencies together with commercial operators serving the public and government contractors, simulating services that are disrupted, degraded, spoofed, jammed or simply gone. SPACECOM’s commercial wargames and the Space Force’s doctrine and wargaming units are a start, but they lean military, so the report wants a sustained whole-of-government program, perhaps within the National Exercise Program, that rehearses civilian consequences too, from backup navigation and communications alternatives to public messaging, allied coordination and emergency response. If civilian life and military power both ride on space, the argument goes, Washington should practice losing it before it happens for real.

In conclusion, Overell and Fixler recognize that space security is no longer separate from terrestrial security. “It is the infrastructure layer that enables modern finance, communications, transportation, agriculture, weather forecasting, intelligence collection, missile warning, and critical military operations. America’s adversaries understand this, which is why they are developing capabilities to disrupt, degrade, and destroy the space-based systems that give the United States its potent military, intelligence, and economic advantages.”

They added that designating space systems as the 17th critical infrastructure sector, naming NASA as the SRMA, and establishing the sector-wide risk management, resilience, and exercise framework outlined above would help protect these advantages. “Congress should address this challenge now rather than wait for a major disruption to space systems. Technologies and strategic trends are converging toward a space environment fundamentally different from today’s.”

Overell and Fixler said that necessary planning and investment decisions made now will shape whether the U.S. is prepared for the new age of space. “Technological superiority alone will be insufficient, but the organization, integration, and clarity of strategic purpose will be decisive. To protect America’s livelihoods and prosperity on Earth, the United States must secure the systems above it.”

[55] Industrial Cyber - PTC expands FedRAMP offerings with Windchill+ and Onshape Government Federal for defense contractors
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 26 Nov 2026 09:51:24 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/news/ptc-expands-fedramp-offerings-with-windchill-and-onshape-government-federal-for-defense-contractors/

[TAM METİN - 408 kelime]
PTC expands FedRAMP offerings with Windchill+ and Onshape Government Federal for defense contractors

PTC announced an expansion of its FedRAMP offerings for U.S. federal agencies and contractors. PTC’s Windchill+ product lifecycle management (PLM) solution is now available on Microsoft Azure Government within PTC Cloud Services, and PTC is introducing its Onshape Government Federal solution, a FedRAMP Moderate Equivalent environment for engineering teams working with Controlled Unclassified Information (CUI). The two new offerings also support compliance with the Department of Defense’s Cybersecurity Maturity Model Certification (CMMC) program for protecting CUI.

Windchill+ is the only direct, full-stack SaaS PLM solution certified on the FedRAMP Marketplace. PTC Cloud Services is certified at the FedRAMP Class C (Moderate) baseline (NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5), so agencies can rely on an existing certification rather than running their own security review.

Onshape Government Federal is the only cloud-native CAD and PDM with a FedRAMP Moderate Equivalent attestation based on NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5. Built on AWS GovCloud (US), it expands its existing support for ITAR and EAR workloads. It also integrates with the Arena PLM for AWS GovCloud solution, giving organizations a connected CAD, PDM, and PLM environment across engineering, quality, and supply chain workflows.

“Aerospace and defense organizations build and maintain some of the most complex, mission-critical products in the world,” said Jon Stevenson, chief product officer at PTC. “For nearly a decade, we’ve invested in cloud solutions that meet federal security requirements, and we aim to continue that investment so these customers can count on PTC from product design through service.”

PTC Cloud Services first achieved FedRAMP authorization in 2017, and PTC has expanded its federal portfolio steadily ever since.

In addition to Windchill+ and Onshape, federal agencies and contractors can choose from other PTC solutions to manage product data while meeting federal security requirements. These include Windchill SaaS, which is authorized for U.S. Department of Defense Impact Levels 4 and 5 (IL4 and IL5); Arena, a cloud-native product lifecycle management (PLM) and quality management system (QMS) with FedRAMP Moderate Equivalent status; and Servigistics, a service parts planning and optimization solution authorized for IL5.

Across its portfolio, PTC is delivering on its vision for the Intelligent Product Lifecycle: enabling manufacturers to build a modern product data foundation, extend that data across the enterprise, and unlock AI-powered transformation. Broader use of product data enables companies to bring higher-quality products to market faster, manage complexity at scale, and support new digital business models.
